### MCTS Is Counting the Wrong Thing: Learning Exploration Counts for LLM Reasoning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22301) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22301) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22301) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22301)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS) has powered landmark successes in reinforcement learning, yet its gains in LLM reasoning can remain modest despite substantial search effort. We identify an overlooked bottleneck: what MCTS counts. Standard UCT assigns independent visit counts to textual branches, allowing different formulations of the same reasoning strategy to repeatedly receive exploration bonuses. Our theoretical analysis shows that this count fragmentation can impair exploration efficiency, as quantified by increased cumulative regret. To address this problem, we introduce Learned Pseudo-Count MCTS (LPC-MCTS), which changes only the exploration statistic in UCT. A lightweight Coin-Flipping Network head on the policy's hidden representations estimates representation coverage, enabling related branches to share exploration evidence. Across 5 mathematical-reasoning and 7 search benchmarks, LPC-MCTS consistently outperforms strong MCTS baselines. Holding each LPC-MCTS-trained policy fixed, replacing standard MCTS counts at inference improves average mean@16 accuracy across 5 mathematical benchmarks by 3.79 percentage points on Qwen2.5-7B and 2.71 points on Qwen3-4B. Applying LPC-MCTS during both training and inference yields gains of 4.96 and 4.37 points over standard MCTS, respectively. Together, our theory and experiments show that changing what MCTS counts can substantially improve its exploration efficiency and reasoning performance.

### Thinking Harder Doesn't Lead to Better Choices: Reasoning Effort Increases the Confidence but Not the Correctness of LLM Shopping Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22033) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22033) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22033) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22033)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous shopping agents that process multimodal product listings (consisting of visual and text data) and recommend purchases on behalf of consumers. Naturally, one might assume that models with more thinking power would make higher-quality purchasing decisions. To test this belief, we ask two questions about such agents: does asking a model to think harder make it choose better products, and does the confidence it reports tell us the likelihood it may be wrong? We benchmark ten frontier models on three product-choice benchmarks built from WebShop, Amazon's SQID and Wayfair's WANDS platforms to see if LLMs with more thinking power actually make better-quality product purchase decisions. Across all three benchmarks, we found that increasing the thinking effort does not increase decision quality in a statistically meaningful way. The same change in thinking effort raises stated confidence at a statistically significant level, so extra thinking buys confidence rather than correctness. We also measure the calibration of these models (how well their reported confidence percentage matches their true correctness percentage) in these tasks and show that most models are not well-calibrated at every effort level. We also find that errors are strongly shared: when one model errs in a task, several others tend to err on that same task. Furthermore, on tasks where several models all err, a large number of them pick the same wrong product.

### What RL on Expert-Built Tasks Teaches Nemotron 3.5 Super
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22161) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22161) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22161) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22161)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

We post-train NVIDIA's Nemotron 3.5 Super (120B total, 12B active parameters) with reinforcement learning (RL) on five expert-built task datasets, one at a time: real-world software engineering, complex constraints, long-horizon tool use, professional chart understanding and enterprise document reasoning. Each run trains a fresh rank-32 LoRA adapter with CISPO on Tinker, with no supervised stage, and each adapter is compared with the base model on the same tasks, harness and settings. On twelve headline benchmarks, eight built by other groups and four by Surge AI, the gains range from +4.5 points on IFBench to +13.8 on GDP_pdf, which rises from 10.4% to 24.2%; SWE-bench Pro V2 rises from 86.9% to 92.1%, Terminal-Bench 2.1 from 63.9% to 70.3%, MultiChallenge from 58.3% to 66.6%, AutomationBench from 14.8% to 27.5% and Chartography from 39.4% to 50.6%. The document adapter, trained without API tools, also gains +8.0 points on AutomationBench. In attempts read side by side with the base model's on tasks each adapter newly solves, the base model's failures recur in a few modes, and each adapter does more often what the base model missed: the tool-use adapter finds the data and the rules before acting, the coding adapter writes its own test or scratch files on SWE-bench Pro V2, the constraints adapter carries earlier turns into its answers, the chart adapter measures the marks in code, and the document adapter reads AutomationBench's spreadsheets.

### Do LLM Agents Still Need to Be Told How to Self-Improve?
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23219) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23219) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23219) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23219)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

LLM agents increasingly drive their own learning. Self-improvement methods delegate various components of model optimization to the models themselves. As models become more capable, this motivates a basic question: do these increasingly capable models still need us to specify how they should self-improve? In this paper we study an autonomous paradigm in which, given basic primitives, resources, and a general goal for self-improvement, agents construct their own self-learning algorithm for context-based optimization. Using the setting of gameplay as a testbed, a frozen LLM agent is given a compute budget and tools for collecting experience and updating text documents, and allowed to freely experiment without an externally imposed loop or access to real-world data. We find that sufficiently capable models do self-improve under these conditions, demonstrating strategies that align with classic cognitive frameworks for self-regulated learning and constructs from machine learning literature on self-generated curricula. Furthermore, we study the extent to which the learned text artifacts transfer to external benchmarks, observing improvement across different game horizons, mechanics, and 3D navigation.

### Think Once, Act Fast: Cost-Effective Recursive Self-Improvement for Decision Model Harness
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22432) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22432) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22432) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22432)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Fast System-1 models enable responsive actions but struggle with complex or unfamiliar tasks. Large language model (LLM)-based System-2 provides strong reasoning capabilities, yet repeated invocation incurs substantial latency and computational costs. To bridge this gap, we propose JevRSI, a System-1/System-2 collaboration framework that transforms LLM reasoning into transferable decision support through recursive harness improvement. A Jev-style System-1 model enables low-latency action selection, while an asynchronous System-2 LLM develops a modular harness. The harness integrates procedures, state memory, and world models to provide decision contexts spanning the past, present, and future. Utility-driven retrieval selects modules based on both contextual relevance and their utility on previous tasks. Environmental feedback guides targeted revisions and enables recursive self-improvement. Experiments across three datasets show that JevRSI consistently improves decision quality, matching or surpassing LLM agents in several settings. It preserves low-latency execution with favorable performance-cost trade-offs. Moreover, System-2 development interventions decrease as the harness evolves, amortizing System-2 costs through continuous improvement. Our project is available at https://github.com/usail-hkust/JevRSI.

### When Anchors Shift the Target: Advantage-Gated Adaptation for Diffusion Reinforcement Learning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23038v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23038v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23038v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23038v1)

2026/11/17 16:37 GTM

Forward-process reinforcement learning adapts diffusion and flow-matching models by re-noising rewarded generations into regression targets. Because negative advantages induce concave updates with unbounded repulsive gradients, existing methods stabilize training using cached predictions from a moving policy copy, yet these anchors also alter what positive feedback teaches the model to fit. We formalize this conflict as anchor coupling: after separating frozen-reference regularizers, affine moving-anchor rules cannot eliminate negative curvature while preserving every positive regression target. We propose Advantage-Gated Adaptation (AGA) to decouple these roles. Positive samples retain standard target fitting, whose self-limiting update vanishes at the observed target independently of the moving anchor. Negative samples instead use the first-order tangent of the concave objective at the moving-policy prediction, preserving the local repulsion direction with a prediction-independent gradient. Within the quadratic-anchor family, these requirements uniquely determine the parameter-free gate $ω(a)=\max(-a,0)$. Because the moving copy provides local linearization rather than retention, AGA assigns long-horizon behavior preservation to a frozen pretrained reference regularized at its operating classifier-free guidance scale, interpreted through an implicit-reward lens. Across compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment, our experiments show that displacing positive targets degrades visual quality and heightens sensitivity to policy-copy schedules, whereas AGA consistently improves the reward--quality trade-off.

### Residual-Guided Lightweight Reinforcement Learning for Risk-Aware Trajectory Selection with a Frozen World Model
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22998) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22998) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22998) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22998)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

In automated driving, world models provide future scene predictions for evaluating candidate trajectories, but fixed selection rules may not capture scene-dependent trade-offs. We propose a residual-guided reinforcement learning (RL) framework for lightweight trajectory selection with a frozen world model and a fixed candidate bank. The framework uses proximal policy optimization (PPO) to fine-tune a joint policy over candidate selection and bounded trajectory residuals. A context-conditioned ranking network learns candidate preferences from feedback obtained by evaluating local perturbations of the selected trajectories. At inference, the selector combines learned preference and predicted risk ranks using fixed weights and returns the selected candidate trajectory without modification. Fine-tuning updates 16,106 parameters across the actor and critic, while the deployed selector retains only the 11,725-parameter ranking network, with the residual branch and critic used exclusively during training. In open-loop evaluation on the nuScenes validation set, the method reduces average Euclidean trajectory error (L2) by 3.99% relative to the RiskWorld model baseline without RL-based selection. Under matched budgets for feedback accesses and actor updates, residual exploration reduces L2 by 0.83% relative to the categorical PPO variant trained without residuals. The method also maintains lower L2 than RiskWorld when risk features are removed or shuffled.

### WATERFALL: Workflow for Adaptive Training with Evolutionary Reward Formulation and Automated Learning Loops
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22854) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22854) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22854) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22854)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Reward design remains a central bottleneck in reinforcement learning (RL), particularly in sparse-reward, long-horizon and partially observable settings requiring dependency chaining, memory, and deceptive affordance disambiguation. While recent foundation-model approaches reduce manual reward engineering, most assume goal-conditioning, task descriptions, expert demonstrations, or ground-truth metrics. These assumptions are problematic in partially observable environments, where task-relevant objectives, affordances, and dynamics must be discovered through interaction rather than disclosed a priori. We formalise this problem setting by introducing WATERFALL, an iterative population-based workflow for automated reward discovery under the strict observability constraints inherent in POMDP environments. WATERFALL achieves this by (i) synthesising diverse programmatic reward candidates via persona-conditioned generation, (ii) evaluating candidate behaviour from raw visual rollouts utilising a Swiss-system evolutionary tournament judged by vision-language models to identify elites, and (iii) leveraging longitudinal assessment histories to iteratively mutate, escalate, or prune candidates. We evaluate WATERFALL across fully observable and reveal-gated partially observable environments ranging from discrete to continuous, comparing against sparse-reward RL, intrinsic-motivation and foundation-model reward-synthesis baselines. Our results indicate that WATERFALL discovers and refines reward programs without access to privileged information, outperforming baselines that retain their native information advantages, most clearly in the POMDP regime. Extensive ablations substantiate that visual evidence, tournament-based selection, and iterative refinement are jointly important. Enforcing this contract is what separates autonomous reward discovery from benchmark-privileged reward shaping.

### Harnesses That Improve Themselves: A Unifying Framework for Self-Improving Agent Harnesses
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22299) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22299) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22299) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22299)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Whether a foundation model succeeds as an autonomous agent depends as much on its runtime software as on its weights. This runtime, the agent harness, manages context and memory, steers multi-turn control, executes tool actions in sandboxes, and verifies work before stopping. Because models differ in how they use tools and how they fail, a static harness tuned for one model or task often stalls, wastes tokens, or regresses on another. This bottleneck has driven a shift from prompt tuning to self-improving agent harnesses: systems that inspect their own execution traces, separate runtime bugs from model limits, and rewrite their own code. We present a unifying architectural and design framework for self-improving agent harnesses. The framework pairs four editable runtime components (context and state, agent control, action interfaces, and verification and recovery) with a four-stage loop that diagnoses failures, proposes code patches, validates candidates, and deploys updates. Using this framework, we show how optimizers trace errors across long trajectories and discover reusable runtime mechanisms such as pre-stop verifiers, loop breakers, and persistent memory. We further show why full-harness rewrites overfit or break across modules, and how modular updates with validation gates generalize across models while bounding token cost. To keep self-modification safe, we establish practical defenses against recurring failure modes including prompt bloat, benchmark gaming, and evaluator tampering. Finally, experiments on SWE-bench Lite validate this framework, showing that gated modular evolution outperforms prompt reflection and full-harness rewrites across models and repositories at a fraction of the token cost.

### The Between-Round Decision in Autonomous Research: An LLM Controller That Reads the Whole Trajectory
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22661) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22661) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22661) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22661)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Executing an idea often changes how promising it looks, so the next step in autonomous research should rest on results, not on proposals. After each round of experiments, the system must decide whether to refine the best solution or start a new direction, which solution to build from, and what goal to set. We call this the between-round decision. Existing systems wrap it in machinery such as rules, scores, search trees, and schedules; where an LLM makes it, what the LLM needs to read is left open. We ask whether an LLM can make this decision from accumulated evidence, without a search structure around it, and how much of that evidence it needs to read. We present BRED (Between-Round Evidence-based Decision), an LLM-based controller that maintains the best verified solution so far (the Champion) and a log of past rounds, and makes one decision per round. On three widely used research-agent benchmark tasks, BRED improves on the task baselines by 4.80% in MAE on ETTh1 (to 0.4172) and by 3.44 and 3.43 points of accuracy on SST-2 (to 95.07%) and CIFAR-100 (to 84.63%), ahead of the published results. When the controller does not read the accumulated evidence, the same loop gains less: with no history it reaches 0.4299 on ETTh1 and 83.71% on CIFAR-100, whereas the full controller's MAE is 2.95% lower and its accuracy 0.92 points higher. On a new task, speech-based depression detection under a leakage-free protocol, it reaches a macro-F1 of 0.6164 on seeds held out from selection, 5.5 points above its baseline and 4.0 above the strongest published system. Code is available at https://github.com/learninginvision/BRED.

### When Agent Skills Go Stale: Runtime Behavior and Selective Maintenance in Evolving Environments
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22525) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22525) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22525) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22525)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Agent skills use past experience and current observations to guide decisions and actions on related tasks. This experience is retained in skill state, whose reuse can amortize inference-time reasoning and environment interaction. When environment changes, parts of this state can become outdated while others remain useful. Across four task settings and two models, we compare skill reuse before and after environment changes. Previously beneficial skills can reduce task performance by over 10 percentage points or increase execution cost by nearly 40% relative to no skill. Task success can also remain unchanged while execution savings decline. These effects depend on which environment-specific details become outdated and the agent's ability to compensate using current observations. We introduce selective maintenance, which replays checks from successful executions without additional model calls. It verifies whether observed differences conflict with skill state, repairs affected content, and validates updates, rebuilding when local repair is insufficient. No description of the change is supplied. Relative to reusing stale state, maintenance improves task accuracy by up to 67pp and reduces execution steps by up to 37%. These results suggest that preserving a skill's inference time value as environments evolve requires selectively maintaining its state.

### Consistency Is Not Correction: Separating Confidence, Abstention, and Recovery in LLM Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21651) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21651) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21651) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21651)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

A controller that resamples an answer, escalates to a larger model, or abstains is usually justified by a confidence signal. Three claims about such a signal do not follow from one another: it ranks answers (discrimination), it supports withholding some of them (selective prediction), and it changes an answer for the better (correction utility). We give an exact decomposition of the third into gated recoveries minus gated harms, which bounds whether a comparison can succeed before it is run: a gate that only replaces a proposal can act only where the base policy was already wrong, so the initial error count caps the discordant pairs any correct gate can produce. When that cap falls below the count a paired test needs, the experiment cannot return a positive result for a working mechanism, nor distinguish one from a gate that never fired. We measure all three targets on two model scales and find the bound binds. In a small-model suite a perturbation-agreement verifier reaches within-policy AUROC 0.990 (95% interval [0.95, 1.00]) while every episode outcome matches single-sample decoding at 4.32 times the call count. In a controlled 840-episode study no verifier replaced the proposal on any of the 60 GSM8K decisions, so that comparison tested nothing; the entire GSM8K gain across six controllers is one task, and it is the task whose identical temperature-zero repeats disagreed, making it the one answer the service does not reproduce. On the diagnostic set the gate could reach four errors while the test needed six, so even a perfect gate had no significant result available. We report two checks that would have caught this before any budget was spent, and show that tie handling in rankings, tied selection cutoffs, and unequal output limits each move a headline number in the same dataset. The binding constraint on this literature is measurement resolution, not the choice of signal.

### Learning to Undo: Transfer Reinforcement Learning under State Space Transformations
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23081) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23081) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23081) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23081)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Transfer learning in reinforcement learning (RL) has shown strong empirical success. In this work, we take a more principled perspective by studying when and how transferring knowledge between MDPs (a source and a target) can be provably beneficial. Specifically, we consider the case where there exists an undo map such that applying this map to the target's state space recovers the source exactly. We propose an algorithm that learns this map by matching state feature statistics gathered from both MDPs, and then uses it to transfer the source policy to the target. We theoretically justify the algorithm by analyzing the setting when the undo map is linear and the source is linearly-$Q^\star$ realizable, where our approach has strictly better sample complexity than tabula rasa RL in the target MDP. Empirically, we demonstrate that these benefits extend beyond this regime: on challenging continuous control tasks and Atari games, our method achieves significantly better sample efficiency. Overall, our results highlight how shared structure between tasks can be leveraged for efficient transfer of policies across environments.

### M3OPD-Reasoner: An Open Recipe for Post-Training Multimodal Reasoning with Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23034v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23034v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23034v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23034v1)

2026/11/17 16:36 GTM

Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (MOPD) enables a single student to absorb the skills of several domain teachers by learning from its own generated responses. We present Multimodal Model Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation Reasoner (M3OPD-Reasoner), a fully open recipe for post-training vision-language models (VLMs) over three reasoning domains: image STEM, image perception, and video. We curate two datasets, M3OPD-SFT-1.35M for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and M3OPD-RL-193K for reinforcement learning (RL) with verifiable rewards. On these data, we compare post-training recipes that start from Qwen3.5-9B: training one model on the mixed data of all domains (MixSFT and MixRL), and training one teacher per domain with Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO), directly or after an SFT warm-up, and distilling the teachers into one student with MOPD. We evaluate on 21 image STEM, image perception, and video reasoning benchmarks after we decontaminate the training data against them. The comparison establishes MOPD of teachers trained with GSPO directly from Qwen3.5-9B as the best recipe: the student closely matches each teacher on its own domain and is on average above the mixed-training baselines on the same prompts. Since MOPD already merges the domain capabilities that MixRL would otherwise have to learn, its student is a stronger starting point for RL, and we ask whether the same data can carry the model further from there: we add MixRL on top of the MOPD student, on the same prompts and with the same settings, which, to our knowledge, no prior VLM recipe has studied. The same prompts still improve the MOPD student, with no collapse, and give our main model, M3OPD-Reasoner-9B. MOPD and MixRL can therefore work as consecutive stages that get more out of the same data. We release all data, the training code, and the checkpoints of every stage.

### Multi-Agent Memory Inconsistency: Where Factual Conflicts Disappear in LLM Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22245) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22245) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22245) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22245)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Multi-agent delegation lets LLM systems distribute evidence collection across workers with separate contexts. This creates a consistency challenge: facts retrieved by workers may be missing from the evidence available to the lead for a joint conclusion. We introduce a benchmark and evidence-flow profiler for studying multi-agent memory inconsistency through controlled factual conflicts in Harvey LAB document-review tasks. Pooled across three models with comparable generation spending, requiring delegation increases retrieval of both conflicting claims from 50.2% to 68.7% relative to single-agent execution, yet conflict detection falls from 16.2% to 13.2%. Within this setting, workers report both claims for only 35.2% of retrieved pairs, and pooled matched comparisons show higher detection than single-agent execution when one worker reads both claims but lower detection when the claims are split across workers. Worker-to-lead handoffs are a central target for improving conflict detection in multi-agent LLMs.

### The Fragility of Reasoning-Importance Attribution for Pruning Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22234) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22234) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22234) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22234)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Attribution-guided pruning scores components by their causal contribution and protects the important ones, and it is increasingly applied to compress reasoning language models without destroying their chain of thought. Its reliability rests on an unexamined assumption: that reasoning importance can be faithfully measured. Across four small reasoning models, we show this measurement is fragile in three ways, each of which changes pruning decisions. First, scoring final-answer tokens, the natural choice, saturates in every chain-of-thought model we test (answer-token negative log-likelihood, NLL, of $10^{-4}$--$10^{-3}$) because the model states its answer inside its own reasoning; guiding head pruning with this target costs 27.6 and 32.2 accuracy points relative to scoring reasoning tokens on the two models we power with five seeds, while perplexity barely moves and even reverses sign at 7B. Second, causal importance is not localizable to single units: single-neuron effects sit at the measurement floor, their cross-split ranking is noise, and they cancel: a group's ablation effect disagrees in sign with the summed effects of its members in 90\% of groups. Reproducible importance emerges only at groups of $\sim 100$ neurons. Third, contrastively isolated ``reasoning-specific'' importance is not separable from generic importance, and protecting it is worse than protecting raw importance. We additionally pre-registered the hypothesis that distillation-induced concentration of importance predicts the pruning cost; it is not supported, and since our grid confounds concentration with scale, we report it as unresolved rather than reversed. The result is a short set of measurement rules: score reasoning tokens, attribute at coarse granularity, and evaluate with accuracy rather than perplexity.

### Eyes in the Update? Learning Where Rewards Land in Multimodal Reasoning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22438) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22438) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22438) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22438)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Multimodal post-training has moved from broad instruction tuning toward reinforcement learning for image and video reasoning, where outcome rewards can improve final-answer accuracy. Yet these rewards are assigned to complete generated trajectories, while visual evidence is used only at sparse and uneven positions inside the response. Recent work has begun to make multimodal RL more token-sensitive through grounding signals or external token-level weighting. We ask a more specific question: can the allocation of the same trajectory-level reward be inferred from the model's own multimodal computation, rather than from an auxiliary annotator, perception module, or process label? We propose Gaze-RL, a representation-driven policy optimization method that redistributes completion-level advantage through a latent reward landing field. The field is computed from internal cross-modal geometry and local predictive uncertainty, assigning larger update weight to positions that are both visually anchored and decision-bearing while remaining mean-one, so the total credit of each rollout is preserved and only its positionwise allocation changes. Gaze-RL requires no process supervision, auxiliary perception module, or architectural modification, and improves both Qwen2.5-VL and InternVL3.5 across a broad range of image and video benchmarks.

### Language Models Can Rewrite Their Own Weights
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21885) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21885) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21885) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21885)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Language models are trained once, frozen and deployed: improving one again takes another training run, and a prompt, a scaffold or extra computation at inference is gone once the task ends. A frontier model has been shown to edit the weights of a much smaller one. We ask whether a model can rewrite its own weights to make itself better, and find that it can, by its own reasoning, with no gradients and no training; we call this self-rewriting. Given its weights, graded answers and the ability to run itself, a 27B model diagnoses its failure and edits the weights behind it. It surpasses itself: its own edit makes it 6.6 points more accurate on 490 problems it never saw, in a test fixed in advance, and another session's edit also makes it significantly better. It keeps getting better: it submits an edit only when its own tests support it, each edit replacing the last, and over its first eight such sessions its accuracy only rises. Unlike test-time scaling, the gain persists in its weights and adds no cost per answer; unlike training, it needs no pre- or post-training infrastructure, only an open model and hours on one GPU. It repairs a damaged copy of itself: it locates the corrupted matrix and rewrites it with values of its own, never reading the undamaged weights; found on text math problems alone, the repair lifts the copy on six image benchmarks it never saw from 7% to 70% accuracy, 83% of the undamaged model's. And the ability emerges with scale: across four sizes of one family, the 2B model makes no edit and the 4B and 9B models break themselves; only the 27B model works on itself as a human would, comparing its hidden states on answers that finish and on answers that run out of tokens, weakening the direction that separates them. Like a human, a model need not separate training from inference; improving a language model may come to take a conversation rather than a training run.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22838) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22838) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22838) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22838)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Generative recommenders decode the semantic ID of a user's next item with a language model, and post-training them with reinforcement learning (RL) after supervised fine-tuning (SFT) has become standard practice. The gains are mostly reported on the first ten results, yet in deployment such models hand hundreds of candidates to a downstream ranker or reward model. We show that RL post-training has a hidden cost: it improves accuracy at the top of the list (hit@K for small K) but, on all five datasets we study, lowers the recall of the candidate list (hit@K for K in the hundreds). The model becomes a better ranker and a worse retriever. Controlled experiments trace the loss to the negative advantages that RL assigns to the candidates it samples, the rollout. The root cause is that only one next item is logged per user, so RL treats every other candidate as wrong. Yet these candidates are the model's most plausible alternatives, and often the next items of other users with similar histories. Penalising them pushes down exactly what similar users choose next, and these items drop out of those users' candidate lists. This diagnosis points directly to a remedy: out-of-rollout anchoring, a single additional loss term that preserves the SFT model's ordering of the items outside the rollout. It keeps the gain at the top and restores recall to the SFT level on all five datasets, and it is effective under other RL configurations as well. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic study of this recall loss: we measure it, identify its cause, and show that, once the cause is known, a simple remedy suffices.

### CrossEnv: Synthesizing Verifiable Cross-Environment Tasks for Long-Horizon Agent Training
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22777v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22777v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22777v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22777v1)

2026/11/17 13:44 GTM

Executable synthetic environments provide scalable training tasks and verifiable feedback for long-horizon agents. Existing environment synthesis methods primarily generate tasks within individual environments. However, these tasks do not adequately capture real-world interactions across services, where actions in one service depend on information obtained from another. Synthesizing such tasks is challenging because data generated across environments must remain consistent to support executable and verifiable interactions. To address this challenge, we introduce CrossEnv, a framework that uses an entity-tool graph to identify connections between environments and aligns task-specific data to synthesize executable, verifiable cross-environment tasks. We further propose CrossEnv-Curriculum, which progresses from single-environment tool use to coordination across more environments and business domains, before introducing user interaction. Across four external benchmarks, combining cross-environment training data with this curriculum achieves competitive performance with fewer training examples. Further analyses support the benefits of cross-environment data and curriculum training, and provide evidence of transfer to new environment combinations, external tasks less similar to the training data, and longer workflows.

Vulnerability exploitation tests whether language model agents can develop working exploits from known software flaws. To test whether context management can elicit further capability, we propose SCoRe, which compacts context at stop bursts and marks these judgments as unproven. SCoRe improves verified capability over summary-based continuation and other evaluated context-management baselines. With SCoRe, MiMo reaches a capability coverage score of 67.0% at 2,000 turns, exceeding both 32.4% under the original stopping policy and the 58.6% computed for GPT-5.5 from ExploitBench's 300-turn reference results on the same tasks. These findings show that stopping need not mark an agent's exploitation limit, and that continued execution with context management can elicit substantial further capability.

The agent outperformed both rules (-7.8%, compared with -3.9% and -6.3%) and adopted midday charging during the solar off-peak period, yet it obtained only about 45% of the saving achieved by the perfect-foresight schedule and did little to limit the demand peak. These results suggest that studies of DRL for HVAC control should state the margin between the best rule and a near-optimal reference. Large language models (LLMs) can now generate Lean proof scripts that capture the high-level structure of many mathematical arguments. This paper presents MathEye, a framework that converts imperfect LLM-generated proof scripts into fully verified Lean proofs by combining symbolic reasoning with minimal human interaction. At the core of MathEye is angelic proof checking, an optimistic execution model that replaces failing proof fragments with placeholders while preserving the overall structure of the argument. This optimistic check produces an angelic proof that isolates the exact proof obligations that the LLM fails to discharge. Because these unresolved obligations are often logically interdependent, asking a mathematician to address every placeholder directly is inefficient. To minimize human effort, MathEye performs residual analysis over a proof dependence graph that captures symbol-level data flow in the angelic proof. By formulating obligation selection as a MaxSAT optimization problem, MathEye selects a minimal-cost set of essential obligations whose resolution suffices to complete the entire proof. MathEye presents these essential obligations to the mathematician in ordinary mathematical language and uses the mathematician's answers to automatically reconstruct the complete Lean proof. Experiments on 30 real-world benchmarks across analysis and algebra show that MathEye solves 93.3% of the problems while reducing the number of required user interactions by 55%.

Masking the cited entity on every frame, instead of the cited box, flips 1.9 times as many answers as its control. The NExT-QA chains were supervised to cite two frames of entities that stay visible on most frames, so this outcome is what the supervision predicts; the point is that the usual check on grounded chains cannot see it. A citation can be verifiable and relevant without the answer depending on it, and a test of grounded reasoning should remove what a chain names, not only where it points.

Language agents rely on long-term memory to personalize responses across extended interactions, yet stored facts quietly go stale as the world changes, and an agent often learns that using memory hurt only through delayed feedback after acting on an outdated record. We then propose RegimeCouncil, a lightweight paired-evidence router that retains both scopes as advisors and arbitrates between them online without retraining the LLM. Using revealed comparisons between answering with memory and answering without memory (the no-memory anchor), a Local Advisor tracks the latest outcome on the queried fact alone, while a Global Advisor pools recent outcomes across all stored facts. A Meta Governor follows the Local Advisor by default to avoid cross-fact interference and switches to the Global Advisor only when global pooling accumulates a strictly positive recent utility margin, guaranteeing finite-time switching under delayed feedback. Across LongMemEval update streams and real-world historical drift on TempLAMA and TAQA with Qwen3-30B-A3B and GPT-4o, RegimeCouncil outperforms the better fixed-scope policy by 5.2-7.3 points on primary disagreement episodes and across all 30 held-out settings, while ablations show that removing any of its three modules reduces accuracy by 2-7 points.

### The Resolution Hypothesis: Agent Evolution's Key Is Identifying Improvements
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23068v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23068v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23068v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23068v1)

2026/11/17 16:57 GTM

Agent evolution follows a simple loop: a proposer writes candidate artifacts (prompts, skills, memories) and selection accepts those that score higher. This loop has shown promise, yet practitioners face a puzzle: methods trade wins across papers, and evolution often fails to bootstrap on certain tasks. We trace this puzzle to the Resolution Hypothesis: agent evolution stalls not because methods fail to propose better artifacts, but because noisy evaluations cannot reliably identify them. Across seven evolution methods and six models (over 10M LLM calls), we find support for the hypothesis at both levels where evaluation scores are used. At the candidate level (choosing artifacts), helpful candidate artifacts exist, but selection is no better than random at telling them from harmful ones, so errors accumulate across rounds. At the method level (ranking methods), typical margins of a few percentage points are smaller than the difference between two independent runs of the same method, so published rankings often rest on luck. Together, these findings indicate that agent evolution is often bottlenecked by reliably identifying improvements. This calls for a shift in how we develop and evaluate self-improving agents, from designing ever more candidate improvement systems to making true improvements detectable and accumulable.

### Ambient LLMs: Data-Efficient Supervised Fine-tuning via Temperature Matching
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23229v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23229v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23229v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23229v1)

2026/11/17 18:59 GTM

Collecting large-scale, diverse, and high-quality post-training datasets for autoregressive language models is challenging. Consequently, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) is typically applied to datasets significantly smaller than those used for pre-training, which leads to overfitting and the catastrophic forgetting of the tails of the data distribution. To prevent this collapse, we introduce Ambient SFT, a principled framework for leveraging imperfect predictions to augment the fine-tuning set. In our method, a "frozen" version of the pre-trained model serves as a noisy teacher that the fine-tuned model must match at a coarse level. In particular, we require that the high-temperature versions of the conditional distributions produced by the post-training model should match the high-temperature versions of the pre-training ones. The temperature hyperparameter controls the level of trust in the pre-trained model, with the limit $T\to \infty$ reverting back to regular SFT and $T=1$ to knowledge distillation. We demonstrate that our approach outperforms standard SFT across domain adaptation, instruction-tuning, and reasoning, leading for up to $21\%$ average relative improvement to standard SFT.

### Proximal Targets: Training Looped Refiners on Problems with Many Correct Answers
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22737) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22737) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22737) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22737)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Looped refiners such as HRM and TRM apply one small network repeatedly to a draft answer, and deep supervision trains every pass toward one stored answer per puzzle. We prove that when a puzzle has many correct answers this target sets a loss floor that no model capacity removes, and that the loss-minimising pass blends the answers into an invalid grid. We propose the proximal target: train each pass toward the correct answer nearest to its own input, found by scanning a list of answers, by a search solver started at the draft, or within a band of quality set by a knob; correct answers become fixed points, and a pass only has to make fewer errors than its input has for the loop to converge, which its training loss bounds. With the same 1.7M-parameter network and 5 seeds, the proximal target solves 98.8% of Sudoku puzzles with 2-64 answers (standard target: 2.5%), 93.8% of sparse puzzles with more than 10,000 answers without any list of answers (0.0%), and 90.9% of 200-node 3-colourings after training on graphs of at most 50 nodes (36.9%), and different random starts end at different answers. Against GRAM, a stochastic-latent reasoner, a single run is correct far more often (98.8% vs. 57.7%) and twenty runs find more of the answers (53.6% vs. 37.4%), while GRAM is slightly ahead on its own 10-node colouring benchmark; in Scrabble the knob becomes a calibrated strength dial.

Tool-using AI agents change files, databases, access rights and public information, yet permission to call a tool does not establish whether a mistake can be undone. The pinned implementation and evidence-linked registry cover 536 tools across 20 MCP servers. All 738 rule-case checks conform to declared expectations, while native annotation mapping differs from draft registry labels for 118 of 481 comparable tools. One AI-assisted reviewer approves 148 ratings, 75 matching the registry; this is not independent agreement. On 2,158 recorded runs, the implemented session guard gates the associated action in all 29 attributable attack-positive runs, compared with 18 for a read/write proxy, while adding nine approval points across 1,209 benign runs. Controlled file, Git, SQLite and three-browser experiments show that recovery depends on captured state, later changes, editor storage and recovery route, and expose a Git merge-state exception. The evidence supports making recovery assumptions explicit and testable. It does not establish registry-wide accuracy, reduced production harm or general recovery reliability.

### DACE: Diversity-Driven Adversarial Co-Evolution for Robust LLM Safety Alignment
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22278) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22278) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22278) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22278)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Ensuring robust safety alignment of large language models (LLMs) is increasingly difficult as adversarial attacks evolve and outpace alignment pipelines built on pre-collected data. Existing co-evolutionary frameworks let the defender chase a moving attacker, yet their training dynamics expose two compounding pathologies: \emph{attack strategy collapse}, where the attacker overfits to a narrow set of high-reward rewrites, and \emph{defense adversarial forgetting}, where the defender loses competence against earlier attacks as the attack distribution drifts. Existing remedies are partial: attacker-side diversity rewards score textual novelty rather than strategy novelty, and defender-side replay relies on discrete judge scores that ignore estimation uncertainty, response stochasticity, and defender non-stationarity. We introduce \textbf{\method}, a diversity-driven adversarial co-evolution framework that targets both pathologies jointly. On the attacker side, \method couples an explicit two-dimensional strategy space (risk category $\times$ attack style) with a \emph{normalized marginal coverage gain} reward, providing a bounded, non-vanishing exploration signal at the strategy layer. On the defender side, \method maintains a unified \emph{Bayesian adversarial replay pool} whose Beta--Bernoulli threat posteriors are refreshed by time decay and sampled via Thompson sampling, anchoring defender training to an evolving threat landscape. Across standard safety, automated-attacker, and general-capability benchmarks, \method improves robustness to out-of-distribution attacks while preserving general capabilities, and yields broader attacker strategy coverage.

General-purpose behavior models increasingly internalize broad procedural knowledge during training, making their adaptation to new goals and skill interfaces less a problem of learning entirely new behaviors than of reconfiguring existing competence. We study this capability as reconfigurability, the ability to realize new behaviors by cost-efficiently restructuring existing components and relations under sparse governing information, where task specifications provide high-level goals and procedure specifications expose a limited set of reusable factors, leaving the concrete bindings and compositions required for execution underdetermined. We propose RecPo, a framework that represents skills task-agnostically through multiple procedure views and materializes executable skill sequences by composing stored transitions to realize task-specific changes. RecPo maintains a procedure knowledge base of skill-induced transitions, with each view exposing a reusable factor and preserving local skill connectivity. At deployment, it interprets task specifications through the same views and resolves the resulting compositional queries over the stored relations to bind skills for execution. On long-horizon tasks in an extended Franka Kitchen benchmark, RecPo achieves 91.9% ordered completion with 1.0 ms of task inference per episode across varying procedure support and task-record sparsity. Real-robot studies further demonstrate adaptation through local memory edits without task-specific retraining.

### ForecastGym: A Live, Controlled, and Replayable Benchmark for Forecasting Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23045v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23045v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23045v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23045v1)

2026/11/17 16:44 GTM

Agents built on large language models (LLMs) increasingly forecast real-world events by searching the Web and reasoning over what they find. How well such an agent forecasts depends on both the retrieved evidence and the model, making control over that evidence essential for evaluation. Live evaluations can mix retrieval with reasoning when each system searches on its own, while retrospective search can leak later information through date filters or today's ranking of historical pages. Comparing models, search sources, and memory strategies requires both fixed evidence and a record of how it evolved before resolution. We introduce ForecastGym, which records what four commercial web search APIs return for open prediction-market questions every two days and replays this frozen evidence offline once outcomes are known, comparing models, memory, and captured search sources under a fixed evidence-acquisition policy. Its first release covers 150 questions, 614 evidence states, one per question and collection time, and 131k search results. Rerunning the same queries after settlement returns results that all but state the outcome for up to 92% of the questions, despite the tested date filters. On the frozen evidence, all tested LLMs trail the market in forecasting accuracy. Our diagnostic analysis suggests that missed public information contributes more to the gap in high-gap states than misreading does. Switching the search API changes a model's Brier score by up to 0.043, more than the spread across models, while persistent notes score similarly to rereading all evidence at half the input tokens. The evidence a forecaster sees can matter as much as the model that reads it.

### Long-Context Generation Is a Sampling Problem (When You Turn Up the Heat)
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22148) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22148) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22148) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22148)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Open-weight language models are widely observed to get worse the longer they generate. Often, attempts to fix this involve more training. At high temperature, The sampler, i.e. the rule that picks the next token out of the model's probabilities, decides whether long and agentic generation survives and/or thrives. We test 29 open-weight models from 1B to 122B parameters on ten established benchmarks plus needle retrieval out to one million tokens, covering SWE-bench Verified coding agents, long chain-of-thought reasoning, long-form writing, long structured output and long-context reading. Samplers that keep their pool of candidate tokens small and steady as temperature rises don't lose accuracy. Samplers that let in low-probability tokens fall apart. At temperature 2, coding agents running top-p, top-k or Top-H resolve 0 of 100 GitHub issues, while the same agents running top-nsigma resolve 46 to 62 and win every issue the two samplers disagree on, on three Qwen3.5 models. Over all benchmarks top-nsigma keeps a median 100% of its temperature-1 score where top-p keeps 26%. Counting how many tokens each sampler lets through explains why. In one Llama-3.1-8B story, going from temperature 1 to 2 grows top-p's pool from 4 tokens to 51,845 while top-nsigma stays at 3. Across 133 combinations of sampler setting and temperature, the typical pool size predicts judged quality (Spearman correlation of -0.77), and when we pick the pool at temperature 1 and only apply temperature afterwards, Top-H, min-p and p-less stop collapsing. At the default temperature of 1 most samplers perform about the same, We release all code and data to the public.

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Scientific document reasoning requires an agent to acquire and integrate evidence distributed across text, figures, and tables. Retrieval can identify relevant sources while missing complementary support, and answer-level feedback alone does not reveal whether an error stems from evidence acquisition or evidence use. We present an agentic Retriever--Perceiver--Reasoner framework for multimodal scientific document reasoning. The Retriever sequentially selects source-aligned evidence under a context budget. Given the selected evidence and question, the Perceiver constructs a question-conditioned latent ontology, an implicit organization of multimodal evidence in a shared continuous space. The Reasoner combines this latent guidance with the original sources to produce grounded answers. We train the three components through diagnostic-guided co-evolution: alternating updates optimize them with a shared answering reward, while Retrieval Potential Feedback (RPF) and Latent Efficacy Feedback (LEF) identify questions that need better retrieval or latent guidance and direct targeted resampling. Across four benchmarks and seven evaluation settings, our framework obtains average scores of 69.9 and 75.0 with 7B and 8B Reasoner backbones, exceeding the strongest baseline by 4.0 and 9.1 points, respectively.

### RAG in a Trenchcoat: When Minimal Memory Is Enough for Agentic Systems, and When It Isn't
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22179) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22179) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22179) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22179)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Agentic memory research has accumulated extraction, supersession, reflection, and graph synthesis on top of retrieval-augmented generation. However, we found that almost none of those primitives earn their cost in our tested memory benchmarks: we show this with BRIDGE, a deliberately minimal RAG memory with chained observation notes, hybrid BM25 with cosine retrieval, and chronological rendering, which reaches parity within bootstrap CI with the prior SOTA on LongMemEval-S and BEAM, and beats the best memory baseline on EverMemBench by 5.7-6.3 points at matched reader. It runs at ~5-7K reader-prompt tokens per query, using a single LLM call for each ingestion or query. We argue that this parity is itself the result: standard benchmarks score what memory retrieves, not what memory enables when the agent acts. To test the latter, we introduce five adversarial probes (Rule Application +-, Pattern Induction Style / Procedural, Dynamic Multiturn) judged at generation time by a cross vendor verification chain, where each query can only be solved by applying a remembered rule or pattern it never mentions. Across 5 probes x 20 scenarios, no architecture wins universally: atomic fact extractors (LangMem) win rule application, while BRIDGE's block-summary memory wins pattern induction. This result holds across four domains, three graders, and three reader families. Each design fails where the other wins, for reasons a recall benchmark cannot see: atomic-fact extractors discard the form a demonstrated pattern takes, while block-summary memory retrieves the rule but the reader does not act on it, a gap that presenting the rule more prominently largely closes. What separates memory designs is not what they retrieve but what they enable an agent to do, and that is what agentic memory should be evaluated on.

### Learning Agent-Centric Vision-Language-Action World Models for Autonomous Driving
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22880v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22880v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22880v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22880v1)

2026/11/17 14:53 GTM

Recent progress in integrating vision-language-action (VLA) models with world modeling has significantly advanced end-to-end autonomous driving by allowing systems to reason about imagined futures instead of merely reacting to current observations. However, most world VLA models generate future frames at the pixel level with plenty of dense visual tokens. Although such generated futures can be visually plausible, they preserve many driving-irrelevant appearance details, i.e., only a small portion of the tokens directly encode critical planning-relevant factors, such as agent geometry, motion dynamics, and interactions. This introduces a significant performance bottleneck. An important question thus arises: what world state should a world VLA model reason over to support planning beyond pixel-level prediction? We argue that planning-oriented world modeling should focus on a compact and structured agent-centric latent state that captures the interactions between objects in the driving scene over time. As such, we introduce AgentWorld, an agent-centric VLA world model that uses discrete agent tokens jointly for reasoning and trajectory planning. Each token is dynamically grounded to a surrounding agent associated with its object's state, such as semantic identity, 3D geometry, motion, and interaction context. AgentWorld learns these tokens through three key processes: explicit geometric grounding, reasoning-oriented supervised fine-tuning, and agent-oriented reinforcement learning. Extensive open-loop and closed-loop experiments under diverse driving scenarios show that AgentWorld enhances planning capabilities, fully exploits visual evidence and agent dynamics, and yields interpretable agent-centric world states.

### KV-LTM: Persistent Per-Session KV Caches as Long-Term Memory for Personal Assistants
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21998) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21998) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21998) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21998)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Personal assistants accumulate months of conversations, but today's long-term memory stacks put derivation or selection between a question and its evidence. In a system-level comparison on LongMemEval, two commercial assistants scored well below the same model reading the history offline [1], consistent with, but not isolating, content loss between write and read. We propose KV-LTM: a memory tier storing each session's raw text plus its KV cache under a fixed reader model, answering by restoring cached sessions and appending the question. KV-LTM is complementary to, not a replacement for, retrieval and indexing. Text is the memory of record; the cache is a recompute-free prefill accelerator, degrading gracefully to text. We specify the design: storage record, budget-derived retention, append-only read path, and bounded aggregation. We size it analytically: across pinned BF16 full-attention (GQA) and latent-cached MLA configurations, per-token state spans approximately 31K-328K bytes (the pinned KDA+MLA hybrid differs: approximately 8.1K bytes/token plus a fixed per-session recurrent term), giving approximately 0.34-3.6 TB per user-year at a declared 30K-token/day scenario, against approximately 49 MB of raw text; per-query cost is modelled as regime-conditional transfer, attention, decode, aggregation, and compute. We define an evaluation protocol needing no persistent-cache implementation for logical QA evaluation: per-session prefill emulates valid cache reuse under the same model/input-state contract and ideal arithmetic, with named arms, controlled baselines, and a preregistered reader-model gate; a separate minimal real-cache replay tests numerical equivalence and measures the cost axis, with outcomes not assumed. KV-LTM trades storage for avoided re-prefill compute and write-time fidelity, within a declared retention budget.

### Runtime Verification of Multiple Natural Language Criteria for Agent Governance
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23074v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23074v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23074v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23074v1)

2026/11/17 17:01 GTM

AI agents are governed by hundreds of natural language criteria, yet existing verifiers cannot efficiently evaluate an agent's output against a large set of such criteria and report which are satisfied, violated, or inapplicable. We introduce the VFM, a tree-attention verifier that encodes the shared context and target text, and scores N natural language criteria in parallel, returning independent per-criterion ternary verdicts. The VFM is pretrained on CriteriaBank, a corpus of 394K (context, target, criterion, verdict) tuples spanning over 321K distinct criterion strings. A finetuned VFM-4B outperforms GPT-5.4 on a synthetic governance benchmark. On the DynaBench multi-criterion benchmark, it outperforms DynaGuard-4B on trace-level failure detection, and its per-criterion verdicts localize the violated rule and support focused escalation to a larger chain-of-thought judge. We show that CriteriaBank pretraining improves cross-domain transfer, particularly in the low-data regime, while matching a strong direct-finetuning control at full data. On a single H100, VFM-4B evaluates 1,000 criteria against a 4K-token context in under 2s at <11 GB VRAM, making it suitable for runtime verification.

### Structure--Residual Inductive Biases for Language Model Pretraining
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23197v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23197v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23197v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23197v1)

2026/11/17 18:38 GTM

Next-token prediction is relied upon to learn both predictive behavior and internal representations. We ask whether representation structure itself should be an explicit inductive bias. Motivated by the two-part coding view of Kolmogorov complexity, we study a structure--residual principle in which a representation separates structure from realization detail. We instantiate this principle in two complementary ways. Boundary Bottleneck Concept (BBC) routes information from token segments through boundary carriers while preserving local autoregressive computation. Continuous Concept (CC) regularizes representation geometry and induces a spectral decomposition into low-frequency structure and high-frequency residual. We derive coding bounds for both views and show that multiplicity-corrected boundary attention becomes exact as the within-segment path energy vanishes. Empirically, BBC and CC exhibit strikingly different depth preferences: BBC works best in early layers, whereas CC is most effective in later layers. At 300M training tokens, combining BBC and CC improves NLL by 1.44\% on GPT-2 Medium. These gains persist with training runs up to 20B tokens. Representation diagnostics show that BBC increases the relative predictive importance of boundary states, while CC reduces spectral residual energy. Together, these results suggest that structure--residual biases can improve language-model pretraining beyond next-token prediction. Code: https://github.com/haihuang-ml/structure-residual

### Structure--Residual Inductive Biases for Language Model Pretraining
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23197) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23197) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23197) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23197)

Next-token prediction is relied upon to learn both predictive behavior and internal representations. We ask whether representation structure itself should be an explicit inductive bias. Motivated by the two-part coding view of Kolmogorov complexity, we study a structure--residual principle in which a representation separates structure from realization detail. We instantiate this principle in two complementary ways. Boundary Bottleneck Concept (BBC) routes information from token segments through boundary carriers while preserving local autoregressive computation. Continuous Concept (CC) regularizes representation geometry and induces a spectral decomposition into low-frequency structure and high-frequency residual. We derive coding bounds for both views and show that multiplicity-corrected boundary attention becomes exact as the within-segment path energy vanishes. Empirically, BBC and CC exhibit strikingly different depth preferences: BBC works best in early layers, whereas CC is most effective in later layers. At 300M training tokens, combining BBC and CC improves NLL by 1.44\% on GPT-2 Medium. These gains persist with training runs up to 20B tokens. Representation diagnostics show that BBC increases the relative predictive importance of boundary states, while CC reduces spectral residual energy. Together, these results suggest that structure--residual biases can improve language-model pretraining beyond next-token prediction. Code: https://github.com/haihuang-ml/structure-residual

### Can an LLM dream? Testing the overfitted brain hypothesis on an agent with text memory
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21648) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21648) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21648) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21648)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Hoel (2021) proposed that dreams distort replayed experience so that the brain does not overfit to recent events. We test this hypothesis on an LLM agent (Claude Opus 5.5) that stores what it learns in a text memory and rewrites that memory each night from the day's conversation transcript. The agent goes through ten simulated days of conversation built from LongMemEval. We corrupt 5, 10, 20 or 40 percent of the transcript's turns before each nightly rewrite in one of two ways. Detail corruption changes one or two details in a turn to plausible wrong ones, or replaces the turn with one by the same speaker from an earlier day. Random-text corruption replaces half of the selected turns with a passage of Moby-Dick and inserts Moby-Dick words into the other half. A size limit on the memory, rising from 19,500 to 117,000 tokens over ten nights, set from human free-recall data, forces it to forget. We learned that corruption did not help the agent apply what it learned in earlier conversations to new tasks in any of 30 runs (ten conditions, three different conversation histories). Replacing details in 40 percent of turns lowered recall from 76 to 60 percent. Random text had no consistent effect on recall, with a paired difference of 0 $\pm$ 2 points at 40 percent, because the model recognized it as corrupted and left it out of memory. Replaced details were stored as fact even when another turn held the correct value, and the agent answered 40 percent of those questions correctly, compared with 75 percent without corruption. These results suggest that a pretrained model can separate corrupted text from real events, so a fair test needs a learner that cannot make this separation as easily, such as a model fine-tuned on the corrupted days or an agent whose days take place in an invented world.

### Inventa: Decentralized Self-Improving Agents for Autonomous Scientific Discovery

Large language model (LLM) agents can generate research ideas and improve scientific discovery through iterative experimentation. However, increasing the number of agents or iterations does not automatically translate to producing more diverse or feasible ideas. Agents may converge on similar candidates, propagate unsupported claims through shared memory components and repeatedly apply search strategies that no longer yield any improvements. We introduce Inventa, a framework for automated scientific discovery that employs autonomous research units and verified shared knowledge. Each unit creates a lineage of candidate ideas using a mutator agent. A local orchestrator agent periodically adapts the mutator's harness. When progress stalls, the orchestrator agent replaces its model or restarts the idea evolution process. Verifiers evaluate each candidate idea and record verified improvements, useful components and constraint violations in a shared knowledge, the only channel between units. We evaluate Inventa on five benchmarks spanning the research cycle, covering retrieval (ScholarCatalyst), ideation (IdeaBench), proposal writing (IdeaScientist), and scientific law discovery (EurekaBench, SciLaws-Bench). It leads all five, achieving 73.6% on EurekaBench versus 56.2% for CORAL. On IdeaBench, it discovers 26.0 novel ideas per paper, compared with 18.6 for ResearchAgent, with 82% fewer model calls.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22537) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22537) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22537) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22537)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

A world model is trusted to answer "what if": how its world responds to an action it has never seen. We run it in retail, where the action is a price and promotion plan, across 2,853 fitted models. First, forecast accuracy is a fair guide between kinds of model (rank correlation with error on new plans 0.32 to 0.66) and a poor guide between versions of one world model: where merchants plan into high-demand weeks the link reverses, down to -0.69. Second, training the world model to learn the effect of actions (JEPA pretraining, a latent consistency check, adaptation at the plan date) leaves its error on new plans unchanged. Third, in generated worlds the recorded regular price and three price rules improve it. Given the same rules, a log-linear model is best when its curve can match the truth; in a world whose curve lies outside the rules, the world model with the rules is ahead of the log-linear model with the rules in 36 of 40 worlds, and does as well without its recurrent cell. On real data (Breakfast at the Frat, The Complete Journey and M5) the rules lower the world model's error on pure price cuts deeper than any it saw (0.63 to 0.56 on the Frat; 0.45 to 0.44, within chance, on The Complete Journey), but not on lasting price moves. Fourth, a small share of random test plans helps every model a little. In a generated dosing world, a stress test in a second domain, the first finding repeats, and the built-up drug level lowers the world model's error (2.86 to 1.91) though it still predicts only 0.12 to 0.32 of the true gain. The test carries to any domain where a world model is asked about actions outside its history.

### A Full-Ledger Counterfactual Audit of Adaptive Reasoning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22778v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22778v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22778v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22778v1)

2026/11/17 13:44 GTM

Adaptive reasoning must decide whether to answer now or spend more compute. Predicting that the current answer is wrong does not establish whether further reasoning will improve it. We introduce the Full-Ledger Counterfactual Value Audit (FLCVA), which compares an immediate answer with four fixed-length continuations from the same frozen prefix under one answer procedure and accounts for the cost of every signal used by a policy. On GSM8K and MATH-500, a random 256-token continuation recovers a wrong answer in 26.6% of Qwen3-8B cases and 18.9% of DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B cases, while damaging a correct answer in 1.4% and 1.9%. Correctness labels from independently sampled continuations predict a separate continuation's outcome in both primary models. In offline replay, using these labels to allocate compute improves performance on DeepSeek; the Qwen3 gain remains inconclusive. Under full cost accounting, the tested lightweight, myopic controllers mainly recognize current errors and add little continuation-specific value beyond that shortcut. A Llama-8B replication and action/cost ablations examine the robustness of these findings. FLCVA separates measuring local continuation value, predicting it from observable signals, and using those predictions to allocate computation.

### Multistage Contextual Optimization for Vehicle Repositioning
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22330) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22330) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22330) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22330)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Ride-hailing platforms reposition idle vehicles to improve spatial supply-demand balances. This problem is prediction-driven and multistage: at each stage, the platform forecasts future demand, executes current decisions, and uses the resulting state to initialize the next stage. We formulate a multistage linear program (LP) with a macroscopic fundamental diagram (MFD) linking accumulation to trip completion. We then develop a multistage contextual optimization approach that uses decision performance to train the prediction model. The learning problem combines multiple coupled stages, context that depends on earlier decisions, and predictions that affect the objective, the constraint matrix, and the right-hand side of the LP. We derive the exact gradient, which has three parts: parameters change forecasts and hence decisions; earlier decisions change the current decision through the inherited state; and this state also changes forecasts as context. An equivalent adjoint uses reverse vector-Jacobian products, avoiding full solution and state-parameter Jacobians. Finally, forward propagation solves many stage LPs one after another, which makes training slow. We propose a parallel block evaluation algorithm that solves blocks of consecutive stages in parallel and repeatedly feeds each block output to the next block. We prove that the algorithm recovers the serial trajectory within a finite number of passes and that its gradient error remains bounded when it stops early. On New York City ride-hailing data, multistage contextual optimization yields 7.7% to 9.1% more business benefit than the same predictor trained by mean squared error, outperforms all compared baselines from five paradigms, and even outperforms realized demand fed to the same policy. With eight workers, parallel block evaluation shortens training by a factor of 1.86 without loss of decision performance.

Tool-using language-model agents must act on information from untrusted emails, webpages, and documents. This creates a central security challenge: malicious instructions and legitimate task directives can arrive through the same interaction channel. Existing defenses are largely hand-designed and static, making further improvements dependent on repeated manual redesign. We introduce Evolution Under Fire, a staged framework that couples safety-harness evolution with adaptive stressor evolution. Utility-constrained, Pareto-scheduled tree search evolves complete executable safety harnesses from execution evidence while keeping the task model, benchmark, tools, and evaluator fixed. Adaptive stressor evolution then discovers utility-preserving attacks that guide a subsequent stage of harness evolution. On AgentDyn with GPT-4o-mini, an evolved harness reduces attack success from 50.4% to 3.0%, improves clean and attacked utility over the initial harness, and offers a stronger security-utility trade-off than reproduced defenses. On Agent-SafetyBench with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, harness evolution reduces attack success from 82.6% to 62.0% while retaining helpfulness and runtime validity. Evolved harnesses also transfer without further evolution to Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, reducing attack success from 32.1% to 0.9% while improving attacked-task utility.

### The Only Edge Was Memorized in Training: LLM Trading Agents Around Earnings and Bad News
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22177) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22177) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22177) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22177)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Large language model (LLM) trading agents are tested on news, but news reaches stock prices within minutes, and a benchmark that replays headlines at their publication time finds no alpha in trading on them. Money can then remain in two places, before news whose date is known, such as earnings announcements, and after bad news, if the market's fall went too far. However, studies of LLMs and earnings read documents published with or after the announcement, and studies of reversal sort monthly portfolios, so neither tells whether an agent can trade these moments after costs. In this paper, we test LLM agents at both moments on the same look-ahead-free benchmark, before 598 earnings announcements and after 409 bad-news drops of large US stocks. Every input is cut at the decision close, staged agents are compared with single calls, simple rules and a logistic regression, and every decision is made with and without the stock's name. Across five models from four vendors, no agent beat the simple bets. Before earnings, the agents mostly bought and earned what buying before every announcement earned, and after bad news, a Gemini-based agent called almost every fall an overreaction while no agent picked better dips than buying them all. The only gains came from what the models had memorized in training. Given only the ticker and the dates, a GPT-based model called the direction of 59.2% of the announcements before its training cutoff and 53.3% after it. Agent builders should therefore compare an event agent with the simple bet and test it blind after the training cutoff.
