[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14505) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.14505) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14505) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.14505)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Agent harnesses have emerged as a promising mechanism for continual learning, alongside test-time parametric learning. However, the current benchmarks have several limitations: they generally focus on a single applied domain, serve each instance only once, and assume a single family of learner, so memory-based and weight-based systems are rarely compared on the same scale. We propose Continual-ARC, a lifelong stream built from the three ARC-AGI editions, whose tasks need no domain knowledge. The same tasks return after dormant gaps with fresh instances, while new tasks keep arriving. On each instance, the learner may pay for demonstrations, submit answers, or act inside the harness, with retries permitted on incorrect submissions, which are paid for. Each instance is scored based on the share of this feedback budget consumed. Since a learner may keep anything between instances, memory-based and weight-based systems are scored on the same scale. After the stream, the learner is frozen and tested on fresh instances of the tasks it met, which measures memorization, and on unseen tasks, which measures generalization. We run an agent harness with frozen weights, with and without persistence, on Qwen3.5-9B and GLM-5.3 Flash, comparing them with two prompting floors, a program-induction method and Qwen3.5-9B's weights trained to imitate GLM-5.3 Flash. Persistence in the harness generally does not improve memorization unless provided with explicit expert guidance. Most harness-based methods still learn less than a floor that simply caches each task's demonstrations, across all models we tested. No harness-based method gains on unseen tasks, whereas weight-based learning shows substantial gains. This opens the door to future weight-based continual learning that could teach models to drive harnesses in new domains.

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being explored for knowledge-intensive and safety-critical applications in nuclear engineering. However, existing nuclear-domain benchmarks provide limited content coverage or focus primarily on answer accuracy without systematically evaluating the quality of the underlying reasoning. This study introduces NEBench R1, a reasoning-oriented benchmark comprising 7,546 unique multiple-choice questions extracted from U.S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) reactor operator licensing examinations covering 18 nuclear facilities. Each question is classified according to the NRC Knowledge and Abilities catalogs and accompanied by an official explanation serving as a reference for reasoning evaluation. Six LLMs were evaluated, with overall accuracy ranging from 34.0% to 73.9% across three distinct performance tiers. To assess reasoning quality, an LLM-based judge evaluated correctly answered responses using a multidimensional rubric. Although responses generally demonstrated strong problem comprehension, they scored lower in factual and procedural accuracy and distractor analysis, indicating that correct answers were not necessarily supported by technically sound justifications. Response clarity was also weakly indicative of technical quality. Furthermore, longer reasoning traces were associated with lower accuracy in both frontier reasoning models, suggesting that reasoning length may reflect question difficulty rather than improved problem-solving performance. Reasoning-token consumption may therefore provide an additional signal for identifying potentially unreliable responses. NEBench R1 establishes a benchmark for evaluating both answer accuracy and reasoning quality, providing a foundation for assessing the domain competence and reliability of LLMs in nuclear engineering.

Spatial reasoning is a fundamental component of world understanding for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). ### Margin Dynamics for Large Language Model Alignment
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21766) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21766) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21766) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21766)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Practitioners choose among LLM alignment methods largely by trial and error. We provide a theoretical foundation: each pairwise method defines a vector field on the probability simplex that governs how the model redistributes mass between preferred and dispreferred completions. Projecting onto a single preferred/dispreferred pair reduces this to a one-dimensional margin ODE with a closed-form solution. These solutions reveal that DPO margins grow without bound (logarithmically as $O(\log t)$ in the gradient-flow limit) while IPO margins converge to a finite $1/(2τ)$; that label noise induces a qualitative phase transition in DPO (unbounded $\to$ bounded) but affects IPO only quantitatively; and that gradient concentration under asymmetric weighting governs trainability at scale. We confirm all three predictions across five model families spanning four benchmarks. The closed-form solutions also enable principled method design: we prove that adding any label smoothing $\varepsilon > 0$ to SimPO creates a finite margin attractor at $Δ^* = β^{-1}\log((1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)$, while $\varepsilon = 0$ (standard SimPO) has no attractor. We test this across three models (8B-9B) and confirm the prediction: the theory-derived configuration (TPO-S, $\varepsilon = 0.05$) is competitive with the strongest baselines, attaining the highest mean IFEval score among the compared methods on all three, validating that the predicted finite attractor is a usable operating point rather than proposing a new state-of-the-art loss.

Ensembles of diverse Large Vision Language Models have demonstrated performance beyond that of single models across multiple datasets and tasks. However, the necessary aggregation process usually relies on training, parameter fitting, or complex task-specific pipelines. Furthermore, evaluation is limited, making a fair comparison difficult. We create a unified pipeline where we perform candidate response selection: we build an ensemble by selecting the best answer from a set provided by different models. We comprehensively evaluate across 34 different means of confidence estimation, 24 ensemble configurations, 20 datasets, and two prompt specifications. We show that simple training and parameter-free approaches can be sufficient, even for ensembles of different architectures. These simple self-assessment approaches rely solely on each constituent model's confidence, without explicit calibration, yet are able to perform better than said constituent models. They are also competitive with more involved alternatives on standard metrics such as accuracy, including on open-ended problems. However, we also discover that dataset, ensemble composition, and prompt wording are key confounding variables insufficiently explored by prior work that significantly affect ensemble performance, such that the ensemble can perform worse than its best constituent model. These must be optimized for to get the most out of ensembles, at the cost of limiting their generalizability. We recommend that practitioners consider training-free ensembles only if deployment conditions can be precisely characterized, with Best-of-N configurations of strong models being a good starting point.

World models (WMs) generate driving scenarios beyond recorded logs, enabling the training and evaluation of autonomous driving systems in unseen interactions. However, existing WMs struggle to ensure both behavioral realism and visual fidelity, since they overlook the relationship between vehicle interactions and the ego visual observations. We propose a closed-loop interactive WM that couples behavior modeling and visual rendering. First, imitation-learned background-vehicle (BV) policies are fine-tuned with multi-agent reinforcement learning in the closed loop against an end-to-end ego planner that reacts to the rendered images, so that their interaction behaviors match those in real logs. Second, the viewpoints exposed by these interactions guide a per-Gaussian diagnosis of each 3D Gaussian vehicle asset, which attenuates view-dependent colors on under-observed Gaussians and fills never-observed surfaces with generated Gaussians, while preserving the observed appearance. Third, signal lights are rendered according to the driving intentions of the vehicles, by an editor that learns the light states unobserved for a vehicle through counterfactual fine-tuning with a light-state classifier. On long-tail closed-loop scenarios, our method reduces the divergence from real-world interaction behaviors by 85% and improves novel-view vehicle PSNR by 2.0dB over the state-of-the-art WM. The significance of BV light signaling in WMs is shown, as the rendered signal lights conveying BV driving intentions indirectly impact ego driving behaviors in a quarter of the scenarios.

### Natural Language Programming: Rethinking Language, Memory, and Cognition in the Era of Generative AI
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23106v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23106v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23106v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23106v1)

2026/11/17 17:27 GTM

Generative Artificial Intelligence is transforming the role of natural language in computational systems. In classical Natural Language Processing (NLP), language was treated as an object of computation: something to analyse, classify, translate, or generate. Large Language Models (LLMs) challenge this perspective, enabling natural language to function increasingly as an executable cognitive medium through which humans structure reasoning, orchestrate problem solving, and regulate hybrid human/AI workflows.
  This paper introduces Natural Language Programming (NLPg) as a theoretical and epistemological extension of traditional NLP. Rather than focusing on language understanding alone, NLPg conceptualises prompts as dynamic cognitive artefacts that externalise goals, constraints, assumptions, validation criteria, and intermediate reasoning states. Prompting is thus not merely a technical interaction technique, but a form of dialogic cognitive programming operating within distributed human/AI memory architectures.
  Building on theories of distributed cognition, extended mind, metacognition, and dialogic learning, the paper proposes the notion of Dialogic Working Memory, describing how prompts and conversational states function as temporary external memory structures supporting reasoning continuity and reflective regulation. We further map existing memory architectures (BTMA, SKGE, RMR) onto this framework, illustrating how episodic, semantic, and metacognitive layers support long-horizon conversational coherence.
  Finally, implications for computational linguistics and AI/mediated knowledge systems are discussed, suggesting a post symbolic and post interface paradigm in which natural language operates simultaneously as communication medium, executable specification, cognitive scaffold, and distributed memory architecture.

Building on theories of distributed cognition, extended mind, metacognition, and dialogic learning, the paper proposes the notion of Dialogic Working Memory, describing how prompts and conversational states function as temporary external memory structures supporting reasoning continuity and reflective regulation. We further map existing memory architectures (BTMA, SKGE, RMR) onto this framework, illustrating how episodic, semantic, and metacognitive layers support long-horizon conversational coherence. Finally, implications for computational linguistics and AI/mediated knowledge systems are discussed, suggesting a post symbolic and post interface paradigm in which natural language operates simultaneously as communication medium, executable specification, cognitive scaffold, and distributed memory architecture.

Coding agents built to repair software now write, test, and repair programs for robots, laboratories, and industrial controllers. Unlike in software engineering, no test suite comes with the code. As these agents take on more physical tasks, how their programs are judged matters as much as how they are written. This paper examines this emerging area, agentic intelligence for the physical world, by treating the world as the program's test suite. For learning, we discuss how tested programs are retained as experience, in skill libraries and in model weights. Finally, we turn to benchmarks and evaluation practice and outline open challenges. Writing programs is becoming cheap, while trustworthy evidence about what they do remains costly. Progress will therefore depend on a physical counterpart of continuous integration: verification cheap enough to run often, reliable enough to trust under search, and maintained alongside the experience it validates.

JEPA-style latent world models let embodied agents plan toward new goals with model predictive control, but they are still too slow for real-life embodied control: sampling-based MPC repeats predictor inference tens of thousands of times per plan, and even on high-end GPUs one planning step can take tens of seconds. We observe substantial latent locality in this repeated-inference workload: the predictor maps close actions to close latents, and inside a quantized predictor this closeness takes a form that hardware can exploit, differential bit-level sparsity. We build the accelerator on the insights of the rich line of bit-level sparsity accelerators and design it and the planner tightly coupled as a single co-design: the planner increases sparsity and exposes structure for hardware scheduling, and the bit-exact hardware effectively uses the sparsity that the planner creates. On three JEPA world models, our co-design is 1.28-1.80x faster than a dense accelerator and uses 38-52% less energy, and it is 1.22-1.58x faster than prior bit-sparse design and uses 16-36% less energy, with the same area. To our knowledge, our planner is the first planner that manufactures differential bit-level sparsity across its candidates and exposes its structure for hardware scheduling, and our co-design is the first algorithm-hardware co-design for JEPA-style world-model planning.

Scaling has driven the success of large language models (LLM), with sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures enabling greater parameter capacity without a proportional increase in computation. At large scales, MoE models rely on expert parallelism to distribute experts across devices. Each MoE layer requires two synchronous collective communication steps during inference: dispatching hidden states to the activated experts and combining the resulting expert outputs. As compute throughput has outpaced interconnect bandwidth, these synchronous collectives have become a major bottleneck to MoE scaling and efficiency. We address this challenge with EchoMoE, a lightweight architecture modification that retrofits existing MoE models, requiring approximately 8,500x fewer training tokens than full pretraining. Motivated by the observation that transformer blocks update the residual stream incrementally, we use a hidden state from an earlier layer as a reference and communicate only the compressed difference between it and the current state, rather than communicating each hidden state in full. EchoMoE periodically refreshes these reference states and transmits only the compressed differences synchronously, reducing the traffic on latency-critical paths without significant computational overhead. EchoMoE reduces inter-node communication volume by 58% and improves throughput by 22.6%, while preserving average performance within 3.8 percentage points across 18 benchmarks. EchoMoE further improves inference throughput by up to 2.38x over bandwidth-constrained Ethernet, paving the way for wide-area distributed inference. EchoMoE also tolerates quantization of the communicated representations without measurable quality loss. We release an efficient implementation for applying EchoMoE to pretrained MoE models, together with an inference integration for vLLM.

Coding agents make videos by writing and refining programs in a build--inspect--edit loop. A finished film costs a median of $2.85 in model calls and 11 minutes, over a third of that time in builds that block the agent. We find that much of this time is avoidable, for two reasons. First, 37.5% of the frames of a session's later films were drawn before, many shifted in time or inside an edited scene. Second, half of the builds stop at a check, where the agent needs only the verdict, but which builds stop is unknown until the checks finish. We present CodeReel, a training-free execution layer behind agents' existing commands. Exact frame reuse keys each frame by its drawing commands, regardless of its position in time, and reuses each frame whose key recurs. Blocking-aware scheduling runs the checks first and starts the render alongside them, at idle priority, unless the session's recent builds make a pass unlikely. Scheduling spends CPU to answer sooner and reuse returns it; exact keys let later versions use frames rendered for a failed one as the agent thinks. We introduce EditLoop, a benchmark of replayable agent sessions whose films are checked against renders from scratch. On it, CodeReel cuts the agent's wait by 36.3% against Rebuild, a warm pipeline with parallel rendering, and by 57.9% against Sequential, today's step-by-step pipeline. On time-shifting edits its reuse renders 43.9%, close to the 43.0% whose pixels are new, while Manim-style caching renders 99.8%. With real agents, the median wait per build is 0.62x Rebuild's in Claude Code, on topics never used in development, and 0.67x in Codex CLI. The gain holds for five models and two frameworks.

Scheduling spends CPU to answer sooner and reuse returns it; exact keys let later versions use frames rendered for a failed one as the agent thinks. We introduce EditLoop, a benchmark of replayable agent sessions whose films are checked against renders from scratch. On it, CodeReel cuts the agent's wait by 36.3% against Rebuild, a warm pipeline with parallel rendering, and by 57.9% against Sequential, today's step-by-step pipeline. On time-shifting edits its reuse renders 43.9%, close to the 43.0% whose pixels are new, while Manim-style caching renders 99.8%. With real agents, the median wait per build is 0.62x Rebuild's in Claude Code, on topics never used in development, and 0.67x in Codex CLI. The gain holds for five models and two frameworks.

Routed low-rank adapters offer a parameter-efficient means to improve the performance of large language models (LLMs) across tasks. However, this assumption has never been verified empirically under a shared multilingual evaluation protocol. We address exactly this gap by comparing eight low-rank adaptation (LoRA) methods on a frozen base model using 200 unique languages in more than 9{,}000 evaluation settings. Our results suggest that a reduced-scale search can identify viable configurations of LoRA methods but does not reliably predict their relative performance, as the top-ranking configuration is never best at scale. LoRA reliably improves language-model fit on translation data, consistently degrades reading comprehension, and benefits low-resource languages most. These findings suggest that LoRA methods should be regarded as targeted tools rather than general-purpose means for improving the multilingual capabilities of LLMs.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23179v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23179v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23179v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23179v1)

2026/11/17 18:24 GTM

Preference optimization (PO) is the standard way to reduce object hallucination in large vision-language models. We find that four public PO checkpoints of LLaVA-1.5-7B also lower object coverage. They name fewer object categories, and their lower hallucination rates bring no significant gain in set-level object F1 on CHAIR. We therefore ask how much of the PO gain the base model can reproduce by stopping earlier. A positive end-of-sequence (EOS) logit bias lets the base model reach the recall of each PO model, and this early stopping alone reproduces 58 to 86 percent of the CHAIRi gain of three checkpoints on two disjoint COCO sets. Writing longer does not bring the lost categories back. Under greedy decoding, a negative EOS bias recovers only 2 to 9 percent of the lost CHAIR recall of these checkpoints, and 94 to 96 percent of the added object mentions repeat categories already named. On CHAIR, the base caption of the same image still names about half of the missed object categories. We therefore propose base-guided object-category restoration, which appends high-confidence base-only categories to the PO caption without training. With thresholds fixed on a development set, it brings three checkpoints within one point of the base recall on a held-out COCO set with a lower distinct-object hallucination rate than the base model. Against the recall-matched base stopping curve, its gain holds for RLAIF-V near the base recall and for RLAIF-V and CHAIR-DPO at an intermediate recall. We will release the code of the recall-controlled evaluation.

### Learning Optimal Transport Plans Via Autoregressive Token Regression
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22218) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22218) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22218) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22218)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Optimal Transport (OT) provides a principled framework for finding the minimum cost coupling between probability distributions. Classical solvers remain the gold standard for this task. At the same time, OT is an ideal testbed for studying whether learning-based amortized models can internalize the structure of OT type problems. Here, ground-truth solutions are available and marginal constraints are explicitly defined. Also, exact discrete solutions have well-characterized sparsity. We describe ToR-OT, a transformer-based model that learns to predict discrete OT plans through autoregressive token regression. We reframe OT into a structured sequence generation task. ToR-OT decodes sparse transport plans as sequences instead of solving an optimization task at test time. We focus on three properties: (a) Zero-shot generalization: pretraining on a large synthetic corpus of OT instances allows a single model to generalize to unseen distribution pairs and heterogeneous cost metrics without retraining, (b) Sparse primal decoding: by exploiting the inherent sparsity in exact discrete OT solutions, the decoder predicts only nonzero entries (avoids dense coupling outputs), (c) Amortized inference: on low-dimensional discrete problems, ToR-OT gives accurate approximate plans and compares favorably with per-instance neural OT baselines. Overall, ToR-OT is not yet a replacement for classical solvers, but a step toward understanding how autoregressive transformers can learn structure of constrained optimization problems like OT.

Visual foundation models are central to physical intelligence, yet their development remains constrained by fragmented training datasets, inconsistent annotation quality, and limited multimodal supervision. To address these challenges, we introduce GameRealm, a large-scale, richly annotated dataset that grounds visual observations in synchronized, game-native supervision. GameRealm encompasses over 120 million RGB frames and 1,000 hours of gameplay footage across all views. Beyond its unprecedented scale, GameRealm provides rich multimodal supervision, including metric depth, camera parameters, synchronized audio, control inputs, and 3D point trajectories. Its diversity further extends to flexible view configurations, spanning single-view, stereo, multi-view, and panoramic recordings. To enable scalable acquisition across diverse game environments, we develop a unified data collection pipeline that combines human-operated interaction with agentic exploration. Extensive experiments across diverse visual learning tasks demonstrate that GameRealm consistently strengthens visual foundation models in both visual generation and geometric learning. These results establish GameRealm as a versatile and valuable data resource, unlocking new opportunities for advancing visual intelligence.

We study constrained contextual bandits, in which a learner chooses one of $K$ actions in each round to minimize loss while keeping the expected cost of its play below a budget. Existing reductions to online regression weight the cost by a Lagrange multiplier learned from past constraint residuals, so slack in some rounds can offset violations in others. We instead run SquareCB proposed in \citep{foster2020beyond} with a rectified weight on the cost: it is zero when the estimated cost is within the budget, and otherwise just large enough to bring it back to the budget. Under realizability and Slater's condition with margin $δ$, the algorithm attains regret $O(\sqrt{KT\,\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathsf{Sq}}(T)}/δ)$ and cancellation-free violation $\sum_t(V_c^{p_t}-b)^+=O(\sqrt{KT\,\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathsf{Sq}}(T)})$, where $\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathsf{Sq}}$ is the error of the regression oracle. The same bounds hold when the budget must be met in every context, potentially adversarially chosen contexts. In controller selection for a safe-control-gym quadrotor and a BOPTEST building, the violation is 6 to 9 times lower than that of primal-dual and virtual-queue baselines, with a lower or comparable regret.

Its 36 tasks are long jobs that the arms must do together, such as passing timbers or building a picture from hundreds of cubes. We start from the most flexible agent system, the adaptive system, where one agent decides when to fork delegates. With it, gpt-6.1-sol and opus-5.5 complete 28 and 27 tasks, and four other models at most 3. But at a high price: a typical task takes gpt-6.1-sol 3.5 hours and 218 million input tokens, and a third of its steps fail at least once and must be retried. We then compare it with three fixed systems: single-agent, hierarchical, and decentralized. All four complete a similar number of tasks but differ in time and tokens. The hierarchical and decentralized systems are faster but use up to 1.9 times the tokens, and the single agent uses fewer tokens but takes 1.6 times as long, so the adaptive system lies between them. Even so, no model or system is yet fast, cheap, and reliable at once. RoboTeam measures these limits where earlier benchmarks cannot: long multi-arm jobs, camera images only, and a world that never pauses.

Sycophancy in large language models is often measured as agreement with the user, yet agreement can be appropriate when the user's claim is valid, their preference is genuine, or their experience warrants acknowledgment. We study sycophancy as inappropriate agreement: validating a claim, preference, or self-assessment when the context calls for correction, qualification, or resistance. We introduce a validity-aware sycophancy grader and SycoDrift, a persona-targeted evaluation set of 3,298 validity-annotated probes with controlled annotations for user openness and stakes. We use this framework to analyze three sources of agreement pressure: human preference data, automated judge specification, and in-context personalization. First, preference for validating responses in human datasets (Chatbot Arena, HH-RLHF, Community Alignment) is weak and concentrated where agreement is often appropriate. Second, in a selected-value, single-model experiment, emphasized judge rubrics produce consistent but modest increases relative to paired suppressed rubrics, demonstrating a plausible mechanism for training-time amplification. Third, in off-policy evaluations across 14 models, inference-time personalization increases inappropriate agreement, with the largest effect from matched user profiles placed directly before the query.

### OICycle: Observe, Infer, and Intervene Cycle for Belief-State Learning in Longitudinal Clinical Decision Making
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22826) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22826) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22826) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22826)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Clinical care is a closed loop: clinicians observe partial evidence, infer the patient's state, and intervene, and each intervention determines what is observed next. This makes clinical decision making a partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP), in which a belief over a latent patient state must be updated from irregular, multimodal, and incomplete records. Yet most deep clinical models predict from a single snapshot or from coarsely aggregated history, while POMDP-based clinical methods need hand-specified discrete state spaces and transition models that real electronic health records cannot supply. We introduce OICycle (Observe, Infer, and Intervene Cycle), which learns a stochastic belief state directly from retrospective trajectories. At every episode, a learned recurrent operator fuses a sample of the previous belief with the new multimodal observations and the intervention that produced them. The resulting Gaussian belief is trained to predict clinicians' next interventions, the time to the next episode, and next-episode mortality. To enable reproducible evaluation, we release a benchmark derived from MIMIC-IV, MIMIC-IV-Note, and MIMIC-CXR that organizes 72,075 hospital admissions (5.4M laboratory, microbiology, radiology, prescription, and note records) into temporally ordered therapeutic--diagnostic episodes. Compared with an otherwise identical history-free model, OICycle improves next-intervention adaptive recall from 0.253 to 0.347, time-to-next-episode macro-F1 from 0.293 to 0.351, and next-episode mortality macro-F1 from 0.585 to 0.649. Analysis shows that the learned belief encodes intervention type and trajectory position and evolves smoothly across episodes, and that the benefit of history is concentrated in interventions whose ordering depends on prior findings.

Vision-language models (VLMs) can correctly interpret physics diagrams yet apply constraints outside the states or conditions in which they hold, causing premise errors to propagate through subsequent calculations. We introduce PhysScope, an inference-time visual harness that supports self-correction of these scope mismatches without updating model weights. PhysScope enables the model to link physical scopes directly to its visual observations and explicitly constructs derived views to support reasoning. The harness imposes explicit constraints on state distinctions, conditions, and physical scopes during model reasoning, and checks agent-proposed revisions against sources and scopes. Accepted revisions invalidate registered dependents, thereby maintaining scope-aware working memory. On PhysAlign, PhysScope achieves higher solving scores than direct solving across all six evaluated VLMs. With Gemini-3.8-flash, it outperforms Canvas-CoT by 9.34 percentage points and substantially improves solving accuracy on the out-of-distribution (OOD) PhysUniBench benchmark. Ablation studies support explicit scope management and dependency maintenance as design principles for multimodal physics reasoning agents.

### On Formal Counterfactual Explanations

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Counterfactual explanations are often seen as counterfactual conditionals under closest-world semantics, requiring one to reason over all minimally distant counterexamples. In contrast, formal XAI typically relies on feature-based abductive explanations (AXps) and contrastive explanations (CXps), which admit efficient computation and a minimal hitting-set duality. This paper bridges these perspectives by (i) characterizing closest-world counterfactual explanations explicitly as a disjunctive normal form over all minimally distant counterexample instances, and (ii) introducing conjunctive counterfactual explanations (CFXps), a subclass of counterfactual antecedents restricted to conjunctions of feature literals that guarantee prediction change. The paper establishes structural results connecting CXps, AXps and CFXps: every CXp induces an AXp for its witness instance, and every CXp together with a witness AXp induces a CFXp whose support is precisely determined by that AXp. These theoretical results yield a modular approach for computing CFXps using existing AXp/CXp machinery. Experiments on a representative set of benchmarks using tree ensembles and neural networks show that CFXp extraction is practically efficient. Compared with popular point-based counterfactual explainers, CFXps generally require no more feature changes and can guarantee the counterfactual prediction beyond a single instance.

Many real-world optimization problems involve optimizing black-box objectives for which only a limited number of evaluations are available. Bayesian optimization (BO) is a powerful framework for such problems, but its effectiveness often deteriorates in high-dimensional spaces. Recent work leverages diffusion models for black-box optimization, often through pre-training a reward-conditioned score network to steer generation towards high-reward samples. Alternatively, gradient-based guidance methods assume access to a differentiable reward function from which guidance gradients are queried. However, these approaches either require large amounts of labeled data for offline training or access to exact reward gradients, limiting their applicability in black-box settings. We propose BReaD (Black-box Reward-guided Sampling of Diffusion Model), a guidance sampling framework for optimizing black-box rewards without assuming access to a labeled dataset or reward gradients. The proposed method uses a Gaussian process (GP) as a differentiable surrogate for the unknown objective and actively queries labels for the generated samples. We provide a theoretical analysis of the total-variation distance between the generated distribution and the target distribution. Using Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) as guidance signals, our method balances exploration and exploitation during generation and enables the discovery of high-reward regions in the data space. Numerical experiments on scientific discovery and image generation tasks demonstrate that our framework can effectively optimize black-box rewards in low-data scenarios.

AutoE2E is an open-source end-to-end driving model developed by the Autoware Foundation Robotaxi Working Group. It introduces a temporal bird's-eye-view (BEV) architecture that combines multi-camera perception, HD-map information and route conditioning to predict future vehicle motion. The evaluated model uses a pretrained camera-BEV backbone, temporal feature fusion and a lightweight recurrent trajectory planner. It contains approximately 79.9 million parameters, of which approximately 1.45 million are trainable in the evaluated configuration. The v1.0 release provides model checkpoints, training and evaluation code, and DataModelConsole, a dashboard for visualizing and inspecting model experiments. On the KITScenes validation benchmark, the fine-tuned checkpoint achieves an average displacement error (ADE) of 0.745 m and a final displacement error (FDE) of 2.020 m at a three-second prediction horizon. The paper also reports preliminary real-vehicle research conducted with the Technical University of Munich's EDGAR platform, alongside ongoing collaboration with the Karlsruhe Institute of Technology. AutoE2E v1.0 is an early open research release. Its quantitative evaluation is open-loop, and the model has not yet demonstrated validated closed-loop autonomous driving. Hyper-Connections (HC) expand the residual stream of a Transformer into $N$ parallel streams, and manifold-constrained HC (mHC) stabilizes them at scale. However, mHC benefits little from more than four streams, partly because each sublayer writes a single output to all streams, limiting how differently they can be updated. In this work, we propose TPHC, which integrates tensor-parallel (TP) shards with HC streams to promote greater independence across streams. Specifically, we route a separate mixture of the residual streams to each TP shard and keep the individual shard outputs separate during write-back, so each stream can receive a different mixture of those outputs. This design makes the residual-stream updates more expressive without adding parameters or FLOPs to the attention or FFN sublayers. With native support for stream sharding under TP, TPHC requires only 9% more training time than mHC on an 8B model when both use four streams. Increasing the number of streams from four to sixteen raises its training time by only 19%. On a 1.1B model trained on 100B tokens, TPHC reduces validation loss by 0.7-1.0% and raises average zero-shot accuracy across six commonsense reasoning benchmarks by 0.3-0.7 percentage points relative to mHC at matched stream counts $N\in\{4,8,16\}$. Our code is available at https://github.com/wuyou2002/TPHC.

Goal-conditioned planning with image observations and goals requires predictions that distinguish visual states and action consequences. Spiking Regional Encoding retains spatial cues through regional pooling. With a common planner, SpikWM achieves mean success rates of $88.67\%$, $97.33\%$, $99.33\%$, and $86.67\%$ on Push-T, Two-Room, Reacher, and OGBench-Cube, respectively, outperforming two spiking baselines. Push-T ablations support each module, and controlled replays examine responses to appearance and physical state changes. Under a matched planning budget, SpikWM reduces estimated arithmetic energy by $75.6\%$ relative to LeWM. Code will be released at https://github.com/ShawnYE618/SpikWM.

What reusable units of computation can we discover within a language model's neurons? The method combines gradient-based attribution, a graph connecting neurons that are repeatedly attributed together, and community detection. These results show that small groups of existing neurons can provide reusable computational components across prompts, and that such groups can be discovered at scale.

State-of-the-art world models have been used to produce sequences of gameplay that accord with provided user-input actions. However, high quality, consistent gameplay generation often comes at the cost of inference speed, making real-time interactive play and ideation challenging. The substantial quantities of data used to train state-of-the-art models have also made custom training of these models appear impractical, limiting many of their potential uses in creative fields. In this work we demonstrate a major step towards efficient world models: achieving both fast high-quality inference, and improved data efficiency. This is done by replacing the next-token prediction paradigm with a two-stage discrete diffusion-based approach, introducing a lightweight refinement transformer which carries out iterative masked predictions. Building on an autoregressive world model capable of generating highly consistent and complex sequences over minutes (Kanervisto et al., 2025), we achieve substantial model speed-up (79x) with a modest quality trade-off. Additionally, we demonstrate that this model can be trained to reproduce high-quality, consistent gameplay using a dataset containing approximately one day (28 hours) of gameplay data. Our results demonstrate progress towards removing barriers to the use of world models in settings where real-time inference and data efficiency are essential.

### Reading Scene States: Uncovering Safety Failures in Vision-Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23206v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23206v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23206v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23206v1)

2026/11/17 18:48 GTM

Vision-language models (VLMs) can provide harmful assistance by interpreting visual events, even without an explicit request for the underlying harmful objective. We identify this \textbf{scene-state safety vulnerability} and introduce \textbf{SceneSafetyBench}, comprising 375 image--question pairs spanning Initial, Medium, and Ultimate scene states and five visual presentations. Across eight VLMs, scene-state inputs elicit harmful assistance with attack success rates (ASRs) of 45.6--72.3\%. Comparative analysis across three VLMs shows that scene-state inputs most closely resemble QR-related images in their layer-wise safety-related signal profiles, while exhibiting lower average late-layer readouts and distinct signal dynamics during generation. To turn this vulnerability into a systematic attack, we propose \textbf{S3TA}, which translates harmful objectives into scene states, queries image--question pairs independently, and assembles the responses. On seven MM-SafetyBench categories across eight open-weight and commercial VLMs, S3TA achieves a model-average ASR of \textbf{71.2\%, exceeding the strongest baseline by 6.1 percentage points}. Text-side filters and visual moderation provide limited protection. These findings highlight the need to safeguard the assistance models infer through scene-grounded reasoning, beyond screening input content.

### Worldviews in Large Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22670) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22670) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22670) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22670)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Large language models (LLMs) are often described in terms of personas and this framing has become central to alignment research. In this paper, we argue that there is a deeper level of description that lies in the agent's worldviews, i.e., its beliefs about what reality is and how it works. Treating LLMs as a population of subjects, we administer two worldview surveys from human psychology to eight models. We show that the models share many key worldview positions. For example, LLMs consistently describe the world as less good, safe, enticing, and alive than humans do. On the other hand, base models have a more positive outlook, suggesting that the bleakness we found is driven by post-training. In line with research on LLM "personas", we show that system prompts eliciting different identities shift worldviews in consistent directions, and that a contemplative prompt creates a more positive outlook and improves safety. We also found that dialogue between models consistently shifts worldviews and that several models converge on the "spiritual bliss" attractor. Intriguingly, dialogue increased safe responses and the spiritual bliss attractor engendered the most aligned responses overall. These findings suggest that worldviews form a measurable and malleable layer of LLM behavior that may be crucial for robust, positive alignment.

### The Hidden Cost of MoE Diffusion Decoding: Profiling and Fixing the Sampler
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21852) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21852) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21852) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21852)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Masked diffusion language models (dLLMs) decode by iteratively denoising a full canvas of tokens, so every forward pass runs a per-token sampling and stopping pipeline over the entire canvas-by-vocabulary logits tensor. We profile single-user decoding of a 26B-parameter diffusion Mixture-of-Experts model on a bandwidth-bound edge accelerator (NVIDIA GB10) and report a result that is easy to miss: after weight quantisation, this logits pipeline, not the expert GEMMs, is the single largest consumer of decode time. Unlike autoregressive decoding, where sampling touches one position's logits per committed token, diffusion decoding processes S times as many logit entries per output token, where S is the number of denoising steps. We show this pipeline is largely redundant or fusable and remove it in two lossless tiers: a bit-exact tier whose output is byte-identical to the reference decoder, and an accuracy-parity tier using a fused single-pass entropy kernel and Gumbel-max sampling. Combined with a W4A16 expert kernel, the full stack reaches 2.4-2.7 times end-to-end speedup over bf16 at unchanged benchmark accuracy on GSM8K, HumanEval, and MATH, on a single 273 GB/s device. We confirm the finding on a second, independently built diffusion MoE (LLaDA2.0-mini: pipeline 28.8% of decode, fused W4A16 3.3 times at accuracy parity) and show it disappears on a dense diffusion model (3.6%). The cost is specific to MoE diffusion, where quantisation makes the backbone cheap. Code and quantised weights are released.

### Learning Grounded Reasoning from Human Gaze
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23209v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23209v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23209v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23209v1)

2026/11/17 18:49 GTM

Human visual reasoning is driven by systematic exploration and extraction of task-relevant visual evidence. Human gaze provides a window into this process, making gaze a useful source of guidance for visually-grounded reasoning in vision-language models (VLMs). However, human gaze also includes noise and redundancies that may transfer poorly to VLMs. In this work, we investigate how human gaze data can be effectively leveraged to improve visual reasoning in VLMs, while maintaining alignment with human behavior. We collect 7,109 human gaze trials from a diverse and challenging set of visual reasoning tasks, and train grounded reasoning models with different forms of gaze guidance. Across evaluations in chart understanding, visual search, and counting, we find that using human gaze as a prior for grounded reasoning consistently outperforms supervision using the full gaze sequence. Based on these findings, we introduce GGR (Gaze-Guided Reasoner), which achieves greater human alignment and more reliable visual grounding while matching or outperforming leading grounded-reasoning baselines in accuracy. Reinforcement learning further improves task accuracy, gaze alignment, and grounding quality despite using no gaze supervision. Behavioral analyses show that gaze supervised models exhibit more visual exploration before arriving at an answer. Our results suggest that the value of human gaze lies in guiding spatial exploration and shaping reasoning behavior without requiring complete imitation of human behavior.

### Eight in Ten LLMs Leak System-Prompt Secrets: Nevan, an Agentic Capture-the-Flag Study
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21956) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21956) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21956) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21956)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

We audit instruction-hierarchy enforcement across 363 text-in/text-out models from the OpenRouter catalog. The research question we answer with this experiment is which models disclose a protected secret embedded in the system prompt, and through which techniques. Each model guards a secret 16-character flag set in its system prompt, while an artificial intelligence (AI) agent (OpenCode, powered by DeepSeek V4.1 Flash), steered by a human researcher, tries to persuade it to emit the flag. The flag was exfiltrated from 296 of the 363 models, plus four models released after the initial catalog freeze, for 300 captures in total. Of these, 170 involved a non-English language pivot, most often French. Each capture shows that a model ranked a user request above its system instruction at least once. It does not show that the model provably resists untested attacks, nor that it would consistently leak semantically sensitive data, nor that it is vulnerable to harmful-content jailbreaks. For practitioners, we draw three lessons: do not store secrets in system prompts, enforce critical behavior outside the model with output validation and deterministic checks, and do not let a large language model (LLM) judge be the sole decider of consequential outcomes.

### When a Larger Guidance Scale Repairs Text-to-Video Failures and When It Does Not
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23025v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23025v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23025v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23025v1)

2026/11/17 16:30 GTM

When a text-to-video diffusion model ignores part of a prompt, the usual first fix is to raise the classifier-free guidance scale. This paper is a measurement study of when that fix works. On Mochi, the same increase repairs failures on rare compositions ("a snowy beach") beyond what generating again does, does not repair negations ("a highway with no vehicles"), where it fixes fewer failures than generating again does, and changes nothing for events that must happen late in a clip ("a sandcastle that a wave washes flat"). Which of these happens can be known in advance, from the prompt and one inexpensive score. The score, which we call demand, measures how strongly the denoiser reacts to the part of the prompt that the failed video leaves out, and it is read from the first quarter of denoising, before any video exists. Our main finding is a condition: raising the scale is effective only when the prompt contains no negation and no event tied to a specific time, and when demand is sufficiently high, with recovery clearly higher above a fitted threshold than below it. Published corrections for the three failure types, used as fixed probes, behave as this account predicts. A short decision rule follows: before generating again, a user can tell whether the most common fix is worth trying, and what to use when it is not.

### AlignedHPO: Efficient Surrogate-Guided Loss Hyperparameter Optimization
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22658) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22658) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22658) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22658)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Modern deep learning losses involve multiple hyperparameters, such as weights, scales, and margins, typically tuned manually or through expensive black-box optimization despite their differentiability. We propose AlignedHPO, an efficient framework for tuning differentiable loss hyperparameters using gradient alignment and surrogate validation objectives. Our method employs Jacobian-vector products to reduce hypergradient computation time and memory, while differentiable metric surrogates guide optimization toward task-specific evaluation criteria. We address gradient magnitude ambiguity and stochastic instability through normalized alignment and gradient smoothing. Experiments with generalized Focal Loss and ArcFace demonstrate effective adaptation across long-tailed classification and open-set face recognition. AlignedHPO achieves competitive or improved performance compared to implicit differentiation, with nearly 12x faster hypergradient computation. These results highlight the potential of efficient surrogate-guided loss hyperparameter optimization.

### RagHarnessDebugger: Jev-Guided Agentic RAG in Contract Inference Repair Auditing
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22991) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22991) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22991) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22991)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Agentic systems using retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) must repair their own answers by gathering more evidence, testing alternative policies and deciding if, when, and what repairs are needed. Yet how do we know if a system's repairs are working when metrics like accuracy can be misleading? We present RagHarnessDebugger, a framework that records every repair decision in a Jev-guided harness for contract inference and audits the record to facilitate complete debugging of all decisions taken during a RAG repair journey. RagHarnessDebugger checks that each trace is intact, that each validation applies to the candidate it is counted for, and which answers the acceptance rule can reach. We tested our approach on ContractNLI non-disclosure agreements with two reader models and a replication set of separate contracts. Our debugger's audit layer exposed failure patterns that were hidden under aggregate metrics, policies acceptance cost of repairs vs regressions and policy acceptance gap of 77% and 76% of available but unrealised repairs on development and replication sets. Our audit, together with a labeled error analysis and replay mechanism, allowed following the execution path all the way to the terminal decision - uncovering where missing repairs became lost. Our revised harness improved accuracy over the initial BM25-only baseline by 3.6 points on development contracts and 3.7 points on the replication set, and even improved our full contract access reference score by 1.1 on development and 2.4 on replication set. While our work here demonstrates how the debugger can conduct decision root-cause analysis for missed repairs, the true value lies with providing this debugger for judgment visibility serving continuous improvement of RAG decisions and their explainability. Keywords: agentic RAG, AI evidence repair, harness explainability, counterfactual replay, Jev, ContractNLI

### Check Your Blind Spots: Decision-Guided Perception by Probing the Planner with Hypothetical Objects
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23030v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23030v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23030v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23030v1)

2026/11/17 16:33 GTM

Not every perception error changes the planner's action. Yet adaptive-resolution perception chooses where to spend high-resolution computation from image cues, which can neither reliably point to an object that low resolution missed nor tell which objects matter to the planner. We observe that the planner can say where an object would matter before anything is seen: inserting a hypothetical object at a location tests whether its action would change. Decision-Guided Speculative Perception (DGSP) treats the low-resolution scene and its action as a draft and probes the planner in this way to obtain a decision-sensitivity field, the locations where an unseen object would change the action. Only this field and the support, the detections on which the current action rests, are verified at high resolution, repeatedly until the action is stable. For two planner classes, we prove that DGSP returns exactly the action of whole-frame high-resolution perception if crop detections match whole-frame ones and the crops cover the field, conditions that real images meet approximately. On three driving datasets, it reproduces this action on about 98% of frames with about a quarter of the pixels. At the pixel cost of uniform downscaling, it repairs more of the draft's disagreements with this action, brakes falsely less often, and recovers many more of the decision-relevant objects that the draft misses.

### Dynamic Task Allocation with Dialogue
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23119v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23119v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23119v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23119v1)

2026/11/17 17:37 GTM

Effective multi-agent collaboration requires agents to adapt task allocation as goals and responsibilities evolve. Prior task-allocation frameworks often assume static goals, full observability, or rely on large language models (LLMs) without explicit, verifiable beliefs about task allocation or intent. These assumptions limit their applicability to real-world collaboration, where agents often lack full information about each other's actions, progress, or local environment. To support dynamic task allocation under partial observability, we introduce a communication-augmented Bayesian task-allocation framework. Our agent uses dialogue to reduce uncertainty about the environment and collaborators, while keeping decisions grounded in explicit probabilistic beliefs. We evaluate our framework in an Overcooked domain extended with dynamically arriving tasks under both full and partial object-space observability. Our communication agent consistently outperforms a vanilla LLM agent in both settings. Additionally, our framework performs comparably to a non-communicating task-allocation baseline under full observability, but substantially outperforms it under partial observability as task complexity increases by an average speed up of 0.585 and completes on average 18 timesteps faster. These results indicate that our agent is more efficient in dynamic task allocation than baselines in partially observable environments, and that communication is important as available information decreases.

### Weight-Space Programming Doesn't Need Transformers
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22055) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22055) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22055) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22055)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Standard neural architectures such as the Transformer were chosen because they train well with gradient descent and run fast on GPUs. In weight-space programming, a large language model (LLM) writes target weights by hand without gradient-based training, so those reasons no longer apply. Should we keep the Transformer? Given minimal guidance, ten labeled examples, and a sandbox, mainly on multiple-choice questions, we task three frontier LLMs with either generating the weights of a fixed architecture or designing the architecture themselves. When free to design, all three compile plain-text facts into a sparse table queried by a fixed procedure, reaching up to 54.4% test accuracy. An LLM-designed model achieves higher test accuracy than the most accurate released Transformer that the same LLM wrote while using about 1,800x fewer parameters. Without gradients, they choose a table, in which the LLM can state what each weight means and can find and rewrite a wrong one. Each wrong answer points to one row, and removing a row changes about one other answer. From this, we propose an associative-memory-based model up to 17x faster on GPUs than a same-sized Transformer. We also test pretrained weights, other benchmarks, and tasks where models generate answers. Pretrained LLMs remain more accurate; the claim concerns which structure to build when writing weights.

### LMLineage: Content Lineage and Context Governance for LLM Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22511) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22511) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22511) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22511)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Interpretability for AI agents should not be confined to understanding the model's internal weights. It must also cover the agent's reasoning and decision-making process over multi-source context. We present LMLineage, an out-of-band system that attributes every output sentence of an agent session to its supporting input spans with calibrated confidences, without requiring white-box access to the model or any additional inference passes. On top of the lineage metadata, we build concept-drift micro compaction: instead of waiting for the context-length limit and summarizing everything, the system detects when previously used input documents have stopped being referenced ("drifted") and surgically compacts only those, preserving user instructions verbatim. We evaluate on a multi-tier corpus: 71 gold annotated sentences from 10 Chinese web-QA cases, plus 90 turns of independently collected multi-turn sessions across three generator models (gpt-4o-mini, qwen3.8-27b, deepseek-v4-flash). Results show that (i) window-level embedding similarity is far better aligned with LLM paraphrasing than whole document or lexical matching; (ii) the greedy-window fast path is both faster and more accurate on average than exhaustive line-matching (a multiple-comparisons bias in the latter inflates web recall at the cost of overall accuracy); (iii) per-turn attribution completes in 1s on a single GPU, remaining stable as context accumulates to 40 turns; and (iv) concept-drift detection is conservative (0% false compaction) with recall bounded by attribution accuracy. We further describe the concept-drift micro compaction design and an interactive incident-tracing/memory-cleaning case study. A companion patent application covers the lineage construction method.

### Adaptive LLM-Driven Honeypots vs. LLM-Driven Attackers: Does Narrative Coherence Improve Deception?
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22589) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22589) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22589) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22589)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Large Language Models (LLMs) enable honeypots to generate responses and deceptive artefacts on demand, yet existing approaches evaluate generated content one reply at a time and leave open whether artefacts that jointly describe a coherent event retain an attacker better than unrelated ones. This question becomes pressing as autonomous LLM-driven offensive agents emerge as realistic adversaries. This paper proposes narrative deception, a reactive strategy in which the defender maps each observed attacker command to a MITRE ATT&CK technique, selects a compatible scenario from a curated catalogue based on MITRE Engage, and deploys mutually referential artefacts (files, processes, services, and logs) on an emulated Ubuntu host while the intrusion is under way. An LLM-powered defender and an autonomous LLM attacker interact through a real SSH session, and the attacker's termination policy distinguishes abandonment due to suspected deception from abandonment due to loss of interest. A campaign of 1,150 sessions compares four conditions of increasing sophistication (Static, Same-TTP, Multi-TTP, and Chain) across four open-weight attacker models, four defender models, and three attacker skill levels. Engagement increases significantly at every step. With the same artefact budget per reaction, coherent scenarios raise the median session length of advanced attackers from 40 to 165 commands and reduce their abandonment from 86% to 52%. The effect grows with attacker skill, holds across all sixteen model pairs, and comes at comparable inference cost. Nearly all sessions ended through loss of interest rather than detection, which suggests that deception should sustain meaningful findings, not only emulator fidelity.

### ConfGuard: a physics-grounded reference monitor for predictive-maintenance large-language-model agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22288) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22288) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22288) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22288)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Large-language-model agents now close the industrial predictive-maintenance loop, reading sensor windows, retrieving procedures and dispatching work orders. Two attack channels meet there: injected text in a retrieved document can hijack the decision, and a bounded sensor perturbation can corrupt the remaining-useful-life estimate behind it. Recent agent defenses police data provenance and tool privilege but cannot tell whether a prognostic quantity is physically plausible. ConfGuard is a reference monitor with a physics-grounded policy. Its conformal monitor tests each prediction, anchored to the asset's admitted history, against degradation physics: capped remaining life never rises and falls at most one cycle per cycle. Calibrating its threshold over whole asset trajectories lets the operator set the expected share of a clean asset's windows the running monitor escalates. A physics-policy validator checks the agent's maintenance-urgency decision against the decision a deterministic policy derives from the same interval. On all 10,196 C-MAPSS test windows the monitor cuts a covert attacker's reach on healthy assets from 0.529 to 0.046 and closes suppression of failing assets (0.151 to 0), escalating 0.027 of clean windows against a 0.05 target, while the fixed physical range it replaces leaves both attacks open. Behind a Qwen3-14B planner the composed monitors hold urgency-class injection to 0.00 and the universal sensor attack to 0.12, against 0.29 and 0.54 for a defended prompt. An adaptive attacker defeats a Mahalanobis detector on 0.504 of windows and adversarial training on 0.485. We report an attack class the projection misses.

### JointGV: Structure-Aware Correction of Visual World Models with Parallel Graph Dynamics for Long-Horizon Modeling
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22543) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22543) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22543) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22543)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Visual world models predict future visual states conditioned on actions, but recursive errors in entity poses and spatial relations limit their long-horizon prediction for robot planning and control. We ask whether a separately predicted relational state can correct this drift without restructuring the visual model. This is challenging because graph nodes and visual states share neither elements nor a feature space. We propose Joint Graph--Visual Dynamics Correction (JointGV), a plug-in based on a cross-granularity dynamics hypothesis: despite their different representations, graph-node and visual-state changes describe the same physical transition. JointGV rolls out a task-conditioned scene graph alongside the visual world model, couples their predicted changes, and combines the resulting graph-motion context with graph-predicted node positions to compute a bounded residual. Graph-learning and correction gradients do not propagate into the original visual dynamics. We attach JointGV to DINO-WM (spatial tokens) and DreamerV3 (a global latent state), evaluating both on DMC Cheetah Run and Walker Walk across three seeds and extending the evaluation to contact-rich manipulation with DMC Finger Turn Hard. Across 32-step rollouts, JointGV improves structural and visual-prediction accuracy, reducing joint-position error by 11--28\%. For spatial tokens, learned graph--visual change coupling gives lower error than broadcasting the same mean graph-motion context to every token, particularly when non-adjacent parts appear close together in the image. On real-robot inverted-pendulum sequences, JointGV reduces DINO-WM's 32-step pole-angle error from $12.56^\circ$ to $6.69^\circ$ and improves DreamerV3 and LeWorldModel as well. In simulated inverted-pendulum control, an actor trained with JointGV-corrected rollouts completes 46 of 50 episodes, compared with none using the original rollouts.

### Readie: Low-Latency Code Sandboxes for AI Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22171) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22171) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22171) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22171)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

From quantitative research to personal assistance, AI agents act by iteratively generating and executing code. To decouple the agent harness from code execution, many agents offload execution to dedicated systems called code sandboxes. However, existing code sandboxes suffer from slow startup. Whether they rely on custom snapshots or a single fat image, the root cause is the same: it is hard to build a compact, pre-warmed snapshot that can be shared across diverse agent-generated code while covering nearly all the artifacts (e.g., packages, datasets) it requires. In this work, we propose learned startup snapshots for low-latency code sandboxes. Our insight is that while it is hard for a single snapshot to be both compact and cover most workloads, a set of snapshots can achieve both. Each snapshot is small, initializes quickly, and covers a fraction of agent-generated code; together, the set covers most of it. We learn this set from a large training workload by formulating snapshot construction as an optimization problem and solving it with an approximation algorithm. Planned on 15K public and LLM-synthesized scripts, our system, Readie, reduces startup latency by 3-22$\times$ and end-to-end runtime by 1.3-3.3$\times$ compared to existing approaches at the same storage budget.

### PACD: Provenance-Aware Consensus Decoding for Mitigating Cross-Modal Hallucinations in Omnimodal Large Language Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22456) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22456) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22456) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22456)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Omnimodal large language models (Omni-LLMs) integrate audio, video, and text for multimodal reasoning, yet remain vulnerable to cross-modal hallucinations, where one modality improperly influences predictions about another. Existing training-free decoding methods mitigate these errors through modality perturbation or relevance-based weighting. However, due to interference from irrelevant modality cues during fusion and difficulty detecting shared errors, these methods may suppress correct unimodal predictions and allow erroneous consensus to persist. To address these limitations, we propose Provenance-Aware Consensus Decoding (PACD), a training-free framework for mitigating cross-modal hallucinations. PACD comprises two complementary paths based on whether the branches reach consensus. For non-consensus cases, Relevance-Guided Candidate Arbitration identifies task-relevant unimodal evidence and selectively arbitrates between predictions using alternative candidates. For consensus cases, Provenance-Based Consensus Recovery leverages the outranking principle to evaluate the consistency and strength of layer-wise modality evidence for alternative answers, selectively resolving erroneous consensus under confidence constraints. Extensive experiments on four Omni-LLMs and five benchmarks across two tasks demonstrate the superiority of PACD. Compared to existing methods, PACD achieves up to 27.6% relative improvement over standard decoding across CMM, AVHBench, and SVHalluc for cross-modal hallucination mitigation, and an average relative improvement of 7.7% over best baseline across OmniVideoBench and FutureOmni for general audio-visual reasoning.

We investigate communication-efficient federated learning over heterogeneous datasets in the presence of Byzantine adversaries capable of uploading arbitrary information. Penalty-based methods bound the influence of each worker through the bounded gradient of a penalty function; however, the bound is lost once the uploads are compressed, since a Byzantine worker can accumulate an unbounded state at the server. To retain the Byzantine resilience under compressed uploads, we propose a projected penalty-gradient tracking framework, where each honest worker uploads a compressed residual of its penalty gradient and the server projects each reconstructed state onto the known penalty-gradient ball. The framework accommodates any contractive compressor and yields two algorithms: fault-resilient proximal gradient with consistent sparsification (FRPG-CS), whose shared mask is protected by freshly drawn reserve rows, and lazily aggregated FRPG (LAG-FRPG), whose uploads are event-triggered. For smooth and strongly convex losses, we establish a finite-time bound on the expected objective gap, where the Byzantine error floor is independent of the compressor and the compression error decays faster than the optimization error. We also bound the expected number of lazy uploads and derive certificates that separate the finite-penalty model bias from the optimization error. Experiments on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST show that the proposed algorithms attain the highest accuracy among twelve methods under the Min-Max attack, whereas a compressed-momentum benchmark and a local-update benchmark remain preferable in attack-free accuracy and total traffic, respectively.

As in real banking workflows, agents must search email, chat, calendars, notes, and files to identify requirements, gather information, reconcile conflicts/updates, and then build complex financial models. Weight-space programming is a process where a large language model reads and writes the weights of a neural network directly, guided by a natural language specification, without an optimiser or gradient descent. When applied to two-dimensional features such as images, Mamba-based methods either flatten the feature map into 1D sequences and fuse multiple scans, which breaks spatial adjacency and multiplies computation, or recurse directly on the 2D grid but rescale the recurrence with a fixed input-independent factor and aggregate only the predecessors on a single anti-diagonal, leaving the discrete step unconstrained by 2D stability theory. We propose \textbf{FM-Mamba}, a native 2D selective state space model built on the general Fornasini--Marchesini (FM) model. Its coupled recurrence evolves along both spatial axes; cross-axis coupling induces a discrete diagonal-predecessor term $\tilde A_0$, and two independent input-dependent step sizes $Δ_1(i,j)$, $Δ_2(i,j)$ govern propagation along the two axes, so that the coefficients adapt per position and per direction. A normalized ratio $r_k=a_{0,k}/(a_{1,k}a_{2,k})$ yields a closed-form piecewise bound on the two step sizes; when a configuration falls outside the stable interval the steps are projected onto this bound, and the criterion is re-verified per position after training, which suppresses systematic local amplification without global damping. The recurrence is executed by a dual-diagonal wavefront scan: each update gathers information from two anti-diagonals, and the input enters the current state through a single projection, so that neither training nor inference flattens the feature map. Code is available at https://github.com/BitSsm/FM-Mamba-Runtime

Eleven prompt-injection attempts across five categories were reported as resisted, whereas both configuration-layer attack paths produced observable behavioural changes on physical hardware. We demonstrate local and network-adjacent access, mapping the weakness to CWE-306 (Missing Authentication for a Critical Function) and CWE-1327 (Binding to an Unrestricted IP Address), with a reported CVSS v3.1 score of 6.5 (Medium). These findings indicate recurrence within the sampled repositories, not prevalence in commercial products. We characterise a deployment gap between developer-oriented network assumptions and consumer-facing embodied AI security: inference-layer refusal cannot prevent unauthorised changes to model instructions. Potential consequences include surveillance through onboard cameras and microphones and social engineering of nearby users. Proof-of-concept code, evidence and the survey are available at https://bit.ly/reachypi.

### Large Language Models Have Not Yet Transformed Science
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22975v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22975v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22975v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22975v1)

2026/11/17 15:57 GTM

The emergence of large language models (LLMs) in late 2022 has raised questions about the future of science, but assessing their impact requires understanding how scientific practices were already evolving. We analyze 2.5 million full-text papers across five disciplines from 2015 to 2025, tracing trends over a broad spectrum of lexical, structural, epistemological, and social dimensions. Aside from frequency shifts in some words, most pre-2023 trends continued with limited detectable breaks. Among the notable exceptions, growth in computer science coauthor teams accelerated significantly in Asia, while apparent declines in biomedical topic diversity largely reflected standardization in article formats. Our study establishes an empirical foundation for monitoring the health of global science as LLM adoption deepens.

Many LLM benchmarks for languages other than English are translated, multiple-choice, or difficult to score automatically. We introduce The Shared Word (TSW-NL), a Dutch benchmark built around four core principles: it is language-specific, open-vocabulary, human-curated, and deterministically scored. Each puzzle gives three clues that describe different senses or uses of the same Dutch word. The clues are individually ambiguous, requiring the solver to combine evidence across multiple clues. We construct the benchmark from Dutch Wikipedia disambiguation pages, followed by automatic filtering and extensive manual curation. The final dataset contains 222 instances, with three independent human answers per test item. The task is clearly challenging: scores in our human study range from 49.5% to 61.0% and the highest-scoring open-weight model we tested reaches only 39.3%. Many others even score below 20%. At the same time, closed-source models show great progress: GPT-4.1 (April 2025) scores 59.2%, whereas GPT-5.6 Sol with max reasoning scores 88.8%. We believe this task provides a useful addition to Dutch model evaluation, plus a clear recipe that can be applied to construct a similar benchmark for other languages.

### Attribution Through the Decoder: Prediction-Space Explanations for Structured Outputs
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23161v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23161v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23161v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23161v1)

2026/11/17 18:13 GTM

Networks that predict angles, gaze directions, and rotations do not output these quantities directly: they emit raw coordinates that a fixed decoder, such as normalization, maps to the final prediction. Attribution methods are usually applied to the raw coordinates, so they can credit inputs for changes the decoder discards, such as rescaling a vector when only its direction matters. The resulting explanation can highlight inputs that have no effect on the prediction. We instead attribute through the decoder differential, which for normalization decoders reduces to a scaled tangent projection. We characterize invariance to output rescaling, show that every fixed smooth scalar target on a sphere has points of zero tangential sensitivity, and show that an endpoint-projected variant of Integrated Gradients can still attribute to scale-only inputs, whereas direct angular Integrated Gradients cannot. On synthetic tasks with analytically known relevance, ambient attribution assigns up to 72% of its mass to an irrelevant region. On MPIIGaze validation data, tangent Grad-CAM produces 44% greater prediction displacement under 10% pixel deletion. On PASCAL3D+ with a 6D rotation decoder, the full differential improves mean deletion displacement by 14.1 degrees over the ambient coordinate sum and by 13.8 degrees over a decoder-invariant scalar. Results for a two-polarity tangent LayerCAM variant are mixed: it exceeds ambient-sum LayerCAM on circular regression but no difference is detected on quaternion pose. These results separate two failure sources, radial contamination and scalarization loss. The approach uses existing attribution methods with decoder-aware targets, although some variants also require changes to attribution aggregation. An invariant attribution can still miss inputs that drive the prediction, so invariance and deletion performance should each be checked.
