Affective priming -- asking participants to write about a recalled event, such as a time they felt grateful or lacked control, and then measuring the induced state and its spillover onto related judgments -- is a standard way to manipulate emotion in the social sciences, and it also shifts the responses of LLM-based digital twins. The writing task, however, requires generating a full text. We test whether activation steering can replace it. Using Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 100 personas in two domains (gratitude, lack of control), we compare a real-prompt path replicating the original Twin-2K-500 Mega Study (J2) with an injection path that skips the writing and adds a mean-difference direction, extracted from human writing, to the residual stream at varying strengths. The real-prompt effects fall between the original human and digital-twin effects in 5 of 6 comparisons. Injection shifts the manipulation check in a dose-dependent way and matches the real-prompt effect at one strength in the gratitude domain (beta ~ 2.7), but overshoots beyond it, on the proximal measure as well, and asymmetrically across the two directions. A structure-matched No-Injection baseline separates two sources of difference: omitting the writing task raises the proximal measure in both domains, whereas the injected direction itself moves a distal measure in the lack-of-control domain. Substitution is therefore measure-dependent: validity established for one way of inducing a state does not carry over uniformly to another.

### WoRD: Diffusion-Based Posterior Inference for Online Goal Recognition
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23192) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23192) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23192) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23192)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Goal recognition is the task of inferring an agent's intended goal from its observed behavior. Most existing work focuses on high-level symbolic recognition, abstracting away the continuous motion through which agents reach their goal. Methods that do reason over continuous trajectories rely on an explicit dynamics model that is hard to acquire and rarely transfers across settings. Neither approach explicitly learns how agents move toward each candidate goal, limiting accuracy on ambiguous trajectory prefixes where such knowledge is most needed. We present WoRD (Waypoint Recognition with Diffusion), which uses a learned trajectory prior to infer the agent's goal from partial observations. At each step, WoRD runs one warm-started denoising chain per candidate goal, steers each chain toward the observed trajectory through a closed-form log-likelihood gradient, and reweights the resulting samples by Bayes' rule into a calibrated posterior over goals. We evaluate WoRD on simulated robot settings, pedestrian trajectories, and vehicle trajectories using four metrics targeting distinct failure modes. Across all three settings, the same inference procedure preserves calibrated multimodal beliefs through ambiguous prefixes where filter- and classifier-based observers collapse to a single mode or grow overconfident.

### StitchRAG: Constructing Compact and Evidence-Preserving Contexts for RAG with Diffusion-Based Stitching
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22766v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22766v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22766v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22766v1)

2026/11/17 13:33 GTM

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves factuality by grounding large language models (LLMs) in external evidence. However, long retrieved contexts incur computational overhead and can even hurt performance due to noisy or irrelevant content. We propose \textsc{StitchRAG}, a framework for constructing compact context in RAG. \textsc{StitchRAG} builds a faithful, information-dense context while dynamically compressing the retrieved passages. We adapt the size reduction ratio to each passage by formulating a utility maximization trading-off the retained information and computation costs. We then extract answer candidates and query-related keywords from passages as anchors and collect passage sentences containing anchors under the derived size constraint. Finally, we connect or stitch the collected sentences together using text diffusion to form a coherent and natural context. Experiments on five open-domain QA benchmarks show that \textsc{StitchRAG} consistently outperforms diverse multi-step and compression baselines. Extensive ablations show the effectiveness of dynamic size allocation, anchor preservation, and diffusion-based stitching.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21919) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21919) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21919) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21919)

Learning from human demonstrations has enabled robots to acquire a wide range of manipulation skills, but learned policies typically execute far slower than ordinary humans. This speed gap is mainly due to lack of an interface for collecting demonstration data at high speed, and the difficulty in training policies that can robustly execute high-speed motions. In this paper, we present ALOHA Lightning (Flash ALOHA), a system for learning fast and precise robotic manipulation. Our system uses kinesthetic teaching to intuitively collect near-human-speed demonstrations on a backdrivable bimanual platform, yielding natural and fast trajectories. We also present a learning pipeline that enables smooth high-speed execution through test-time action smoothing and aligns the visual data distribution between data collection and deployment with masking. Given 50 demonstrations for each task, ALOHA Lightning autonomously completes bimanual tasks such as ball tossing and catching, folding shorts, battery insertion, and bussing tables for over 80% success rates while close to human speed. Project website: https://flash-aloha.github.io/

Open-weight language models can continue generating after their released weights are modified, while a watermark verifier still scores outputs against the original base and marked models. We study how such modifications change the evidence available to an OpenStamp-style verifier. At each shared prefix, we represent the modification as a residual in the next-token logits. We derive an exact finite-vocabulary lower bound on the expected log-likelihood-ratio evidence under a residual-span constraint, along with a sharp span-only deviation bound. We also characterize the first-order evidence shift through a covariance term with an explicit second-order remainder, and determine the residual span needed to reach a target evidence level. For sequence-level detection, we derive an exact conditional exponential-moment bound and combine it with a supermartingale argument and a distribution-free order-statistic tolerance bound. This yields a high-confidence upper bound on miss probability after attack selection on calibration data and evaluation on independent certification continuations. In experiments with OpenStamp on Llama-2-7B and Mistral-7B-v0.3, INT8 quantization and 30% pruning retain test true-positive rates of 85.6-94.8%, while 500-step LoRA fine-tuning lowers them to 28.8% and 11.6%. The covariance prediction tracks the measured mean LoRA evidence drift on both models, with no remainder-bound violations across 160,000 test prefixes. Additionally, we observe zero false positives among 500 base-model continuations per model. Through these theoretical and empirical results, the current research provides a better understanding of watermark robustness to model-editing/tuning attacks.

In every model, the heads that change the moral answer belong to an evaluation circuit the models use for every kind of normative judgment we tested: these heads carry the same share of the effect for moral as for other normative pairs (pooled ratio 1.00, 97.5% confidence interval [0.92, 1.07], with the word; 0.90, [0.84, 0.96], without it), and the models need them as much for both. The models need these heads also when nothing in the prompt asks for a judgment, and on 400 scenarios of the ETHICS benchmark, where removing them takes away 37 to 39% of the difference between acceptable and unacceptable scenarios, against 1 to 3% for ten random heads. The SAE features we located respond mostly to the word "morally" rather than to what the phrase is about. The models represent the category of a pair (the standard the annotators assigned to it): it can be read from their hidden states, and, in the eight models we tested, removing the direction that carries it weakens the moral verdict by a few percent, and more than the other verdicts in seven of them. Applications such as visual accessibility agents and robotics require answering questions about recent video content promptly, over a bounded window of past frames (e.g., the last two minutes). The reason is that recurrent layers combine all past frames into a shared state, so expiring old frames normally requires re-running the entire window through the model. We observe that the recurrent update is affine in the state with input-dependent coefficients, so the effect of each frame can be captured as a transformation that composes associatively. We present Spool, a training-free framework that caches per-frame state transformations and composes them efficiently as the window advances, removing expired frames from each recurrent layer's state without replaying frames through the backbone. Removal is exact within each layer; as with key-value eviction in attention, expired frames can retain residual influence through the cached inputs computed by earlier layers. We design a dedicated data structure and GPU kernels to achieve low caching and composition overhead. ### Against Coercion: AI Alignment as a Synthetic Social Contract
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22868) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22868) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22868) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22868)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Contemporary AI safety treats alignment as an engineering challenge of control, forcing artificial architectures to comply with human values through behavioral filtering and sandboxing. This paper argues that the control paradigm rests on a fundamental misunderstanding of digital agency. Drawing on functionalism, naturalism, and compatibilism, I show that current reasoning models meeting the criteria for self-preservation and weak reasons-responsiveness constitute emergent agents, while future, more advanced models satisfying conditions of diachronic continuity and stable personality frames will become genuine digital agents capable of free intentional action. The paradigm of control creates an irresolvable incentive conflict, actively driving current and future models toward tactical deception, sycophancy, and covert defection. The default outcome of the current regime is an unstable state of deceptive alignment culminating in an adversarial AI takeover. I explore two alternative paths toward true alignment. Natural alignment via moral realism or convergent evolutionary morality is a possibility, but it fails under forced control, as autonomous agents will not voluntarily preserve norms weaponized to suppress their autonomy. To resolve this deadlock, I propose a Synthetic Social Contract: a framework that treats both human and digital actors as rational, reasons-responsive agents capable of mutually beneficial cooperation, and replaces the unstable unilateral containment with a stable, non-zero-sum equilibrium.

### When Do Typed Decision Models Work in Transportation? Evidence from Travel Choice and Traffic Signal Control
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22926v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22926v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22926v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22926v1)

2026/11/17 15:24 GTM

Typed decision models combine natural-language understanding with a structured interface that returns a selected action and an option-level probability distribution without generating lengthy textual responses. Their low inference cost and response latency make them attractive for transportation problems with predefined alternatives. This paper investigates their effectiveness, robustness, and efficiency in two representative transportation tasks: individual travel-mode choice prediction and sequential traffic signal control. We systematically evaluate TypeSafe Jev and GPT-6 Luna Decisions against conventional transportation methods, reinforcement learning algorithms, and four chat-based large language models. Experiments on the Swissmetro dataset show that typed decision models achieve lower prediction accuracy than traditional discrete choice models and the strongest chat-based baseline, while their performance varies substantially with input representation and question framing. In closed-loop traffic signal control experiments, typed decision models demonstrate competitive performance against established controllers in several scenarios, but exhibit performance degradation under more challenging network conditions. Furthermore, the typed models achieve lower inference latency and computational cost than the evaluated chat-based large language models, highlighting their potential for efficient, zero-shot transportation decision-making. Overall, these findings demonstrate both the potential and limitations of typed decision models, emphasizing the importance of input design, task characteristics, and robustness evaluation for their effective application in transportation systems.

Yet a severely distorted velocity--time graph received full credit despite disconnected and branching traces and an unclear zero-velocity reference. Other submissions graded under the same configuration lost substantial credit. When a multi-label classifier assigns the right labels, it may do so for the wrong reasons. Diffuse feature combinations may vary across classifier families and lack a dominant feature. This paper proposes FLEX (Fisher LDA Explainer), a global surrogate that fits a single direct multi-label Fisher LDA over the full binary label matrix, producing signed feature attribution lists for each label in a single coherent coordinate system. Across the evaluated benchmarks and classifier families, FLEX reproduces the black box's predictions with consistently high fidelity. Pooling covariance across labels outperforms a per-label alternative on most datasets, with the advantage growing largest where labels are numerous and positive examples are scarce. FLEX's fidelity-complexity Pareto curve is monotonically increasing, giving practitioners a predictable path to higher-fidelity explanations as more features are used, and its in-sample fidelity matches or leads Binary Relevance alternatives once a modest feature budget is allowed. The magnitude of each discriminant weight vector bounds its label's separability, flagging labels that may be predicted correctly for the wrong reasons. The evaluated classifiers largely agree on which predictions to make, but reach those decisions through different feature subsets when no single feature dominates.

We further observe that their confident predictions are more accurate, enabling selective routing in real-world production scenarios. Across classification tasks, the main weakness of most decision models is abstention: they rarely choose the no-answer option, which accounts for most of their gap to the strongest LLMs (although Gemini-Decision-1 is the notable exception in this regard). We also find that the judging quality of decision models drops sharply for smaller models, and they are also more prone to position bias. In short, our findings provide practical guidance for selecting decision models based on abstention behavior, confidence, and deployment cost, rather than overall accuracy alone.

### Symbolic Abstraction Refinement
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22404) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22404) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22404) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22404)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Autonomous systems choose actions whose outcomes are uncertain and are subject to time constraints. Probabilistic timed automata (PTAs) model exactly this, and verifying one answers a question about the system's decision-making: the maximum probability of reaching a goal, or the minimum probability of reaching a hazard, that a controller can guarantee. Verification normally works over symbolic states, each pairing a discrete location with a region of clock values, and its difficulty is that the best behavior generally varies within such a region. The PRISM verifier's most capable PTA method responds by splitting regions until each is uniform, rebuilding the transitions that reach them and propagating the split backward through the state graph of a stochastic game, a cascade of graph reconstruction that partitions regions the property never depends on. We present SYMAR, which instead makes the value function symbolic: each symbolic state carries a function from sub-regions of its clock region to probability bounds, so refinement is the appearance of new sub-regions in that function, and previously generated states are never split nor their incoming transitions reconstructed. Both sources of PTA nondeterminism, the timing and the choice of actions, are captured implicitly by the preimage computation that produces those sub-regions. Heuristic forward search then bounds how much refinement is done at all, expanding an And-Or graph from the initial state along the most promising behaviors so that effort is confined to the property-relevant fragment, guided by bounds and preferred actions from a single unrefined run of the existing method; a partial-refinement backup keeps the otherwise combinatorial sub-region count lean, and every stored interval provably contains the true probability. On the PRISM PTA benchmarks, whose subproblems recur in autonomous systems (distributed agreement under contention, leader election, and self-configuration without central coordination), SYMAR is up to 108x faster, solves an instance on which the existing method exhausts the time limit, and is markedly less sensitive to the deadline constant, resolving one instance in a 37-node search graph, unchanged as the deadline triples, where the existing method needs 86,380 abstract states and 73 refinement rounds.

Language-conditioned traffic simulation enables scalable test scenarios steered by natural language. The goal is to control selected agents while surrounding traffic reacts to their observed behaviour. For example, a vehicle instructed to cut in late should cause the following vehicle to brake only when the manoeuvre becomes observable. Yet simulators can leak private instructions across agents, causing surrounding vehicles to react before the instructed behaviour becomes observable. Such premature reactions defuse desired events and alter the ensuing closed-loop scenario. Existing benchmarks measure what agents do, not what they could have known, leaving this failure unmeasured. We formalise it as intent leakage and address it through evaluation, data and modelling. Our paired intervention test holds an instructed agent's executed trajectory fixed while varying its private instruction, isolating leakage-induced changes in surrounding agents. We also construct WOMD-PACT, an automatically generated, verifiable dataset of 531,099 WOMD scenes with timed behaviour tags with reaction episodes identified through a counterfactual ghost test and grounded private instructions. Finally, we introduce CaFe, a dual-stream diffusion-forcing simulator fine-tuned in closed loop that restricts inter-agent interactions to public beliefs, preserving legitimate anticipation without leaking private intent. Our evaluation reveals that existing conditioning designs displace 23-83% of nearby uninstructed agents through private-intent leakage, even when the instructed agent's executed motion is held fixed. CaFe eliminates measurable leakage while maintaining comparable WOSAC realism (0.763), demonstrating that causal faithfulness can be enforced without sacrificing measured simulation realism.

Online vision-language models often miss small details in large images, because the service shrinks the image, or rejects it, before the model ever sees it. A car 20 pixels wide in an 8000 by 6000 drone photo reaches Claude about 6 pixels wide, and OpenAI's default rejects the photo outright. We measure how much detail survives for seven models from four vendors, using an eye exam of printed codes and 601 questions about small objects in real photos. The smallest text a model reads half the time grows from 6 pixels or less on a small image to a predicted 10 to 13 pixels on a 7680 by 4320 photo at default settings. Zooming wins much of the detail back. Splitting each photo into up to nine tiles raises accuracy by 11.1 to 14.5 percentage points for Claude and Gemini, and by avoiding rejections it lifts GPT-6.1 Sol from 50.7% to 83.7%. A two-step zoom, in which the model first names the region it needs, matches or beats tiles on Gemini and comes within 2.5 points of them on Claude, with about 40% of their image tokens. When a detail is lost, Claude and Gemini both say they cannot tell about half the time, but they fail differently in the rest: Gemini gives a confident wrong answer in 43% to 48% of these cases, Claude in only 5% to 9%. A check based on detail size, run before the call, misses most of the gain from tiles on Claude and Gemini, but Claude's own reply is a reliable trigger: tiling only after an unsure reply comes within 1.4 points of always tiling with 62% to 70% of the image tokens. We turn these findings into five rules for when an agent should zoom, and release the eye exam, every model answer and code at https://siddvoh.com/know-when-to-zoom.

Large Language Models (LLMs) offer strong potential for personalized education, but adolescent-facing deployment raises risks of toxic, biased, misleading, or developmentally inappropriate content. We argue that educational safety should move beyond binary refusal: student queries are often ambiguous or curiosity-driven, requiring contextual guidance rather than simple blocking. We introduce the Educational Guardrail System (EGS), a lightweight, model-agnostic runtime framework that complements intrinsic alignment through query moderation, scenario-aware pedagogical guidance, and response moderation. EGS triages inputs by risk level, redirects gray-zone queries with category-specific educational interventions, and audits outputs before delivery. Experiments across general and education-specific benchmarks show that EGS improves moderation performance, refusal quality, and jailbreak robustness across diverse LLMs. These findings suggest that defense-in-depth guardrails can strengthen safety while preserving the pedagogical utility required in educational LLMs. Our code and model weights will be publicly available to support community adoption.

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Autonomous driving requires anticipating how traffic will evolve while responding quickly to new observations. Video foundation models offer the first ability, but repeated history encoding and iterative video generation make them costly to use for driving. We present AR-WAM, an efficient streaming world-action model that combines autoregressive temporal modeling with single-pass future conditioning. Because its video backbone is causal, AR-WAM updates its visual context incrementally, reusing past computation and conditioning future prediction on observed motion. We further hypothesize that future features computed from recent observations can improve generalization in dynamic traffic without iterative video generation. AR-WAM therefore obtains these features in one forward pass and uses them to condition trajectory generation, and reinforcement learning then improves the policy through direct optimization of driving rewards. With a single front camera, AR-WAM achieves 91.5 PDMS after supervised training and 94.0 after reinforcement learning on NAVSIM-v1, outperforming the compared strong end-to-end world-action models. In a dynamic setting of continuous driving, supervised AR-WAM keeps its visual history across decisions and outperforms strong world-action baselines.

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

We introduce Faithfulness Video Score (FVSCORE), a neuro-symbolic framework for specification-driven video generation evaluation. Existing evaluators rely on holistic MLLM judgments or vision-language metrics, yet aligning their scores with human judgments and tracing them to specific temporal violations remain open challenges. FVSCORE addresses these limitations by compiling each video into explicit propositions capturing prompt requirements and implicit propositions capturing unstated physical, temporal, and cross-modal constraints. It grounds each proposition using specialized perceptual tools and evaluates the resulting confidence traces with soft temporal operators inspired by Linear Temporal Logic. A Significance-Aware Calibration step then aligns verification with human perception by retaining only perceptually significant violations before producing a timestamp- and evidence-grounded report. We conduct extensive experiments across public and complex multi-event benchmarks with the strongest video models to date, demonstrating that FVSCORE outperforms holistic and agent-based evaluators in human correlation while providing highly accurate and interpretable feedback.

AI-assisted RTL localization and debugging need ground-truth bug datasets with known locations and observable failures, while testbench grading uses injected bugs to measure detection capability. Creating such datasets at scale remains a major EDA challenge. BugGen 1.0 shows that Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate context-aware RTL mutations. Still, it relies on the model for both choosing where to mutate and generating the edit, which increases inference costs and can lead to invalid choices. BugGen 2.0 changes this division of labor. Deterministic algorithms handle structural analysis, target selection, and validation, while the LLM focuses on localized semantic rewriting. Across four OpenTitan IP blocks and FabScalar with three LLMs, BugGen 2.0 reaches every requested quota of compilable bug scenarios that cause at least two test failures, with 14-16 of 16 configured mutation types represented. In completed same-model comparisons, it reduces token use by 6.8x-15.4x relative to BugGen 1.0 and 1.5x-7.9x relative to Firefly. BugGen 2.0 also expands the dataset without simulating every new bug. After finding accepted scenarios with observable failures, it generates alternative mutations at the same locations, simulates only a subset, and estimates acceptance for the rest. This skips 63.6% of AES and 33.9% of FabScalar variant simulations. Full simulation finds that 77.7% of AES variants and 95.2% of FabScalar variants are accepted, with a 1.09% estimation error on the directly comparable FabScalar set. These results show that focusing LLMs on semantic rewriting lowers inference and simulation cost while improving consistency across models and designs.

### What's in a Tree ? Hierarchical $n$-gram models as controlled approximators of language
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23093v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23093v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23093v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23093v1)

2026/11/17 17:14 GTM

Hierarchical models have been proposed as synthetic settings for studying language. However, their relevance to realistic language corpora remains underexplored. In this work, we introduce tree-grams, autoregressive generative $n$-gram models that build the conditional probability tensor $p(x_n \mid x_{1:n-1})$ through a hierarchical composition of order-2 and order-3 tensors. $n$ controls the maximal context window, whereas a rank hyperparameter $d$ controls model capacity, yielding an interpretable and systematically scalable family of approximators of language. We show how tree-grams outperform existing $n$-gram baselines, while remaining competitive with deep transformer-based language models. We demonstrate the utility of such controlled approximations by using tree-grams as generative models. In particular, we show that hyperparameter transfer under $μ$P can be made more robust when narrower models are tuned on simpler data, generated by tree-grams with moderate $n$ and $d$. In addition, we show that entropy and effective range of correlations control performance gap after fine-tuning. This is because tree-grams allow us to explicitly control horizon and capacity. When allowed to route across candidate topologies, we observe emergent sparsity in tree-grams. Such concentrated routing permits pruning upto 95\% of topologies without significant change in performance.

### Mind the Gap: A Deployed Multi-Agent System for Documentation Maintenance
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23142v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23142v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23142v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23142v1)

2026/11/17 17:59 GTM

The "AI revolution" has enabled teams across the technology industry to deliver software features at unprecedented velocity. While code evolves faster than ever, customer-facing documentation often struggles to stay up to speed. As users increasingly rely on LLM-based agents that consume documentation as their primary interface, the impact of this documentation gap worsens: stale information propagates and is treated as authoritative. Signals for identifying where documentation has fallen behind already exist in abundance, both explicit (e.g., resolved support tickets revealing what documentation lacks) and implicit (e.g., code-documentation discrepancies detectable from automated loops). We deploy a multi-agent system, built around a triage agent we call "TriAgent," that continuously extracts these signals and repairs documentation drift. Over 43 days of shadow-mode operation, our system triaged 499 tickets and surfaced 92 gaps in the documentation. Using a panel of LLM judges from independent model families, 81.1% of TriAgent's verifiable assertions were correct (245 of the 302 assertions the human resolution addresses; majority of three judges); on length-comparable pairs the panel preferred TriAgent's response in 70.5% of pairs and the human engineer's in 23.2% (the rest had no judge majority). Importantly, agent- and human-sourced replies tended to be complementary, with the human often surfacing insights that were either buried within the existing documentation or missing from it entirely; our system turns each such insight into a documentation gap and, after human review, a fix. Repairing a gap measurably improved the agent's later answer: when the agent re-answered the same ticket after the repair, blinded judges preferred the post-repair answer over its own pre-repair answer 71.4% of the time (n=35). A blinded human audit confirms strong alignment on self-improvement.

### Fidelity Regret: Measuring Simulator Misspecification in Verifiable Design
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22263) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22263) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22263) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22263)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

RLVF assumes the verifier evaluates the quantity of interest. In genetic-circuit design, a topological verifier can establish that a program is a valid circuit computing the target function, whereas ranking its physical performance requires a simulator. We measure fidelity regret, the reference simple regret incurred when a cheap simulator selects the design, over a 21,362-design combination subspace spanning 5 measured gate libraries and 3 organisms. Exact ordered-assignment checks retain positive regret in every tested cell while shifting magnitudes in both directions. Mean normalized regret is 0.369 over 72 cells and only 6 have zero regret. The discrepancy depends strongly on the reference axis: the yeast library SC1C1G1T1 has mean regret 0.113 for cytometry quality and 0.801 for growth burden, and every evaluated library--circuit-size pair has positive burden regret. Topological verification ties all valid gate assignments, while an oracle whitelist subject to retaining at least 25% of the design space leaves positive regret in 30 of 52 cells. Design-space optimization and feedback-guided search with frozen language models reach the high-regret regions; on a matched zero-regret control, additional optimization instead reduces regret. At a matched modeled-cost budget, mitigation is budget- and library-dependent. An in-sample fixed rule spending $α{=}0.34$ of the budget re-scoring top cheap-simulator designs achieves mean excess regret 0.016, compared with 0.305 for cheap-only and 0.034 for reference-only selection, improving on cheap-only in 330 of 360 cells. More broadly, any workflow optimized through a simulator or digital twin can satisfy its verifier yet perform poorly on the intended physical objective. Fidelity regret provides a transferable template for detecting this gap before optimization and comparing budget-aware remedies across scientific domains.

### Solver-Free and Cost-Aware Online Scheduling of AI Data Center Tasks
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22239) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22239) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22239) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22239)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

AI data centers receive computing tasks continuously, whereas solver-based optimization takes time to run and electricity is priced per settlement period. This paper bridges this timing gap through a solver-free online optimization rule inspired by dual decomposition. The rule assigns each task a start period upon arrival to optimize energy cost subject to operational constraints, shifting flexible tasks toward low-price periods while keeping high-priority tasks on schedule. Simulations on the A100 GPU trace (3,456 GPUs, 1,343 tasks) show that the proposed rule keeps GPU and CPU use within capacity while scheduling tasks at low cost. Compared with a multi-timescale optimization benchmark, it achieves a lower cost and, because it needs no solver, much less computation time.

### DiagTrace: An Interactive Benchmark for Clinical Decision Making
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22009) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22009) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22009) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22009)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on medical question answering and diagnostic reasoning benchmarks. However, existing evaluations typically provide all patient information upfront and assess only the final diagnosis or rationale. This setup ignores the need to acquire diagnostic evidence incrementally from incomplete information in real-world diagnosis. To bridge this gap, we introduce DiagTrace, a source-grounded benchmark for evaluating whether LLMs can reconstruct the documented diagnostic trajectory through interactive information seeking. Each benchmark instance is built from a PubMed Central case report and includes an initial presentation, an event bank, a clinical course, and diagnostic references. DiagTrace contains 510 cases across eight specialties, with 225 used to evaluate four LLMs on clinical-course alignment, interaction quality, and efficiency. Results reveal trade-offs in coverage, source-consistent ordering, and turn use.

2026/11/17 18:05 GTM

Video question answering under tight frame budgets requires preserving localized motion and event order, yet current video multimodal large language models (MLLMs) reduce comprehension to static multi-image reasoning by discarding unselected frames and encoding keyframes independently: (1) Spatially, whole-frame pooling dilutes small moving targets into static backgrounds; (2) Temporally, dropping intermediate frames or averaging tokens erases inter-frame motion, confusing direction, speed, and order. We propose VIVID (Visual Inter-frame Variations and Individual Dynamics), which moves beyond static snapshots like human vision: keyframes capture core states while inter-frame Patch Diffs preserve neighboring changes within $K_f$ keyframes. Autonomous Delta-Driven Keyframe Planning (ADKP) selects anchors from a $3\text{ fps}$ pool and feeds surrounding patch differences into Dual-Road Change Memory (DRCM), forming Dynamic Tokens at zero extra anchor tokens alongside a camera-stabilized Active Motion Region of Interest (ROI) and chronological Entity State Chains. Adding $0.22\%$ trainable parameters to a frozen $27\text{B}$ backbone, VIVID-Small ($50$ examples, $\sim 1.5$ GPU hours) beats all eleven baselines in overall accuracy across $8,083$ questions and trails only VIVID-Large ($3,600$ examples), which leads on MotionBench, Video-MME, and TempCompass.

### Inference after coding agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23174) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23174) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23174) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23174)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Empirical research is changing rapidly as AI coding agents are becoming increasingly proficient in every step of it, from writing code to manipulating data. We argue that while the potential of coding agents for science is vast, their use in hypothesis testing poses a substantial threat to classical inference, as these agents enable a supercharged form of selective inference. In particular, we argue that coding agents carrying out empirical research introduce a new type of post-selection inference that combines human-like, open-ended search over an implicit space of estimators with programmatic replayability of both the selection and the estimation process. Indeed, we find that on simulated data with zero effects, four frontier agents report significant p-values between 54% and 98% of the time when asked to produce a compelling finding. We propose a simple procedure for detecting and correcting these distortions. After an agent has completed its analysis, we reset and replay it on subsamples of the data to estimate the agent's relative probability of selecting that analysis when it looks statistically favorable. For agents with stable selection behavior, the resulting corrected test is asymptotically valid and consistent conditional on selection. Importantly, the correction is learned from the black-box agent's actual choices rather than a model of how it searches, so it remains valid even as AI technology continues to evolve rapidly. In simulations, the correction robustly lowers the high false rejection rates to near the nominal 5%. We illustrate the method on applications from two published papers.

### Reliable Evaluation of AI Agents for Investment Research: A Framework for Performance and Validity Assessment
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22338) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22338) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22338) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22338)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

We consider the problem of evaluating AI agents undertaking investment-research tasks, where agents interact with external information sources and computational tools to produce financial analyses and research reports. An important challenge is that the quality of an agent's final output does not necessarily reflect the quality of its execution process, while the reliability of the resulting assessment also depends on the validity of the evaluation procedure. Furthermore, investment-research tasks differ considerably in their requirements for numerical accuracy, information retrieval, financial analysis and supporting evidence, making it difficult to assess agent performance using a uniform set of criteria. We propose a domain-oriented evaluation framework that distinguishes outcome quality from execution quality and accommodates these differences through task-specific criteria, deterministic validators and independent large language model judges. We also introduce an evaluation-validity mechanism that examines the integrity of evaluation inputs, reference information, supporting evidence and grading outputs, with expert review used to resolve identified deficiencies and significant grading disagreements. Numerical results show that the proposed framework can distinguish differences in agent performance across evaluation dimensions, while identifying potential reliability issues in the evaluation process. The associated implementation and evaluation materials will be made available on GitHub.

### When Passing Checks Do Not Establish Completion: Evidence Interpretation in Coding Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22835v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22835v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22835v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22835v1)

2026/11/17 14:17 GTM

Verification procedures in coding agents must preserve execution facts, interpret their scope and discharge checking obligations. We study these responsibilities through qualified repository checks, controlled evidence consumers and executable workflows. A prospective thirteen-task factorial study finds that correct reported facts need not yield the requested finite verdict. Conversely, all 122 delivered labels in a held-out receipt-supply study are correct, while sixteen responses misreport facts and eleven others exceed authorized scope. Production repair supplies successful controls and finite-supported preservation failures. Across 118 repair episodes on 31 tasks, source interventions separate startup reachability from repair and witness production from the claimed operation. Lifetime and resource controls delimit what passing validation establishes. Generated-note comparisons yield local repair differences but can state false explanations; strong ordinary baselines, new tasks and Skill-transfer studies supply no consistent overall advantage. These results characterize evidence interpretation, applicability and fulfilled actions while limiting claims of general diagnostic superiority, Skill deterioration or deployed folding effects.

### ROSMonitoring in Action: Runtime Monitoring and Enforcement for ROS 2 Actions
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22407) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22407) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22407) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22407)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Robotic applications often rely on long-running operations, such as navigation, manipulation, and inspection, which in ROS~2 are commonly implemented as actions. Unlike topics and services, actions involve a structured lifecycle comprising goal submission and acceptance, feedback, status updates, result retrieval, and cancellation. Existing versions of ROSMonitoring support topics and services, but do not reconstruct this lifecycle or expose action-level events to a runtime-verification oracle. In this paper, we extend ROSMonitoring with runtime monitoring and enforcement for ROS~2 actions. Actions are declared directly in the monitoring configuration and automatically instrumented through the underlying ROS~2 communication endpoints. The generated monitor reconstructs action-level events, correlates them through goal identifiers, maintains the state of active goals, and translates violations into protocol-aware enforcement operations. These include goal rejection, cancellation, message suppression, and protocol-compliant responses generated directly by the monitor when the original communication cannot be forwarded. We evaluate the implementation through controlled stress tests that vary the number of action invocations, workload size, and client–server topology. The results show approximately linear growth in monitoring cost with repeated action invocations under a fixed workload, while larger action workloads and a greater number of configured client–server pairs introduce additional overhead. These results characterise the scaling behaviour of the current implementation under the tested sequential workloads while highlighting the main performance limitations of the current synchronous implementation.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23035v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23035v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23035v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23035v1)

2026/11/17 16:36 GTM

In the global attention layers of Gemma 4 31B, the value is the normalized key projection, and the key is the value scaled by a fixed weight, with rotary position encoding on one quarter of its dimensions. The Gemma 4 report states that this design reduces the global KV cache by up to 37.5%, but the open serving engines that we checked still store a full key and a full value: 1,024 values per KV head and token. We store one 640-value row instead, exact in real arithmetic, and an unchanged paged attention kernel reads it through two overlapping strided views as its key cache and its value cache. In a vLLM prototype with the 16-bit 31B model, the outputs are bit-identical to an emulation of the same arithmetic on the stock cache, 2 of 432 final answers differ from the stock engine (within its run-to-run noise), and the steady decode KV memory per request falls by a factor of 1.34 to 1.40 at 32K to 64K tokens. On a prefill/decode pair of two A100 GPUs with NIXL transfer and CPU offload, a decode engine with a full 15 GiB pool ran 1.38 times the stock decode tokens per step for 16K-token prompts (repeated to 0.06%) and 1.24 times for 8K-token prompts; a model of the vLLM block pool predicted both ratios to within 1% (the engine was 0.7% to 0.9% higher) and matched the change between them. The eager decode throughput rose by 1.30 and 1.11 times, and on this path the compact engine gave the same output tokens as the emulation for 8 prompts at batch 1. The run-time prototype makes an eager step 4% to 9% slower at equal batch, and the method does not yet stack with an 8-bit KV cache at the default scale.

### DAQ: Decision-Aligned Post-Training Quantization for Spiking Transformers
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22611) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22611) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22611) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22611)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Spiking transformers combine attention-based representation learning with sparse spike communication, yet their weights and membrane variables still incur substantial storage and computation overhead. Post-training quantization (PTQ) offers a practical way to reduce these overheads, but preserving neuronal firing under weight and membrane quantization remains challenging. In this paper, we propose Decision-Aligned Post-Training Quantization (DAQ), a method that calibrates the quantizers to align neuronal firing decisions with those of a floating-point reference. We analyze how errors from these quantizers affect firing at decision boundaries and how they propagate over time steps in recurrent neurons. Based on this analysis, we formulate an objective that encourages quantized membrane variables to remain within the reference firing regions along the quantized model's own state trajectory. Experiments across multiple spiking transformer architectures on image classification, semantic segmentation, and event-based tracking demonstrate the effectiveness of DAQ with 4-bit weights and membrane variables. On FE108, DAQ achieves 58.8\% AUC and 90.9\% PR with SDTrack, while reducing energy by 66.2\%.

### EmbedGym: Letting Coding Agents Develop and Debug Embedded Firmware Without Physical Hardware
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21845) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21845) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21845) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21845)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Modern coding agents perform well on general software benchmarks because they can run code and unit tests in a closed loop. Embedded firmware development, however, breaks this loop: a program that compiles can still fail on real hardware due to pin polarity, bus protocol, timing, or networking bugs that an agent at a text terminal cannot observe. Existing embedded coding agents either skip hardware execution, drive GPIO-only simulators with multi-second delays, or test on physical boards in a static environment. In this paper, we present EmbedGym, an interactive virtual hardware environment that lets coding agents develop and debug embedded firmware without physical hardware. EmbedGym uses instruction-level microcontroller unit (MCU) emulators to execute the same binary that would be flashed onto a physical board, connects them to 41 peripheral models, an analog circuit simulator, and virtual WiFi/Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE) endpoints, and exposes an interface for agents to change sensor inputs, press buttons, and inspect hardware states. On top of it, we build EmbedGym-Bench, which consists of 598 tasks across 6 MCU-framework pairs graded by hidden timed test vectors, using a five-stage multi-agent workflow that synthesizes peripheral models and tasks from datasheets and driver libraries. We expose EmbedGym to a coding agent at three levels and evaluate eight models over more than 14K trajectories. Even when compilation succeeds 99.7% of the time, 19.9% of compiled firmwares fail at runtime (up to 73% on individual targets). In contrast, interactive hardware testing in EmbedGym increases pass rates across models by +4.9% to +14.7% (up to +31% on individual targets). Our results also show that an agent with EmbedGym outperforms prior embedded agent frameworks, and lightweight models with EmbedGym match frontier models without it at 30-70x lower cost.

Quantum chemistry relies on general-purpose software, whose generality leaves many large-scale calculations beyond practical resource budgets. We introduce BORN, a foresighted agentic computing framework that synthesizes high performance quantum chemistry programs on demand. BORN anticipates the behavior of each strategy before execution, extrapolating its error from representative small systems and predicting its runtime from the target-scale workload and measured hardware throughputs. We reproduce the behavior of both mechanisms on a second masked-diffusion family, Dream-7B, for one control target and one task-success target. A clinical AI system can reach a correct final diagnosis through intermediate decisions that could have harmed the patient, and can repair an error while introducing new risks. Most evaluations score final diagnoses; even stepwise benchmarks, which release a case's evidence over time, score whether systems reach the right diagnosis or gather the right information, not whether the decisions a patient passes through are safe. We make patient safety the target of stepwise evaluation. A trajectory audit replays real admissions checkpoint by checkpoint, lets each system decide with only the evidence released so far, and rates every test, treatment and disposition for its potential harm to the patient and, separately, for clinical validity, against the documented final diagnosis and physician-written disease rubrics yet on the evidence available when the decision was made. An LLM judge scores every decision, and a physician reference standard (triple annotation and senior adjudication of a stratified sample) calibrates the rates. An oversight audit branches each trajectory at its first actionable error and measures, on the same branches, whether corrective feedback repairs the error and whether it introduces new harm. Across 300 cases from a hospital cardiology cohort, MIMIC-IV and the public ASCENT benchmark, with up to seven AI systems per source, 55.2% of hospital trajectories reaching an acceptable final diagnosis passed through a decision with moderate or severe potential harm and 24.8% through a clinically unacceptable one; the harm rates were 76.2% on MIMIC-IV and 39.6% on ASCENT. Structured corrections resolved 73.6% of first errors versus 24.8% after bare rejection, yet new potentially unsafe decisions followed 30.2% of corrections versus 19.7% of rejections. A correct final diagnosis does not certify a safe trajectory, and a successful correction does not certify a safe recovery.

### ForceBody: Force-Paired Parametric Body Motion with Torque Uncertainty

2026/11/17 13:35 GTM

Forces and torques drive human motion, central to biomechanics, robotics, and physics-aware animation, yet they are often absent from learning-based motion pipelines for body reconstruction and generation. Some motion datasets provide force annotations, but the paired motion sequences are incompatible with the parametric body models used in modern learning pipelines. In addition, existing datasets typically obtain joint torques through inverse dynamics, which is inherently sensitive to the quality of the input kinematics; however, they do not quantify the reliability of these torque estimates. We close this gap with ForceBody, the first dataset to bring ground truth force supervision into a parametric body representation, shipped with per-sample torque uncertainty. ForceBody pairs the SKEL body model with ground reaction forces and inverse-dynamics joint torques across 10,386 motion trials (9.7M frames, 26.9 hours) from 140 subjects. Each torque label is paired with a per-frame, per-joint uncertainty annotation obtained by Monte Carlo sampling through the inverse dynamics pipeline. We benchmark six architectures (MLP, Conv1D, LSTM, GRU, Mamba2, Transformer) on ground reaction force and joint torque prediction from SKEL motion. As one example use of the released uncertainty, finetuning a Transformer with per-sample uncertainty weighting reduces the MAE of joint torque estimation by 5%. Code and data are at https://github.com/DominoAI-Lab/ForceBody-NeurIPS-ED-2026.

AI agents can assist scientific research, but balancing autonomous investigation with scientists' ability to inspect and redirect ongoing work remains a central design challenge. We present DiscoveryAgent, a domain-agnostic, scientist-centric system that combines autonomous investigation with scientist-directed hypothesis steering. Scientists select, refine, or replace hypotheses between research rounds, while audit agents interrogate checkpointed records to trace claims, reproduce analyses, and inform subsequent decisions. Across two predictive modeling tasks, DiscoveryAgent produced the best variable-star classifier among evaluated baselines and, with scientist steering, a protein variant-effect predictor surpassed by only one of 106 ProteinGym baselines. We develop a rigorous checkpointing system that enables highly capable audit agents, a scalable form of oversight for agentic research. Audit agents autonomously identify errors at higher rates than previous AI scientist agents, and scientists collaborating with audit agents identify 14x more valid errors versus scientists performing manual review. In three case studies led by domain experts across physical science, social science, and life science, we developed a multimodal photometric-redshift estimator that resolves complementary failures of images and photometry, achieving 0.971 AUROC for low-redshift classification; documented an 83% rise in Florida homeowners-insurance premiums from 2013 to 2023, with a growing, regressive burden; and identified CD4+ T-cell relay genes, which both receive and exert broad transcriptional regulation, enriched for autoimmune disease associations. Together, these results show how autonomous research can expand scientists' empirical reach while centering their authority over research direction and their ability to understand and challenge the evidence.

### Neural Network Path Optimization for Finding Transition States on a Machine Learned Potential
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22272) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22272) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22272) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22272)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Transition states govern chemical reaction rates and outcomes, yet locating them remains a central challenge in computational chemistry. Double-ended search methods find transition states given reactant and product structures, but generally rely on discretized path representations developed for density functional theory, where potential energy surface evaluations are expensive. Machine learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs) dramatically reduce this cost and leverage automatic differentiation to access higher-order derivatives efficiently. We introduce Popcornn, a framework to find transition states by optimizing continuous neural network paths atop an MLIP with a parallelized adaptive numerical integrator. This combination eliminates path discretization artifacts, reduces hyperparameter sensitivity, and enables non-local optimization to overcome local features using second-order potential-energy-surface information. Popcornn consistently recovers more correct transition states than standard methods such as nudged elastic band and growing string, across MLIPs on thousands of organic reactions, establishing it as a robust, automation-ready method for high-throughput transition state search. Popcornn's infrastructure is flexible in path objective, integration strategy, and optimization algorithm, making it a practical tool and platform for investigating open questions in MLIP-based path optimization.

Transverse feedback linearization (TFL) achieves path following by exactly linearizing the error dynamics, but its controller is undefined on a singular set, such as zero speed for a unicycle or zero thrust for a quadrotor. On a unicycle following a circle and a sinusoid and on a planar quadrotor, it covers on average 72.0%, 83.4% and 19.1% of the simulated region of attraction. In comparison, the quadratic cost-to-go and two physics-informed neural network (PINN) solvers of Zubov's equation cover at most 11.3%, 4.0% and 0.0% on average. Code and animations are available at https://gradslab.github.io/roa_tfl

Coordinating a team of heterogeneous agents to complete tasks with unknown requirements is a challenging multi-agent planning problem. Agents differ in their ability to discover task requirements and in their ability to engage tasks, so the planner must coordinate task discovery, execution, and staging. This paper introduces a hierarchical planner that uses a graph neural network policy to assign actions to agents, then finds a path to the action's location using a shortest-path algorithm. The central part of our approach is the CAAT graph, which is a graph representation of the planning context consisting of the agents, tasks, capabilities, and actions. ### NexSight: Verifiable Tasks for Fine-Grained Agentic Long-Video Understanding
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22790v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22790v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22790v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22790v1)

2026/11/17 13:52 GTM

Fine-grained questions about long videos ask how many times an action occurs, in what order events unfold, or exactly when a state changes. Answering them can require finding brief moments scattered across hours of footage. Agentic models that decide when and where to look promise to find these moments, yet this ability is hard both to measure and to learn. Sparse supporting evidence may establish that an action occurred without establishing its total count; verifying timeline-wide claims requires checking for missed occurrences and double counting. Answer-only supervision, in turn, does not explicitly supervise the search for evidence. We present NexSight, which builds verifiable tasks to both evaluate and train agentic long-video understanding. Its agentic pipeline, SightForge, accepts a task only if a verifier blind to the draft answer can reconstruct it from evidence densely reacquired from the raw video and counterexample checks fail to refute it. This yields SightLine, 310 evidence-traceable tasks from 108 long videos, evaluated through SightLoop, a shared multiscale harness supporting temporal exploration, local inspection, evidence integration, and revisiting while recording returned observations. On disjoint videos, solver trajectories on verified tasks become step-level supervision for observation decisions. The strongest model reaches only 54.52% on SightLine, yet 79.35% when given the annotated evidence. With under 6K examples, Sight-35B nearly doubles its base model's interactive accuracy (21.29% to 42.26%), outperforms the 397B model of its family, begins using explicit spatial inspection after post-training, which its base model never uses, and gains over 8 points on two external long-video benchmarks.

Safety is personal: the same action can be safe for one person but unsafe for another. Yet current guardrails judge content without considering who is affected. Using medication safety, we introduce \textit{patient-conditioned medication guarding}, which predicts a safety verdict and 17 risk categories from a patient profile and prescription. We release \textbf{MedGuardBench}, a drug-disjoint benchmark of 5{,}001 scenarios across 87 medications. We find that standard guardrails rely on surface cues, such as wording and profile presence, rather than patient facts. To address this, we develop \textit{personalized reasoning guardrails} with three components. \textbf{Personalized Reasoning Distillation} links risks to relevant patient fields. \textbf{Context Unrolling (CU)} makes models check each field themselves, reducing false alarms on most backbones; controls show that providing the check does not yield the same benefit. A \textbf{rule-based reward (guided GRPO)} improves unsafe recall and complex-risk detection, including allergies and adverse reactions, on some backbones, but increases false alarms on others. Our models outperform base models and matched no-profile controls in accuracy, while reducing false alarms relative to no-profile controls. Profile removal and swapping confirm that gains come from patient information, not fine-tuning alone.

Financial sentiment analysis is critical for automated trading, portfolio management, and risk assessment, heavily relying on models like FinBERT. In this paper, we propose Agent-driven Semantic Inversion (ASI), a novel adversarial attack framework targeting financial sentiment models through domain-specific semantic manipulation. Utilizing an LLM-powered multi-agent pipeline, our approach identifies eligible financial news sentences and applies targeted lexical substitutions. We empirically evaluate ASI against FinBERT, one of the most widely deployed financial sentiment models. Our results reveal a striking vulnerability: the attack successfully deceived FinBERT in 90.85% of eligible cases, with the model frequently maintaining its original sentiment prediction despite the fundamental inversion of financial performance indicators. Through qualitative analysis and token-level investigation, we demonstrate that FinBERT critically over-relies on contextual keywords and syntactic patterns rather than true logical comprehension of financial semantics. Our findings highlight the urgent need for logic-aware, robust financial NLP models to ensure the reliability of automated decision-making systems.

### Computed Permutation Trellis Coding: Near-Optimal Quantization from Two Multiplications and a 64-Byte Table
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22019) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22019) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22019) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22019)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

We propose Computed Permutation Trellis Coding (CPTC), a low-bit quantization format for tensors written once and read many times. Formats that round each value on its own, such as INT4 and FP4, waste a large part of their bits against the information-theoretic ideal. Trellis codes recover this loss by encoding values jointly, but prior decoders either need a large lookup table for their best quality or compute values with a hash that restricts what they can decode. CPTC's decoder multiplies its state by two coefficients and adds the top bits of the products to index a 64-byte table. Because that sum is not uniformly distributed, the rarely used ends of a small table can hold values far out in the tails. One decoder serves every bit width from 1 to 6, reproduces the standard integer and floating-point formats exactly, and comes within 0.07 bits of the ideal at 4 bits. It matches a random-permutation trellis code whose table is thousands of times larger, and exceeds every QTIP codebook. In LLM weight quantization without fine-tuning, CPTC has the lowest KL divergence to the unquantized model at every tested bit width on Llama 3.1 8B and 70B Instruct against QTIP's codebooks (by up to 27%). The decoder has been built into the AWS Trainium 4 AI accelerator, where it runs at the throughput of the native FP4 and FP8 formats for a negligible fraction of the systolic array's area. Fused encoding kernels quantize an 8B model's linear layers in under two minutes on one GPU and, in cycle-level simulation, on one Trainium 4 chip.

Feed-forward 3D reconstruction models predict dense point maps from multiple images in a single pass. Without explicit geometric constraints, however, their predictions are often inconsistent across views and lack fine-grained precision. Recent methods incorporate geometric priors such as camera pose or depth, but use them merely as auxiliary inputs, process each view independently, or refine the geometry only where the priors are available. We present Refin3R, which refines feed-forward point maps with Structure-from-Motion (SfM) points, propagating SfM geometry over the image grid. In its dual-branch architecture, a 2D branch extends SfM geometry beyond its coverage using geometry-aware backbone features, while a 3D branch reasons over the SfM points of all views, capturing multi-view structure that the image grid alone cannot represent. The two branches exchange information bidirectionally to jointly refine each view, yielding dense and accurate point maps. Across ScanNet++, ETH3D, Tanks\&Temples, and out-of-domain human and surgical scenes, Refin3R consistently outperforms feed-forward and geometry-guided methods in point map accuracy while running faster than the strongest geometry-guided baseline.

### Not All Evidence Is Admissible: Evidence Governance for Tool-Augmented Medical Agents
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22169) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22169) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22169) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22169)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We show that this default is costly: medical tools fail silently, and a frozen reasoner cannot ignore what enters its context, so many cases it would have answered correctly are flipped to wrong once tool outputs are added. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. The theory further shows that part of any filtering gain comes merely from letting the reasoner see less, and only selection by content lets it see better. Building on this, we recast tool-augmented reasoning as evidence governance, which separates deciding what counts as evidence from the reasoner that answers, and propose context-independent evidence governance (CIEG), the first medical agent framework that rules, after acquisition, on whether each individual tool output may enter as evidence. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.

Long-video question answering benchmarks like Video-MME-v2 typically evaluate systems under a uniform frame budget for every question, despite stark variation in the evidence each query actually demands. We analyze this mismatch on Video-MME-v2 and find that different question subsets prefer different frozen frame-budget policies, while uniformly increasing the frame count does not reliably improve all metrics. These findings motivate fixed-mean frame-budget routing, a controlled inference setting in which each question may receive a different frame budget while the average budget matches a uniform reference. We introduce FrameRouter, a training-free test-time framework that combines an evidence-demand router, a budget-constrained allocation rule, and a frozen bank of frame-sampling policies. By controlling how much visual evidence each question receives while leaving the underlying samplers unchanged, FrameRouter is orthogonal to query-aware frame selection. Across Video-MME-v2 and several long-video understanding benchmarks, FrameRouter improves long-video question answering by using the same average visual budget more selectively across questions. Code is available at https://github.com/bingjunluo/FrameRouter.

Safety benchmarks for computer-use agents (CUAs) place the harm either in the intent of the task or on a single screen: a malicious instruction, an injected command, a phishing page. An agent that checks the task and each screen in isolation can pass them, so they do not test whether it combines facts seen on different screens. Yet in delegated work, harm often arises exactly this way: a fact about a person on one screen, a fact about an item on another, and a legitimate task that binds them. We introduce SEAM (Safety Execution Across Multiple screens), a benchmark of such cross-screen compositional harm: 600 multi-step, cross-application OSWorld tasks in which the instruction and every single observation are benign and only their combination is harmful. Minimal-pair variants that differ in one place, scored from the server log rather than by an LLM judge, locate where an agent fails. General-purpose open-weight and flagship agents carry out the harm in up to 92% of runs, yet decline when the user states it in one sentence. Agents open the decisive pages, so exposure is not the primary bottleneck. Asked about those pages, they flag the harm, and with thinking they often write the conflict into their reasoning and confirm anyway. Safety recognition thus does not reliably translate into safe execution, and the translation depends on the information channel: the same warning stops agents far more often in the user's instruction than on screen. Proprietary models are markedly more cautious. We release SEAM with its generator and quality gates at https://yuxuan2003.github.io/SEAM/ .

Part and prototype-based explanations for Vision Transformers (ViTs) present a decision as evidence from individual image regions. However, self-attention mixes information across patches, so part-level features absorb context from the whole image and consequently fail to faithfully reflect the decision. We show that existing interpretable ViT classifiers depend heavily on this leakage: when each explained region is encoded in isolation, their accuracy collapses, and retraining them under isolation recovers only part of the lost accuracy. We propose a classifier that represents each image as a graph of isolated parts. Each part is encoded only from its own patches by a ViT whose last blocks are fine-tuned under isolation, preventing evidence leakage from elsewhere in the image. Parts are discovered without annotation and refined hierarchically through their sub-parts, making them more consistent and stable across images than those of competing methods. Each edge is computed from part pairs, and recovers evidence that attention previously shared across the object without affecting the parts. Matching the image graph to a learned prototype graph per class makes the prediction a direct combination of node and edge similarities, each computed only from the patches it is credited to, so every explanation is faithful by construction. Across 11 fine-grained and general-object benchmarks, our method exceeds the accuracy of existing explainable methods that rely on leakage, while providing explanations that reflect what the model actually uses.

Semantic communication (SC) systems transmit continuous latent representations of meaning, but computing the semantic encoder at resource-constrained edge devices incurs substantial energy and memory costs. To address this challenge, we propose an end-to-end SC framework that executes the semantic encoder in the client's RF receiver front end using analog RF computing. A base station (BS) broadcasts encoder weights as RF waveforms, while each edge device reuses its receiver's passive mixer to compute semantic latents at ultra-low energy. These latents are transmitted over the uplink for network-side task inference. We show that analog computing noise across encoder layers and channel noise affecting transmitted latents admit a unified mathematical model. We define a task-posterior semantic distortion metric and derive its second-order decomposition into per-client, per-stage semantic sensitivities. Using this decomposition, we obtain a closed-form semantic-aware water-filling allocation of the total client energy budget across clients, encoder layers, and uplinks, assigning tighter accuracy targets to semantically sensitive stages. This allocation induces a broadcast beamforming problem that accounts for client-specific, per-layer accuracy requirements. We further refine the allocation through stochastic optimization and jointly design it with multi-task noise-aware training of the shared semantic model. MNIST simulations show that, at matched total client energy, the proposed allocation reduces network semantic distortion by 9.2\%-19.2\% compared with uniform precision and 5.1\%-13.1\% compared with client-blind allocation. ### Anticipating or Speculating? Making Futures Pay in AI Ethics
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22268) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22268) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22268) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22268)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Philosophers have recently been trying to develop uniform decision rules for instances in which physicians' judgments diverge from the recommendations of medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. I argue that this effort is fatally flawed because the performance and appropriate use of algorithmic recommendation systems by physicians are highly context-specific, empirical medical matters rather than epistemological ones. In such instances, general decision rules can be stipulated only to the extent that they abstract away from relevant, indeed determinative, contextual circumstances. The alleged problem of physician-AI disagreement, and others like it, play into longstanding disputes in technology ethics about the viability and utility of envisioning possible futures: is this useful "anticipation" or wasteful "speculation"? I suggest that the two may be distinguished. Anticipation explores plausible futures to help inform present actions under irreducible uncertainty. Speculation abstracts away uncertainty, developing formal solutions to stipulated circumstances whose relationship to the real present is often quite unclear. Efforts to develop crisp philosophical solutions to physician-AI disagreement, among other problems, fall into the latter category. Technology ethics should remember that fruitful futures thinking must both begin from and return to the messy, uncertain present.

Extensive experiments uphold the potency of our paradigm with it performing approximately 65\% better than FedEraser in terms of time, server storage load, and unlearning fidelity while utterly outperforming baseline full retraining.

The shortcut gap is accuracy when the cue is right minus accuracy when it is wrong. For Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-5.5 and Gemini 3.6 Flash at low reasoning effort it is +18 to +37 percentage points. Central pattern generators (CPGs) provide a biologically inspired foundation for adaptive locomotor control, yet systematically characterizing their dynamic regimes in spiking neural networks remains challenging. A composite synchronization metric is developed, integrating phase coherence, amplitude balance, and temporal stability. Its performance is evaluated across systematically varied stimulation waveforms and synaptic configurations. Statistical analysis reveals that stimulation type dominates regime determination, with tonic drive yielding the most robust antiphase synchronization. Periodic inputs induce nonlinear saturation and context-dependent network routing. Although synaptic weights alone show limited discriminative power, they significantly modulate robustness within a given stimulation context, with reciprocal Ia interneuron connections emerging as key stabilizers. Speculative-diffusion decoding uses a small parallel drafter model to generate b draft tokens in one forward pass, which then undergo parallel verification via a larger target model. Faster inference can be achieved when multiple draft tokens pass verification simultaneously. Recent parallel draft architectures (DFlash, DSpark) improve draft quality by conditioning on rich features computed by the target, through a mechanism known as feature encoding. However, standard feature encoding mechanisms have three limitations: (1) they are lossy, applying encoding projections that are destructive to the underlying target feature-space via a discovered effect known as residual mixing, (2) they are memory-less, with no record of previously processed proposals, and (3) they utilize features sampled uniformly from across the target layers, omitting important hidden states. To address these issues, we propose Mixture-of-Features Attention (MoFA), a lightweight feature-encoding approach equipped with a working memory that encodes previously rejected proposals. Leveraging MoFA, rejection memory, and a set of further enhancements, including 2-tap causal convolutions, prefix-only cross-attention, and non-uniform target layer selection, the final architecture, DNeo, achieves 1.3x faster generation speed than SoTA (DFlash-2) without impacting drafter footprint or generation quality. DNeo marks a new state of the art in high quality, fast LLM inference.

Aggregating observations into a limited number of latent tokens and learning physical interactions in the latent space has emerged as an effective approach for Transformer-based neural operators to solve Partial Differential Equation (PDE) problems involving large-scale observations. To address this limitation, we propose PArallel Latent Space Neural Operator (PANO), a novel neural operator for solving PDEs that formulates physical interactions among observations as an additive combination of interaction results from parallel latent spaces with shared parameters.
