Science advances from frontier to frontier as discoveries reshape what is known and create the basis for further discovery. We present Colombo, a self-organizing AI researcher that drives this renewal, first instantiated in oracle-based optimization. Here, the central question is how efficiently a problem class can be solved through a prescribed information interface and what costs no admissible algorithm can avoid. The gap between achievable guarantees and unavoidable limits defines the frontier recorded in its Complexity Atlas, a living map of frontier settings and the current state of knowledge. Self-Organization turns unresolved gaps into adaptive research programmes, coordinating conjecture formation, mathematical investigation, and replanning as evidence accumulates. Adversarial Co-Ascent develops algorithms and hard families through reciprocal challenge, exposing mechanisms of information use and concealment that guide stronger methods and proofs of unavoidable costs. Independent review and Lean-based formal certification check mathematical claims, while the Long-Term Memory carries supported findings and research experience across settings. To date, Colombo has established optimal or near-optimal rates for more than fifty settings in the Atlas. Colombo thus takes a concrete step toward Autopoietic Science: a scientific process whose validated advances regenerate the conditions of its own progress, continually reshaping how inquiry is organized and what it can discover.

In data streams, labels often arrive late or only for some instances, class prevalence drifts, and rare classes vanish from short windows. Standard evaluation then confuses a degraded decision boundary with a change in prevalence, and is biased when labels are delayed or selected by the model. We extend an algebraic framework that obtains pooled (micro) and per-class (macro) measures from one confusion matrix to streams, weighting the counts in time by exponential fading or a sliding window; the update costs O(m^2) per step, whatever the stream length, and each pooled measure stays a weighted combination of the per-class ones. Delayed and selective feedback enter as an observation mask: until its label arrives, an instance is counted with the model's calibrated class probabilities, and the bias of this interim estimate is driven by their calibration error. Audited conventions report no value for a class without verified instances. We separate prior shift from concept drift by monitoring prior-free coordinates (recall and specificity) and attribute a change in a measure to the two sources with a first-order Jacobian decomposition. On synthetic streams and four real datasets, a detector on these coordinates with a memory per class raises no excess false alarms under label shift, unlike the Jacobian alarm and error-rate detectors. The correction for delayed labels recovers recall in some real settings and is only partial for precision, specificity and F1. Twenty-five statements are checked in Lean 4, and the code is in the aggmetrics package.

### ARC-2: Continuous Compliance Through Delta Management

ARC 1 introduced a three-layer governance architecture for autonomous systems comprising model safety validation, cognitive certification (CRAB), and operational authorization (LEA). However, it did not address how compliance should be maintained after deployment. This paper introduces Delta Management, a continuous governance framework that measures deviations between a system's current operational state and its certified baseline, defined as a region rather than a fixed point. Compliance is assessed through continuous monitoring instead of static certification. Four extensions are proposed. First, CRAB evaluation incorporates the worst verified behavioral deviations, with isolated outliers quarantined pending confirmation to prevent unjustified restrictions. Second, a layered trigger taxonomy distinguishes hard safety violations, critical single-dimension failures, correlated multi-system degradation, and risk-weighted composite indicators, each triggering proportionate responses. Third, a graduated safe transition protocol separates withdrawal of operational authorization from physical shutdown, consistent with established safety standards and EU AI Act requirements. Fourth, environmental delta is introduced as a distinct baseline validity condition: environmental drift beyond the certified Operational Design Domain (ODD) triggers recertification or ODD revision rather than automatic authority contraction. Together, these extensions transform ARC from a deployment-time certification architecture into a continuous framework for monitoring, evaluating, and enforcing compliance throughout the operational lifecycle of autonomous systems. The framework addresses the continuous compliance obligations established by EU AI Act Article 72(2).

This paper presents the design and implementation of Auriga Robotics' mobile manipulator robot, developed at the Robotics and Intelligent Automation Laboratory, Department of Electrical Engineering, Shahid Beheshti University. Hereafter, this platform is referred to as the @Work Robot. The robot targets industrial tasks, with an emphasis on improving efficiency in repetitive or hazardous work environments. It uses a 4-wheel Mecanum drive for omnidirectional movement and a 5-degree-of-freedom manipulator arm with a 3D-printed gripper for object handling and navigation. [arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22094) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22094) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22094) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22094)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) require visual encoders that extract fine-grained visual features while constructing semantically disentangled and discriminative representations. Conventional methodologies encounter a fundamental trade-off: contrastive learning enhances inter-class discrimination by suppressing intra-class variability, whereas generative reconstruction emphasizes perceptual fidelity but lacks explicit comparative supervision. The naive aggregation of these objectives via composite loss functions often induces optimization conflicts and instability due to gradient interference within the shared latent space. To resolve this, we introduce a unified representation-learning paradigm that reformulates generative reconstruction as a discriminative ranking problem within the generative output manifold. Our approach maintains perceptual reconstruction as a supervisory signal and integrates a ranking-based discriminative constraint by systematically ordering matched and mismatched reconstruction outputs. This formulation embeds discriminative information seamlessly into the generative process, alleviating the risk of loss competition. Specifically, we propose AUC Generative Reconstruction (AUCGR), which optimizes the area under the ROC curve (AUC) by ensuring higher scores for matched pairs than for mismatched pairs, and Partial AUC Generative Reconstruction (pAUCGR), which addresses supervision bias by focusing on mismatched comparisons within reliable feature subspaces. The proposed objectives are designed to be plug-and-play, requiring no architectural modifications to existing generative reconstruction frameworks. Theoretical analysis establishes rigorous connections to AUC/pAUC optimization and provides generalization guarantees. Extensive experiments validate that our method advances both discriminative accuracy and fine-grained perceptual representation.

An expert who corrects a concept prediction, such as a BI-RADS descriptor in breast ultrasound, expects the diagnosis to improve. Objectives that reduce the harm of such corrections are judged by harm and accuracy, yet harm also falls when a model responds less to every correction or starts from a worse uncorrected prediction, the reference for harm. We propose a correction audit, a reproducible evaluation protocol that separates these explanations: it splits each correction into harm and useful gain on identical events, separates the starting prediction from the correction response, and compares objectives with amplitude baselines matched in source harm. On three breast-ultrasound sources, worst-case correction training, evaluated from the mean-case model's start, lowers harm but gives up more useful gain in all 21 source comparisons pooled over six seeds, raising the corrected negative log-likelihood (NLL); from the same start, selecting between the objectives by harm picks the less helpful model on all three sources. On CUB, a monotonic harm penalty at its reported weight lowers path-level harm in all 80 concept sets while raising the NLL in all 80, almost entirely before any correction. At a fixed start, rescaling both responses to the same source harm reproduces the penalty's mean harm, useful gain and NLL on single-concept corrections within a prespecified margin of 0.0005 nat, although the two responses point in different directions. Lower harm therefore signals better corrections only when it is read together with useful gain, absolute risk and the start it is measured from.

### Hierarchical Diffusion Transformer for End-to-End Image Generation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22962v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22962v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22962v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22962v1)

2026/11/17 15:47 GTM

Pixel-space diffusion learns visual representations and image generation jointly in a single end-to-end training stage, without a separately pretrained visual encoder. However, plain pixel-space Transformers still trail leading two-stage latent diffusion models in generation quality and training efficiency. We hypothesize that part of this gap arises from retaining one token per patch throughout the backbone, which does not explicitly encourage a visual hierarchy that summarizes related content at progressively coarser levels. To address this, we introduce Hierarchical Diffusion Transformers (HiT), which progressively group related tokens and represent each group with a single token. The main Transformer bottleneck processes these compact group tokens, and ungrouping restores features to their original image positions. A compression budget regulates the average compression ratio across the batch, allowing group memberships and counts to vary across images and diffusion timesteps during both training and generation. The model exhibits emergent semantic structure, learning to group coherent objects and object parts without segmentation labels. In text-to-image generation, HiT further learns to group caption words with image patches depicting the same concepts, without region annotations. HiT achieves 1.48 FID on ImageNet $256\times256$ and reaches comparable generation quality to JiT using approximately 40\% of its cumulative training FLOPs. For text-to-image generation, HiT exceeds UniLiP's average score across five benchmarks with roughly $6\times$ fewer denoiser parameters and $3\times$ fewer forward FLOPs. These results suggest that learning a visual hierarchy within the generator breaks down the barrier to stronger image generation without external visual pretraining.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21978) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21978) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21978) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21978)

Large language models are moving from isolated assistants toward autonomous agents that can plan, use tools, and coordinate with others, motivating an emerging Agentic Web of independently developed agents and services. Realizing this vision requires agent behavior to move across creators, hosts, and runtimes without coupling publication to a particular execution environment or transferring authority over user resources. We introduce WebAgents, which unbundles these roles through a signed agent.pkg that carries portable workflows, skills, schemas, and resource requirements. An OpenClaw-based agent-native browser/runtime verifies the package, obtains authorization, and binds it at run time to user-controlled models, tools, permissions, and budgets, while hosts distribute the public program and may retain private services server-side. We demonstrate this contract with three unrelated workflows executed unchanged under local and API-backed models, including literature review with live services and a website-published travel agent using semantic tools and user-selected personal resources. In a controlled suite, WebAgents explicitly blocks all 716 injected contract violations while accepting all 295 matched benign controls, with median automated pre-execution processing below 0.5 s for all three packages. We conclude by outlining a path toward NANDA-based discovery and interoperable package ecosystems while retaining execution authority in the user's runtime.

On the public TU Delft digital substation and MSU/ORNL power system attack benchmarks, we compare the DQN with fixed subsets and orders, calibrated entropy stopping, a greedy value of information rule, and tree ensembles trained per subset. Selective acquisition can pay off, but on these benchmarks it does not need a learned policy.

### Towards Physics-Constrained Rectified Flow for Climate Downscaling: What Projections Can Certify?
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22283) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22283) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22283) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22283)

Statistical downscaling restores the regional detail behind flooding and crop stress, but diffusion and flow-matching generators can produce sharp fields that break the conservation laws of their coarse input. Physics-Constrained Rectified Flow (PC-RF) is a conditional flow-matching downscaler. Its training penalties target wind divergence, negative precipitation, and mass drift from the coarse total, and a projection after sampling removes the last two exactly. On one corrupted synthetic field with no trained network, the projection drives the Non-negativity Violation Rate (NVR) from 0.236 and the Mass-Conservation Error (MCE) from 0.184 to zero, and the penalties lower the Divergence Error (DE) by about 30%. On ERA5 the projection gives PC-RF and RF-base, the penalty-free rectified-flow baseline, an NVR of exactly zero and an MCE of zero up to float32 rounding. The penalties add no perceptual gain that we can separate from training budget or run-to-run variation. At a matched budget on 2019-2021, PC-RF has no edge over RF-base (SSIM 0.939 vs. 0.942, PSNR 31.81 vs. 31.79, CRPS 0.771 vs. 0.772). Our unprojected CorrDiff reimplementation beats both flow models on PSNR (28.38) and CRPS (1.092) on 2020, yet 45% of its ensemble-mean precipitation cells are negative. On ERA5 a sign error in the divergence operator made that penalty and DE act on a deformation-like term. The fine reference is interpolated from ERA5 and accumulates precipitation over a longer window, so the perceptual scores cannot establish downscaling skill. NVR and MCE need one pass and no fine reference, so we argue they should run first as pass-or-fail checks, with DE as a diagnostic.

### SeasonRainBench: Global Seasonal Precipitation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22281) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22281) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22281) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22281)

Accurate seasonal precipitation forecasts help societies prepare for drought, flooding, and crop failure months in advance. While data-driven methods have made rapid progress in weather and subseasonal forecasting, operational seasonal precipitation prediction still relies on numerical ensemble systems. These systems are computationally expensive and exhibit biases and drift that increase with lead time, motivating more efficient data-driven alternatives. Yet this transition faces three linked challenges: comparing data-driven forecasts with operational ones requires (1) a global benchmark that does not exist, scoring them requires (2) metrics that capture operational priorities beyond anomaly correlation (ACC) and RMSE, and training them must (3) rely on a single, short observational record. To address the first two challenges, we introduce \BenchName, the first such benchmark, covering monthly precipitation forecasts 1--5 months ahead. It pairs global $1^\circ$ satellite precipitation with oceanic and atmospheric predictors, includes seven operational centers, and provides 310,944 simulated months from 52 climate models for training. Beyond standard scores, we introduce two operationally motivated metrics for anomaly strength and monthly rain-belt position. To address the third challenge, we develop \MODEL, which learns from simulations what the record lacks and transfers it to Earth through three modules: SimForced aligns the climate background, SimMemory transfers a history encoder pretrained on simulations, and SimAnalog supplements Earth labels with simulated analog outcomes. Extensive experiments show that \MODEL{} improves ACC, CRPS, anomaly strength, and rain-belt position by 12.0\%, 3.6\%, 2.9\%, and 0.3\% over the strongest operational or data-driven model, opening a path from simulated worlds to Earth system prediction.

When a twelve-lead electrocardiogram is written as text for a large language model, the number of tokens has to be balanced against the information lost through compression. This paper presents a deterministic encoding that writes eight independent leads as amplitude letters and exact time gaps and recovers the remaining four limb leads algebraically. The key points are selected with a cardiac-cycle-dependent tolerance, the amplitudes are parameterized by a 26-letter logarithmic codebook learned from a training set, and a 10-step tolerance schedule keeps all records within a fixed token budget. The encoding was applied to 21,388 records from the public PTB-XL database and evaluated using an exact decode-and-re-encode round trip, waveform reconstruction error, and a paired comparison of convolutional classifiers trained with 5 different seeds on signals from the original and decoded databases. All records were contained in 4,080 tokens (average: 2,866 tokens, 64.3% fewer than a per-sample transcription), and all stored lead strings were exactly reproduced. The average correlation between the decoded and reference signals was 0.982. The macro-averaged area under the receiver operating characteristic curve was 0.9167 for the reference signals and 0.9157 for the decoded signals (paired difference -0.0010; 95% patient-level bootstrap interval, -0.0028 to 0.0007), within the pre-specified non-inferiority margin of -0.010; this is not a confirmatory result, because the test fold had been used during encoder development. The diagnostic content of a compact text can therefore be preserved, while the language model's ability to read the text is yet another matter.

### Fragmented Markers, Mixed Results: A Systematic Review of AI Coding Assistants and Developer Productivity
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21632) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21632) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21632) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21632)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

AI coding assistants are increasingly embedded in software engineering practice, yet the literature still offers no cumulative, easily interpretable picture of their effects on software developer productivity. The central problem is not a lack of studies, but a lack of comparability: prior work operationalizes productivity through heterogeneous markers, settings, and reporting styles, making cross-study conclusions difficult. To address this, we conduct a staged systematic literature review. In the first stage, we synthesize studies that explicitly examine developer productivity. In the second, we extend the scope to productivity-related developer outcomes using SPACE as a theory-informed lens, capturing human-centered evidence that explicit productivity studies often omit. Across both stages, we analyze how productivity is operationalized, which confounding factors are discussed, and how reported effects are distributed. We identify 187 unique productivity and productivity-related markers, while individual studies typically only use five, revealing a broad but highly fragmented evidence base. Explicit productivity research is dominated by time-, output-, and artifact-oriented proxies, whereas the broader SPACE-aligned corpus makes satisfaction, psychological aspects, and well-being substantially more visible. Reported effects are often positive, but this pattern is stronger in perceived and tendency-based findings than in objective or statistically evaluated ones. Overall, the literature suggests that AI coding assistants can support productivity, but current evidence does not yet support simple, context-independent conclusions. The key contribution of this review is therefore a structured basis for making productivity evidence more interpretable, multidimensional, and comparable across studies.

Recent advances in text-to-image generation have substantially improved image quality, particularly through diffusion-based methods that require multiple inference steps. We further observe that improved reconstruction fidelity does not necessarily yield better generation quality. AI systems increasingly support literature analysis, candidate evaluation and hypothesis refinement, but enabling researchers to explore research directions beyond established disciplinary approaches remains challenging. We developed Abelio, an AI system designed to generate novel research directions by expanding investigators' avenues of inquiry. Abelio reformulates research objectives, uses these alternative formulations to identify relevant elements outside conventional disciplinary boundaries, and refines the resulting hypotheses. Each stage integrates divergent exploration with convergent selection through a dedicated subsystem. In quantitative evaluations, proposals generated by the Ideation Core (Abelio's hypothesis-generation core) outperformed those of four frontier large language models in scientific soundness and hypothesis diversity, and three of four in distance from existing literature. Subsystem ablation showed that each subsystem contributes to the property it targets, with the contribution of Element Selection measurable before Refinement. To investigate intestinal epithelial barrier restoration, Abelio proposed physical interventions involving membrane-tethered poly(ethylene glycol) (PEG) and cholesterol-anchored complementary DNA. Both proposals were archived before experimentation and subsequently tested independently in damaged Caco-2 monolayers. PEG preserved barrier function in a membrane-surface-dependent manner, whereas complementary DNA enabled sequence-specific restoration that was reversible on demand. These findings demonstrate how structured research ideation can identify experimentally testable approaches beyond established disciplinary conventions.

The regulation of Artificial Intelligence is susceptible to various forms of capture by industry actors that hold undue influence over the policymaking process, due in part to their information advantage in developing these technologies. In the following paper, we investigate the roles of public commenters in providing evidence to potential regulation in the USA. We build a corpus of 40 potential regulations, and 2,664 public comments submitted by a variety of stakeholders. We computationally process these texts to extract evidence meant to inform regulators, in the form of formal citations and informal references. We find significant differences in the type and amount of evidence supplied: Holding comment length constant, business commenters introduce altogether less evidence, and government, university, and civil society commenters provide significantly more research and legal sources. The publication status of cited research is nearly evenly split, with preprints and peer-reviewed articles being introduced by commenters of all types. We further explore patterns of self-citation and find that while it is prevalent amongst all groups, a small cluster of organizations account for over half of all self-citations in our corpus. Our findings complicate a narrative that public comments alone are means to information capture. We interpret these results in terms of the role that commenters attempt to play in the policymaking process-being either an Issue Advocate or an Honest Broker. These findings on the supply of evidence in public comments shed light on an often overlooked form of policymaking that has implications for how and through what sources information is made accessible to regulators of AI.

Precipitation nowcasting models suffer from errors in intensity, location, and spatial structure. Activation steering is a simple, training-free way to modulate LLM behavior, yet existing methods typically inject vectors at a single layer and lack mechanisms for depth-aware propagation or internal verification. We combine two recent ideas - Attention Residuals (AttnRes) providing learnable cross-layer pathways, and Jacobian-lens (J-space) concept readout - into a lightweight depth-aware steering pipeline. On two small open models (Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3.5-0.8B), distributing a persona vector according to AttnRes-style depth weights yields stronger internal alignment (deeper-layer concept cosine and rank, p<0.0001) and stronger behavioral shifts than single-layer injection. The effect is sign-specific: reverse vectors invert all six metrics, random directions degrade alignment (p<0.0001), and a short LoRA fine-tune erases the imprint. Injection rewires attention strictly downstream of the injection point (upstream JS = 0.00000). Three practical findings follow. First, a layer-wise geometric decomposition of cosine readouts: at the injection layer 123% of the increase is arithmetic, while 5-11 layers downstream 100% is dynamical. Second, an intensity window: effects are monotone over 0.3-0.7, then generation collapses (617 to 306 to 25 chars). Third, metric discipline: keyword-rule metrics fail LLM-judge validation (kappa <= 0.09), and two rule-based conclusions were retracted. Training the depth weights on a steering objective (200 steps, frozen backbone) raises final alignment 10x on held-out prompts. Cross-persona vectors retain 96-99% of the effect: specificity holds against random, not semantic, directions. We contribute a practical, low-overhead depth-aware steering method with a careful evaluation protocol. Code and data are released.

We also investigate the interaction between various impairments in training and inference, demonstrating that visually demanding distractions are separable from cognitive distractions. ### From Top to Bottom: Quark Mass Hierarchies in Heterotic String Theory
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21751) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21751) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21751) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21751)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Standard Model fermion masses exhibit large hierarchies, spanning more than five orders of magnitude in the quark sector alone. In this paper, we study whether these hierarchies can be generated perturbatively at tree level in smooth heterotic Calabi-Yau compactifications, where the supergravity approximation remains under computational control. In such backgrounds, physical Yukawa couplings combine holomorphic superpotential integrals with matter field Kahler metrics. The former can be computed algebraically, while the latter require solving a coupled system of partial differential equations for the Ricci-flat metric, Hermitian Yang-Mills connections, and harmonic bundle-valued forms. Within this framework, we identify two new tree-level mechanisms by which complex structure moduli can generate fermion mass hierarchies and show analytically and numerically that both mechanisms are obstructed for previously studied tetra-quadric models. Motivated by these obstructions, we scan the existing databases of tens of thousands of complete intersection Calabi-Yau line bundle models and find a unique smooth quotient admitting a rank-three up-quark Yukawa matrix at tree level. Evaluating the physical couplings on this manifold and evolving them under the renormalization group, we obtain realistic mass hierarchies for the top, charm, up, and bottom quarks in the smooth supergravity regime.

Language models are increasingly being used as agents capable of planning and executing projects, as well as refining their actions through feedback. The best-performing agent achieves a mean score of 57.38, indicating that current agents still struggle with realistic scientific workflows. Our failure analysis identifies three recurring weaknesses at the workflow level: difficulty translating scientific knowledge into task-specific procedures, weak integration of evidence across different modalities, and limited capacity for scientific self-correction. These findings highlight the importance of workflow-based evaluation for measuring progress toward reliable AI systems for scientific research.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21937) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21937) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21937) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21937)

Modern sensing systems generate large volumes of heterogeneous data streams from multiple sensor modalities, increasing reliance on datacenters for computation, artificial intelligence/superintelligence (AI/SI) inference, and AI/SI training. Each sensor stream may have different network and compute requirements depending on its type and data volume. However, current wireless systems typically allocate resources at the device level, leading to contention among co-located sensor streams, where bulk data transfers may starve latency-critical telemetry. To this end, this paper introduces Sensor Slicing (SL). SL provides deterministic, medium access control (MAC)-level isolation for individual sensors within a single user equipment (UE) in open radio access networks (O-RAN). Using agentic coding techniques, SL is implemented in two O-RAN-compliant packages, reducing development time by 400 times (2 h 40 min [OCUDU] vs. 6.5 weeks [OAI]), 228-fold fewer human prompts (4 vs. 913), and less than half the tokens (0.64B vs. 1.43B). An associated end-to-end framework, SLED, is implemented to establish per-sensor radio and network isolation across edge, campus, and remote high-performance computing (HPC) tiers. Over-the-air wireless experiments demonstrate that while conventional network slicing causes critical delays to increase more than 2,300-fold under high traffic load, SLED maintains stable latency. Scaling evaluations on the two RAN stacks show that throughput remains within 0.9% and 1.4% of expected allocations even as the number of sensor slices increases. The results demonstrate that SLED preserves latency-critical telemetry while effectively utilizing resources across the sensor-to-HPC pipeline. The SLED software and evaluation datasets are publicly available at https://github.com/UNL-CPN-Lab/SLED-Sensor-SLicing-from-Edge-to-Datacenter.

### Retrieval-Augmented Correction for Generalist Medical Image Segmentation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23189v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23189v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23189v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23189v1)

2026/11/17 18:33 GTM

Automated segmentation is routinely used as the first step in quantitative image-based clinical workflows, where predicted masks are subsequently reviewed and corrected by human operators. Automated correction of the residual structural errors is currently addressed one anatomy at a time, so the number of trained correctors scales with the number of segmentation targets. We present GRACE, a retrieval-augmented refiner that edits an input mask under context drawn from a shared knowledge base of verified prior cases. The refiner is trained once on a multi-organ corpus spanning compact organs, cavities, branching vessels, and tumour lesions. In the held-out dataset-transfer evaluation, three held-out datasets query a fixed knowledge base built from training data only. A separate leave-one-anatomy-out experiment tests parameter-free index extension: verified cases of an unseen solid organ enter the knowledge base, and the refiner stays frozen. A light shape-aware projection head on a frozen multimodal encoder maps embedding similarity to the shape agreement of the masks, so the knowledge base returns cases that the refiner can use as a shape prior. We evaluate GRACE on 18 public datasets spanning CT, MRI and ultrasound, using nine refinement configurations and seven upstream segmentors that span weak, mid, and strong operating regimes. We report each change with the DSC of its input mask. On the weak tier, the input DSC is 0.386 and GRACE adds up to +0.35 DSC. On a strong segmentor, the input DSC is 0.932 and the mean change is small. Transfer to an imaging modality that is absent from training and from the index is partial and depends on the anatomy, and remains an open research direction. Our results suggest retrieval-conditioned refinement is a practical alternative to anatomy-specific correction models. Code: https://github.com/robustml-eurecom/grace

Building change detection (BCD) relies on dense annotations that are costly to collect across regions. A few annotated examples offer a more economical way to specify the target, but change maps provide incomplete building supervision: they label only changed buildings and leave the building-containing date unspecified. Our key observation is that these annotations nevertheless reveal features that recognize buildings in other images, including unchanged ones. We present Linear INduced Discriminants (LIND), a training-free framework that turns regional feature contrasts into a reusable linear building classifier. Using a single frozen vision transformer, it unifies building recognition and temporal comparison in the same feature space. This approach requires neither text prompts nor an auxiliary segmentation model. Only one parameter is tuned on a single validation dataset and then kept fixed across datasets and shot settings. Extensive experiments demonstrate strong detection accuracy, cross-dataset transfer, and efficient inference, with extensions to other semantic classes and broader land-cover changes. Change annotations thus provide a compact visual specification for detection in new scenes. Code: https://github.com/lzcn/lind

### AdaFlowLite: Scalable and Non-blocking Inference on Asynchronous Mobile Data

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

The rise of mobile devices equipped with numerous sensors, such as LiDAR and cameras, has driven the adoption of multi-modal deep intelligence for distributed sensing tasks, such as smart cabins and driving assistance. However, the arrival time of mobile sensory data vary due to modality size and network dynamics, which can lead to delays (if waiting for slow data) or accuracy decline (if inference proceeds without waiting). Moreover, the diversity and dynamic nature of mobile systems exacerbate this challenge. In response, we present a shift to \textit{opportunistic} inference for asynchronous distributed multi-modal data, enabling inference as soon as partial data arrives. While existing methods focus on optimizing modality consistency and complementarity, known as modal affinity, they lack a \textit{computational} approach to control this affinity in open-world mobile environments. % \sysname pioneers the formulation of structured cross-modality affinity in mobile contexts using a hierarchical analysis-based normalized matrix. This approach accommodates the diversity and dynamics of modalities, generalizing across different types and numbers of inputs. Employing an multi-modal lightweight Swin Transformer (MMLST), \sysname facilitates real-time and flexible data imputation, adapting to various modalities and downstream tasks without retraining. Experiments show that \sysname significantly reduces inference latency by up to 80.4\% and enhances accuracy by up to 62.1\%, while achieving nearly a 50\% reduction in energy consumption, outperforming status quo approaches. Also, this method can enhance LLM performance to preprocess asynchronous data.

### Big Brains, Tiny Board: Pupavision, an Agentic Physical AI Instrument for Monitoring Drosophila Pupariation and Lifecycle Transitions
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22136) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22136) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22136) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22136)

Many biology experiments are limited by the availability and ease of use of instruments that monitor and record them. For example, the timing of pupariation in the fruit fly Drosophila melanogaster, a widely used model organism, can provide biologically relevant information about growth regulation and is often recorded manually. Here, we present Pupavision, a small physical AI instrument built from a single-board Linux computer and a day/night camera for roughly 45 to 75 US dollars in parts. The key idea is that a frontier coding agent runs its agentic harness directly on the tiny board. The model thinks in the cloud, but the agent works on the device. The agent assists image capture, writes code to repair errors, and checks the experiment every day to keep raw data capture reliable. If recording stops, the researcher gets a ping on their mobile phone. A simple deterministic capture service records one image every ten minutes and never depends on the agent. We report a 26-day pilot in a fly incubator (about 3,580 frames), including eleven days in which the agent was logged out and capture continued, and a 51-hour outage whose cause led to the layered design. A researcher-in-the-loop workflow then produces per-vial pupariation curves by tracing selected pupae backward in time from a late reference frame. Future work will validate the assay for lifecycle tracking accuracy against expert-labeled data and expand real-time computer vision analysis. Pupavision offers a blueprint for do-it-yourself agentic instruments that put frontier intelligence on a tiny board, with deterministic code underneath. We present this as an early case study from a pilot lab deployment and contribute lessons learned toward a future where scientific domain experts are empowered by DIY agentic hardware.

Whole-slide visual question answering (VQA) offers a natural-language interface to pathology, allowing diagnostic and phenotypic questions to be answered directly from tissue slides. However, current vision-language models (VLMs) rely heavily on linguistic priors: their answers are sensitive to the order of the candidate answers, and can be reached through shortcuts that bypass the tissue evidence. To address these challenges, we introduce SlideJev, a framework that separately verifies candidate answers against the slide and learns to ground these judgments in tissue evidence. To connect parallel candidate evaluation with visual evidence learning, we adopt Jev and score each candidate by the difference between support and rejection log-likelihoods from the original language-model head. We compute these scores in parallel using separate prompts with the same format and no option-position labels, then normalize them for answer selection. To ground these scores in tissue evidence, candidate-conditioned reading and comparison guide gated updates to visual tokens across decoder layers. With the backbone frozen, we train these visual modules through the same scores using question-answer and paired-image supervision. Across three whole-slide pathology VQA benchmarks, SlideJev achieves the highest mean overall accuracy and 100% answer consistency across four option orders. Image-control experiments further show that SlideJev achieves the largest average accuracy gains from correct images over mismatched and image-free inputs across the three datasets. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/leowu-ai/SlideJev.

### Distortion-Free Semantic Watermarks for Black-Box Generative Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23208v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23208v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23208v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23208v1)

2026/11/17 18:49 GTM

We present a provably distortion-free semantic watermarking algorithm that requires only black-box sampling access to a generative model and black-box access to a semantic embedding. A watermark is called distortion-free if, averaged over its secret detection key, it leaves the generator's output distribution unchanged; this provides a formal guarantee that watermarking does not reduce output quality. Conventional such watermarks are often brittle to transformations. This is especially problematic for images, where benign operations such as compression, cropping, or resizing change the entire pixel representation. Semantic watermarks therefore require detection to be robust to content-preserving transformations. Previous methods combining these properties rely heavily on specific white-box properties of the underlying model: either to autoregressive text generation or to diffusion-specific access, such as control of the initial noise or diffusion inversion. In contrast, our construction is fully generic: It only interacts with the generative model and the semantic embedding, a function that determines the notion of "preserving content" under which detection should be robust, via sample and query access. We then instantiate this framework for image generation. We characterize the two competing properties required of the semantic embedding: it should remain stable under transformations that preserve an image's content, but should distinguish independent generations from the same prompt. We train an image encoder directly for this tradeoff. The resulting encoder improves separation while maintaining stability over off-the-shelf alternatives and enables strong aggregate detection while remaining robust to various transformations. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our watermark scheme on both open-source and closed models, which is possible only due to restriction to black-box access.

Speech applications require decisions about term presence, turn completion, and language within an audio window. Language identification uses the ASR model's own language scores. On private conversational test sets, decoder AUROC is 0.80-0.92; on public read speech it is 0.92-0.97. Speech Commands four-way accuracy is 0.93; binary lexical ECE is at most 0.08. Layer-12 speaker readouts detect speaker change with AUROC 0.966-0.980 on AMI and VoxConverse, above an ECAPA baseline, and count speakers (0, 1, 2, or 3+) more accurately than pyannote 3.1. The turn-end readout reaches AUROC 0.930 versus Smart Turn v3.2 at 0.982 on Smart Turn's own test set. On a subset of ICSI meetings labeled by next speaker, AUROC is 0.716 versus 0.712; the paired confidence interval includes zero.

We present DARWIN, a geospatial foundation model for marine intelligence. Recent Earth-observation foundation models have concentrated on terrestrial domains. The marine realm poses distinct challenges: missingness, multi-modal physical and biogeochemical variables, sparse labels, and dynamics that are transient, multi-scale, and non stationary over time. DARWIN adapts a masked-autoencoder backbone to these properties through missingness-aware encoding, expanded spatial and temporal masking, and Fourier-style positional embeddings, and is pretrained on 26 years of global Copernicus reanalysis together with GEBCO bathymetry. On predicting chlorophyll-a, the pretrained backbone attains the lowest monthly relative error in every month of 2022, outperforming both no-encoder baselines and an identically architected random-init encoder. At 10% of the supervised training data, DARWIN already surpasses the random-init model trained on 100% of the data. Embedding analysis and qualitative reconstructions show that DARWIN captures coherent oceanographic structure rather than memorising input statistics, providing a scalable backbone for downstream marine applications such as anomaly detection, species distribution modelling, and environmental monitoring.

Animated 3D blockouts support previsualization and provide structural conditions for video generation, but manually reconstructing them from reference videos is costly. This limits both their use by creators and the production of paired training data. We introduce MAVEBench, a benchmark of 50 film shots and 416 keyframes for evaluating camera motion, spatial layout, and character blocking. We propose MAVE, a video-to-blockout framework combining Metric observation, Agentic modeling, and evidence-based VErification. It first recovers cameras, human motion, and geometric evidence in a shared metric coordinate system. An agent then constructs an editable environment under constraints from these observations, while programmatic checks report inconsistencies to guide refinement. The outputs are editable Blender scenes and frame-aligned blockout videos. On MAVEBench, MAVE improves overall scores by 27.5-41.1 points across three agents over their corresponding vanilla baselines. With DeepSeek-1017, it achieves a score of 73.2 at an average API cost of only USD 0.054 per shot, outperforming the strongest vanilla baseline while reducing total reconstruction time by 53%. We also construct MAVEData-3K, a dataset of 3,000 blockout-video pairs for model training, and will release it publicly. Identifying whom a person looks at is central to understanding multi-party conversations, yet gaze geometry alone becomes ambiguous as more people appear. Conversations offer cues that geometry lacks, such as who is speaking and what is said, but no gaze dataset provides them for many multi-party scenes. We introduce SORBET, a benchmark of 3,340 samples from 1,578 clips with synchronized speech, transcripts, and attended person labels, covering more multi-party scenes than existing gaze datasets. Prior gaze models struggle to use these cues, which come in different forms, must be linked to the right person, and can conflict with gaze geometry. We propose Nunchi, a framework that constructs a text prompt from the transcript and the outputs of gaze and speaker models, links every cue to the candidates with shared labels, and lets a vision-language model (VLM) weigh gaze geometry against conversational cues to identify the attended person. Nunchi outperforms the strongest existing method by 4.8 percentage points. On SORBET, conversational cues help whereas additional visual cues do not, and the gains hold across three VLMs, with automatic transcripts, and on two existing datasets.

Periapical radiographs (PRs) are central to endodontic diagnosis, but interpreting them and preparing reports are time-consuming. ### FMT: Lossless Image Compression in a Fixed Number of Parallel Steps
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23108v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23108v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23108v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23108v1)

2026/11/17 17:28 GTM

Learned lossless codecs make smaller files than common classical codecs, yet the images we archive and train on still use classical codecs. A main reason is time: a learned codec takes seconds to decode a photo that a classical codec opens in milliseconds, and their faster variants give up most of their gain in size. We argue that much of this time is spent waiting, not computing: the network waits for the values it conditions on, and the arithmetic decoder reads them one at a time. We introduce FMT (Fixed-step Multiscale Tree), a lossless image format whose decoding order and entropy coder are designed together so that neither wait grows with the image. FMT stores an image as a coarse-to-fine tree whose steps hold independent values: a GPU predicts each step at once and decodes it in parallel rANS lanes that carry their states from step to step, so an image of any size decodes in a fixed number of sequential steps. On this tree we build a family of bit-exact codecs, from a rule with no network to large networks, and no codec we ran on the same GPU beats any of them on both size and speed. Without a network, FMT beats NVIDIA's HTJ2K on size and speed; with a large one, it decodes Kodak photos 9 to 61X faster than learned codecs we ran on the same A100, at the same size or smaller.

An agent or robot in a scanned building must locate its camera in rooms that no model saw during training. Point cloud registration methods can align each RGB-D frame with the building's colored point cloud, accurately but at a high cost. We observe that geometric refinement brings nearby initial poses to the same final pose, so the learned component only needs to find the place. Kolibri does this with a graph network of 55,712 parameters that describes 10 cm cubes of points, called cells, by their color, local shape and neighbors. With the same refinement, it matches GeDi on 96 unseen ScanNet rooms (81.4% against 80.3% within 5 cm and 5°) and on a held-out test of 10 further rooms, in 82 ms per frame on an RTX 2080 Ti. The saved time allows for search without a room label. In one point cloud of 100 rooms, Kolibri loses 1.3 points against knowing the room, while retrieving the room first loses 56.0. In a hospital emergency department scene from a real scan, it localizes 86.5% of the frames rendered from the scan in 384 ms each, where GeDi needs 91 minutes per frame on cubes of the scan for a similar recall. The method needs colors that agree between the frame and the point cloud. On gray frames and across cameras with different color responses, point cloud registration methods keep the lead.

### ReTouchGS: Ultralight Render-free Aesthetic Color Enhancement and Transfer for 3D Gaussian Splatting
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23054v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.23054v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23054v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.23054v1)

2026/11/17 16:47 GTM

3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables photorealistic scene reconstruction. Existing 3DGS color transfer and interactive editing methods are guided by reference images and user-specified color adjustments. However, in many applications, users simply want aesthetically guided color enhancement that automatically makes the scene more visually appealing while maintaining consistent colors and tones across viewpoints. We present ReTouchGS, to our knowledge the first framework for reference-free aesthetic color enhancement of pretrained 3DGS models. It predicts a scene-level color lookup table (LUT) from the model and its associated camera parameters. ReTouchGS also supports reference-guided color transfer by fitting a scene-level LUT using one edited view of the same scene and its corresponding original render. A shared blend-aware color refinement network with only 44.7K parameters refines an analytic color update to realize either color mapping without rendering. It preserves geometry and delivers standard 3DGS assets in seconds. We also design the ReTouch3D benchmark to support the evaluation of aesthetic color enhancement and reference-guided color transfer across viewpoints. Experiments demonstrate high color accuracy and strong generalization to unseen scenes in both aesthetic color enhancement and reference-guided color transfer.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21781) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21781) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21781) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21781)

Deep-learning methods have achieved strong performance in EEG artifact removal, but evaluation remains dominated by reconstruction metrics such as error, correlation, and signal-to-noise ratio. Although these metrics assess similarity to clean EEG, they do not establish whether models effectively suppress artifacts, preserve neural activity, or provide reliable explanations. To address these limitations, we propose TriX-EEG, a three-level framework for trustworthy and explainable EEG artifact removal. Level 1, Signal Validity (SV), evaluates reconstruction quality and neural band-power preservation. Level 2, Removal Faithfulness (RF), assesses the removed component through artifact error and residual artifact/neural leakage. Level 3, Explanation Reliability (ER), examines model behavior through temporal alignment, causal relevance, and stability. We evaluate TriX-EEG using nine published neural denoisers across three public benchmarks derived from EEGDenoiseNet and the PhysioNet Motion Artifact Contaminated EEG Dataset, covering EOG, EMG, and motion artifacts. Results show that reconstruction metrics alone are insufficient for trustworthy denoiser assessment: RF complements it by revealing whether errors stem from residual artifact or suppressed neural activity, while ER captures distinct aspects of explanation behavior, supporting joint evaluation of three aspects within a unified framework rather than reliance on any single factor. External validation on an independent EEG/EOG dataset and the BCI Competition IV-2a motor-imagery dataset further demonstrates broadly consistent rankings under dataset shift. Higher-ranked denoisers generally preserve more task-relevant information or cause less downstream performance degradation. Overall, TriX-EEG provides a comprehensive basis for EEG denoiser assessment, encouraging models that are not only accurate but also faithful and explainable.

### As Student Rollouts Continue, Visual Backdoors Keep Growing
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22446) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22446) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22446) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22446)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Conventional knowledge distillation can facilitate the propagation of backdoors from teacher models to student models. However, how this risk manifests in On-Policy Distillation (OPD) remains insufficiently studied. OPD has recently emerged as an important post-training paradigm for Vision-Language Models (VLMs). Unlike traditional offline distillation, OPD does not require teacher-generated responses. Instead, it uses the teacher's probability distributions to supervise response trajectories generated online by the student. Since the initial student model typically generates benign responses and receives no explicit supervision from malicious target responses, OPD appears to offer some inherent resistance to backdoor propagation. In this paper, we challenge this intuition and reveal a more subtle risk of visual backdoor propagation in OPD. Through experiments across multiple model families and datasets, together with theoretical analysis, we identify three main findings. First, unlike the relatively gradual propagation observed in traditional offline distillation, backdoors rarely emerge in OPD at low poisoning ratios. However, the attack success rate rapidly approaches 100% once a critical threshold is exceeded. Second, backdoor propagation does not depend on explicit malicious responses. Instead, it is driven by the continuous accumulation of anomalous probability mass assigned by the teacher. Finally, based on these findings, we propose a novel trigger design that enables visual backdoor propagation with zero explicit trigger injection. These findings further reveal an overlooked mechanism of backdoor propagation in OPD and highlight its potential security risks.

Photographic color grading shapes the tone and color of a photograph to achieve a desired look. Text-driven photographic look transfer lets users specify these adjustments in natural language, with the goal of reproducing the requested look across photographs while preserving their content. However, prior research on text-driven color grading uses different datasets, text instructions, target definitions, and metrics. These differences make it difficult to compare how faithfully the methods reproduce the same look. To compare methods under a shared protocol, we introduce LUTBench. We collect 91 three-dimensional lookup tables (3D LUTs) and define one photographic look per LUT with a specified input conversion. Applying these looks to 5,000 MIT-Adobe FiveK photographs produces 455,000 input-target image pairs with reproducible target images. For each look, we use templates to turn measured tone and color changes on fixed color samples into candidate clauses. We use training image measurements to verify applicable clauses before composing the text description. The evaluation measures target fidelity, tests sensitivity to predefined input conversions, and separates generalization to unseen looks from reproduction of seen looks on new photographs. Across 16 methods, stronger text alignment does not guarantee target fidelity. However, most supervised variants improve fidelity on both seen and unseen looks.

On Synapse, for instance, it attains a DSC of 76.57% with only 8.025M parameters and 241.72 FPS, confirming a favorable accuracy-efficiency balance. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of combining wavelet-based multi-scale feature extraction with lightweight local-global feature fusion for efficient medical segmentation. ### A high-dimensional spatial-physical coupling operator for generalizable super-resolution of three-dimensional turbulent flows
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22951v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22951v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22951v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22951v1)

Accurate representation of turbulent flows requires simultaneously capturing multi-scale spatial structures and the complex, nonlinear coupling between distinct physical quantities (\textit{e.g.}, velocity components and density). In practice, however, turbulent data are often constrained by low spatial resolution due to hardware limitations and computational costs. While current data-driven super-resolution methods have shown promise by learning mappings via convolutional neural networks (CNNs), they typically treat physical variables as independent channels. This simplified approach neglects intrinsic cross-variable correlations, causing models to struggle with high-fidelity reconstruction, particularly under extremely sparse conditions or in out-of-distribution regimes. To bridge this gap, we propose an architecture-agnostic spatial-physical coupling operator that extends the standard convolution into a joint spatial-variable dimension. Unlike conventional linear channel mixing, our framework constructs a unified spatial-physical representation within CNNs to explicitly capture local inter-variable interactions. By lifting features into a higher-dimensional manifold, this operator significantly enhances the model's representation capacity. Experiments demonstrate that this approach enables strong reconstruction performance and zero-shot generalization, offering a physics-aware inductive bias for super-resolution of three-dimensional turbulence. More broadly, this work suggests that high-dimensional operators offer a promising avenue for modeling intricate multi-scale turbulent flows.

We show that an appropriate visual harness can substantially improve the performance of general vision-language models (VLMs) across diverse medical imaging tasks. We introduce \ours, a medical visual harness that equips general VLMs with interactive visual tools and systematic diagnostic evidence management. With \ours, general VLMs interactively explore medical images and progressively acquire diagnostic evidence, which is preserved in a lossless memory for subsequent retrieval and use. After submitting an initial answer, the VLM receives an assessment of potential omissions and the reliability of identified findings. This assessment guides further image inspection and measurement, followed by answer revision in an iterative agentic loop until sufficient evidence is obtained. Across 11 tasks and four imaging modalities, \ours improves performance on all tasks with Qwen3.8-27B, with notable gains in spleen measurement (63.3\% to 98.3\%), renal search (57.5\% to 71.2\%), and pulmonary nodule detection (0.0\% to 28.2\%). High-capability API-based VLMs achieve even higher performance with \ours. For example, GPT-6.1 Sol improves pulmonary nodule detection from 10.8\% to 69.2\% with \ours, demonstrating the complementary benefits of stronger model capabilities and an effective medical visual harness. Our results highlight the potential of visual harnesses as a new paradigm for scaling general VLMs to medical imaging tasks.

Recent multilingual large language models (LLMs) have improved machine translation, yet culturally adaptive translation for low-resource languages (LRLs) remains underexplored. This is particularly challenging for recipes, where ingredients, cooking tools, measurements, and procedures may require adaptation to the target cultural context. In this work, we introduce ReciLoReL, a benchmark of 600 expert-translated and culturally adapted recipes across Yorùbá, Hausa, and Igbo (three of the most prominent languages in Nigeria), and ReciLex, a multilingual culinary lexicon containing approximately 15,000 translated entities across ten recipe entity categories, including ingredients, tools, chef actions, state of tools, and durations. We evaluate state-of-the-art multilingual LLMs for English-to-LRL recipe translation under zero-shot and few-shot settings and investigate whether augmenting LLMs with ReciLex improves culturally adaptive recipe translation without requiring large target-language recipe corpora for training or fine-tuning. Beyond conventional reference-based translation evaluation using BLEU, chrF, and TER, we introduce five criteria for evaluating cultural adaptation, adapted from established cross-cultural adaptation methods. Across the selected LLMs and languages, ReciLex improves average BLEU from 13.57 to 14.51 and chrF from 37.89 to 38.74, while reducing TER from 77.14 to 75.12. Arbitrary-scale super-resolution (ASSR) of remote sensing images remains challenging due to the limited high-frequency information in a single low-resolution (LR) observation. While multi-frame SR can exploit complementary sub-pixel information, existing approaches often require real multi-frame training sequences and repeated feature encoding, increasing data acquisition demands and computational costs. To address these limitations, we propose SPS-GS (Sub-Pixel-Shifted Gaussian Splatting), an encode-once framework that learns from multi-phase LR observations synthesized from individual high-resolution images, without requiring real multi-frame training sequences. Specifically, Phase-Coherent Aliasing Demodulation (PCAD) exploits shift-dependent aliasing through Fourier-based alignment, phase-aware residual fusion, and least-squares aliasing estimation, enabling multi-frame information to be integrated before a single deep encoding pass. Gaussian Projection Alignment (GPA) then iteratively refines Gaussian centers and colors using multi-observation back-projection to promote observation consistency. The refined Gaussians support continuous rendering at arbitrary scales. Extensive experiments on five remote sensing datasets demonstrate strong reconstruction performance against single-image and multi-frame SR baselines, while maintaining competitive inference efficiency at common scales. The code will be publicly released.

Biomedical image segmentation must keep up with new anatomical structures, subjects, imaging modalities, and acquisition protocols, yet dense expert annotations are scarce and models trained on one cohort often struggle to transfer. In-context segmentation (ICS) offers a way out: a new structure is specified by a few annotated exemplars at inference time. However, reliable correspondence remains challenging in biomedical images, where adjacent structures often share similar appearances, and variations across subjects and imaging conditions further obscure their distinctions. We introduce CAST, a training-free framework built on a frozen DINOv3 backbone to calibrate fine-grained correspondences. Its stability test improves feature reliability at both global and patch-specific levels, while its coherence test assesses whether candidate correspondences are supported by consistent local structural context. Together, these tests enable more reliable correspondence across diverse anatomical structures and imaging conditions. Across eight biomedical benchmarks spanning 2D and 3D segmentation, cross-subject, and cross-modality transfer, CAST outperforms the strongest overall baseline by 12.34 percentage points in Dice and ranks first across all six volumetric settings. The versatility of CAST further opens up opportunities for challenging biomedical applications, including 2D-to-3D segmentation and atlas label transfer.

Power-amplifier spectral regrowth on unpopulated null subcarriers resists indoor multipath at high SNR but decays quadratically below the thermal noise floor, whereas populated active in-band preambles survive low SNR yet degrade under severe multipath, causing static fusion to lose up to 44.7 percentage points (pp). To our knowledge, we propose the first RFFI framework that dynamically modulates active in-band waveforms and unpopulated null-subcarrier regrowth via a blind physical SNR gate: Pluggable Gated Subband-Decoupled Fusion with Unlabeled Capture-Aware Prototype Calibration (GSDF-CAPC). Avoiding the 20-35 dB subband gradient imbalance of joint training, GSDF pairs any frozen pretrained host backbone with a 46.3K null-subcarrier plug-in encoder (NS-Branch), fusing scale-standardized logits and gate-warped spherical embeddings under a source-calibrated lag-64 thermal-SNR gate with exact low-SNR fallback. Over unlabeled target AP captures, forward-only unbalanced optimal-transport calibration (UOT-CAPC) aligns shifted prototypes under packet-arrival skew and recovers parameter-free balanced Sinkhorn (U-CAPC) at equipartition. Across DataPort-WiFi, WiSig, and ORBIT MobRFFI, zero-retrain GSDF wins all 72/72 paired runs across eight backbones with 0.00 pp low-SNR loss, while edge IB-Host + GSDF (92.6K-93.3K active parameters, 1.82 ms single-core CPU latency) and timing-synchronized GSDF-STO / GSDF-Tri reach 57.50%-65.05% three-benchmark Macro-F1 (89.04%-100.00% over 5-10-packet IoT bursts).

### How Much of SAR Vehicle Recognition Is the Vehicle? Background Shortcuts, Angle Gaps and Partial Libraries on MSTAR and ATRNet-STAR
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21827) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.21827) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21827) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.21827)

2026/11/18 13:32 GTM

Deep networks score near 99% on the MSTAR vehicle benchmark, and the newer ATRNet-STAR benchmark was built to be harder. We ask how much of these scores comes from the vehicle itself. With a modern network (ConvNeXt-Small, 3 seeds) we first reproduce the known MSTAR result: with the central 96x96 pixels zeroed out, accuracy is still 73.3% (chance 10%). The same shortcut is present in ATRNet-STAR, which was collected across several scenes: with the vehicle removed, accuracy is 63.3% on the 40-class SOC split (chance 2.5%). Under the official scene-change split, the background stops helping and starts hurting. Accuracy is 22.8% on full chips and 30.8% when only the vehicle is shown. A SAR foundation model, SARATR-X, leans on the background more than ConvNeXt does (+4 to +6 points with the vehicle removed, in every seed) and does not solve the scene change. We then hold the amount of training data fixed and vary the depression angles used for training. Accuracy is set mainly by the gap to the nearest training angle: 57% at 15 degrees, 39% at 30 degrees and 26% at 45 degrees. Adding more training angles does not help. Finally, when most classes have training images at the test angle and a few do not, the missing classes do worse than when no class has them: 9.0% instead of 22.4% at a 45-degree gap. Most of their images are assigned to known classes. We recommend reporting background-only and target-only controls, and the angle gap, next to any accuracy figure. Code is public.

Distribution planners need installation-level inventories of rooftop photovoltaic (PV) capacity, but interconnection records may be incomplete or spatially aggregated. Conventional image-based estimators segment PV arrays, convert the mask to physical area, and multiply by an estimated power density, coupling capacity prediction to mask-derived area. We propose Direct Head, a segmentation-guided architecture that retains U-Net segmentation and soft-mask pooling while directly regressing nonnegative capacity from PV-region features and area descriptors. We evaluate three independent runs on 7,590 French rooftop installations under the original split and a department-based holdout. Relative to a parameter-matched area-density neural model, Direct Head reduces mean absolute error by 8.7% and 12.5%, and symmetric mean absolute percentage error by 14.0% and 20.7%, respectively. Comparisons with conventional estimators, controlled architecture variants, auxiliary-loss ablations, and representative cases show that segmentation provides useful spatial evidence without requiring capacity to be determined by an area-density product.

Routine X-ray modalities like cone-beam computed tomography (CBCT) are optimized for high-contrast mineralized tissues, leaving soft-tissue abnormalities buried beneath photon noise and below human visual perception. Thus, detecting non-calcified pathologies requires secondary magnetic resonance imaging (MRI). We demonstrate that sub-visual radiographic signatures of soft-tissue pathology are physically preserved within routine CBCT scans and can be extracted for opportunistic screening using a targeted, physics-guided deep learning pipeline, even under extreme paired-data scarcity. Using the temporomandibular joint (TMJ) as a model system (N = 212 paired CBCT-MRI joints), we address the severe overfitting of high-capacity 3D networks through a two-stage framework. First, we employ a frozen full-grid 3D masked autoencoder (BertsWin) pre-trained on 6,550 unlabeled CBCT volumes via the GradientConductor (GCond) optimizer and an anatomy-aware objective (PhysLoss) redirecting gradient flow into the [-300, 300] HU soft-tissue window. Second, automated localization of the condylar zenith drives a deterministic 3D cropping that eliminates 81% of background osseous variance. Evaluated via a patient-isolated 3x10-fold stratified cross-validation protocol, a statistically robust sub-visual signal for MRI-verified synovial effusion (ROC AUC = 0.793) emerges exclusively from representations pre-trained with the PhysLoss and GCond regime. Crucially, only this setup captures true 3D anatomical chirality (p < 0.001) and high intra-patient structural consistency (0.791) over inter-patient noise; conversely, identical architectures pre-trained with standard L2 loss or unmasked variance decomposition (MVC) collapse to random guessing. These findings establish a generalizable paradigm for repurposing routine, low-contrast radiographic scans into opportunistic screening tools for occult soft-tissue disease.

Global canopy height maps are increasingly used to estimate forest carbon, yet their regional accuracy is rarely tested. ### Propose & Verify: Fast Reflection Symmetry Detection via Harmonic Fields
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22856v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22856v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22856v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22856v1)

Detecting multiple reflection symmetries in 3D shapes requires aggregating geometric evidence over a large space of candidate planes, making traditional voting approaches computationally expensive. We introduce the Fourier Accelerated Symmetry Transform (FAST), a training-free framework that combines geometric pair voting with efficient spectral computation. Exploiting the fact that global reflection planes pass through the shape centroid, we reduce the search to plane orientations, while geometric consistency filtering and independent witnesses suppress unreliable pair hypotheses. Our key insight is to represent these votes as a signal over plane orientations and efficiently compute a continuous, smoothed symmetry field in the spherical-harmonic domain. Specifically, we exploit the Funk-Hecke theorem, the spherical counterpart of the Fourier convolution theorem for rotationally symmetric kernels, to transform Gaussian smoothing into multiplication of harmonic coefficients. This enables efficient field evaluation, from which symmetry candidates are extracted, refined, and geometrically verified. Across two benchmarks, including a new benchmark of highly symmetric shapes, FAST achieves the highest clean multi-plane detection $F_1$ scores among evaluated methods (0.861 and 0.947), with GPU inference below 4 ms per shape: over $100\times$ faster than the next-fastest evaluated multi-plane GPU baseline. FAST also demonstrates strong robustness to geometric noise and competitive single-plane detection, achieving 94.9% success on clean single-plane shapes.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22820v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22820v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22820v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22820v1)

Text, images, audio, touch, and even robot actions can, in principle, all be rendered onto a single canvas, suggesting an ambitious goal of unifying all modalities within vision. Language provides the natural first test. To subsume it, a visual model must reason directly over text in pixels. Across a wide range of image, video, and unified multimodal generators, however, we find that their visual generation modules cannot perform symbolic reasoning over text on the canvas. They succeed only when an underlying text module or prompt rewriter already supplies the answer, effectively reducing the generator to a mere renderer, a limitation that post-training does little to resolve. To test whether this failure stems from pixels themselves or from current training paradigms, we train a family of purely pixel-space generative models from scratch without any text pathway. With images as their sole input and output, these models read questions, write intermediate steps, and produce answers directly on the canvas. Remarkably, they exhibit hallmark behaviors of large language models. Performance improves predictably with scale following a power law. Writing intermediate steps frame by frame substantially outperforms single-frame answering, echoing chain-of-thought, while reinforcement-learning post-training further strengthens reasoning. Moreover, sharing one canvas allows the same model to reason across modalities, a capability that text-only language models lack. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first systematic study of these phenomena in purely pixel-space generative models free of text tokens.

Underwater object detection is often limited by scarce and imbalanced training data and by strong appearance variations caused by turbidity, lighting, and differences between sensors. Existing data augmentation strategies, such as geometric or color augmentation or fully synthetic image generation with Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs), either lack semantic realism, fail to respect scene physics, or require additional annotations. In low-data domains like underwater environmental monitoring, the need for additional annotations is often the core bottleneck. Collecting new footage is hard, but labeling this dataset is even more time-consuming and costly, which limits how quickly a usable dataset can be constructed. We propose a unified fully automated zero-annotation pipeline that inserts class-conditioned trash objects into real underwater frames while preserving the original background context. Starting from sampled bounding boxes, each box is converted into an insertion mask and the object is pasted or generated within it, while the same box coordinates are automatically written as the corresponding annotation. Because labels are derived directly from the sampled boxes, the pipeline generates fully labeled training data for low-data settings without any manual annotation. Within this single pipeline we compare three strategies: copy-paste, Stable Diffusion inpainting, and DALL-E image-conditioned generation. We evaluate bounding-box faithfulness, visual realism, and the impact of the generated samples on downstream detector performance on the SeaClear underwater debris dataset. Results show that DALL-E improves detection performance in four of five domains, with relative gains of up to 96 percent over the real-only baseline.

Maritime search and rescue requires locating people in the water quickly, motivating the use of UAVs to survey the search area. During flight, camera motion and the longer exposures used in low light can blur small targets and hinder person detection. Event cameras record brightness changes as they occur, reducing the motion blur associated with longer exposure times. We investigate their use for maritime person detection by generating synthetic events using the v2e event simulator from public MOBDrone rescue videos and retaining the existing annotations. We compare the recurrent vision transformer model RVT-T with YOLOv8n and RT-DETR-R18 trained on exposure-matched images. Increasing exposure times from 50 to 200 ms progressively reduces frame-based detection accuracy, widening the gap to the event model. To evaluate the feasibility of on-board processing, we deploy our detector on a Jetson Orin Nano platform. The RVT-T model retains its accuracy and requires approximately 136 ms and 2.02 J per sample, allowing real-time processing on the UAV.

### A tiny object detection dataset for high-altitude open-water search and rescue
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22825v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22825v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22825v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22825v1)

2026/11/17 14:08 GTM

Large aerial platforms often operate above 1.5 km to improve the efficiency of open water search and rescue (OW-SAR). However, long imaging distances, wide fields of view, and limited sensor resolution reduce objects to a few pixels, increasing their susceptibility to environmental interference and making detection harder than for conventional small objects. To our knowledge, existing datasets focus on low-altitude scenarios and do not support automatic detection at 1.5-2.0 km. We therefore present TinyWOD, a tiny object detection dataset for high-altitude OW-SAR using lifebuoys as detection targets. It comprises 11,359 manually annotated images and 19,505 annotated instances spanning diverse environments, lighting conditions, water colors, and surface ripples. Compared with existing datasets, TinyWOD contains much smaller objects and a substantially higher proportion of tiny instances; its average equivalent side length (ESL) is 12.7 pixels, with 76.96% of in stances having ESLs below 15 pixels, reflecting actual high-altitude water-rescue imaging conditions. We define APtiny, a tiny object detection metric, and benchmark mainstream and state-of-the-art networks using this and conventional metrics. All evaluated networks effectively detect tiny waterborne objects, demonstrating TinyWOD's reusability and utility. We expect this dataset to support research on tiny object detection in high-altitude OW-SAR.

We expect this dataset to support research on tiny object detection in high-altitude OW-SAR.

### Fiberpose: state-of-the-art fiber morphometry and instance segmentation with a distance-transform neural network
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22953v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22953v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22953v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22953v1)

2026/11/17 15:41 GTM

Analysis of fibrous structures in SEM images is a routine task in biomedical engineering and materials science. However, most available methods for fiber quantification still rely on classical image binarization followed by simple post-processing. These approaches often produce unreliable results in images with weak boundaries, heterogeneous contrast, and densely packed fibers. Here, we introduce Fiberpose, a framework that reformulates fiber analysis as direct regression of a smooth distance-transform (DT) field, together with a novel set of geometry-based algorithms specifically adapted to this representation. The predicted field directly encodes fiber geometry, while the accompanying algorithms enable instance reconstruction and measurement of diameter and orientation within a unified workflow. The framework is implemented as a standalone Python desktop application and a web tool https://fiberpose.org. On an independent dataset of fibrous SEM images, Fiberpose achieved state-of-the-art instance-segmentation accuracy in comparison with SAM2 and Cellpose-SAM, and more accurate diameter measurements than DiameterJ, Fibar and GIFT. The method remains effective in dense and weakly contrasted regions and shows qualitative generalization across microscopy modalities not represented in the training data. Fiberpose therefore provides an interpretable geometric representation and an automated pipeline for reproducible quantitative characterization of fibrous materials.

### SWIR-cGAN: Synthesizing SWIR Bands Using VNIR Imagery from CBERS and AMAZONIA-1
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22922v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.22922v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22922v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2611.22922v1)

2026/11/17 15:22 GTM

Spectral band synthesis is an important research topic in remote sensing. The Brazilian satellites CBERS-4, CBERS-4A, and AMAZONIA-1 provide high temporal resolution (1-3 days) visible and near-infrared (VNIR) imagery through the Wide-Field Imager (WFI), but lack short-wave infrared (SWIR) bands. Recent deep learning methods have shown promising results for spectral synthesis, but challenges remain for cross-satellite generalization and spectrally distant bands. This paper presents a conditional Generative Adversarial Network (cGAN) with an attention-enhanced U-net generator and a multi-loss objective to synthesize Sentinel-2/MSI SWIR bands 11 (SWIR-1) and 12 (SWIR-2) from WFI VNIR inputs. Experiments used 4,125 patches for training and cross-validation and 1,026 unseen patches for testing, with hyperparameters optimized using a subset of the training data. On SWIR-1, the model achieved SSIM of 0.900, PSNR of 31.44 dB, and MAE of 0.019 in TOA reflectance. On SWIR-2, it achieved SSIM of 0.917, PSNR of 33.35 dB, and MAE of 0.015. Benchmarking was performed on the test dataset, with each model optimized separately prior to evaluation. SWIR-cGAN outperformed the baselines, particularly for SWIR-2. The ablation study identified the multi-loss as the main performance contributor, while the attention and residual modules provided additional improvements. Our results demonstrate the feasibility of synthesizing SWIR bands from WFI imagery. The SWIR-cGAN is available at https://github.com/marujore/swir-cgan.
